    Apple Senior UI Engineer — Interview Prep Guide

    2026-10-28

    Start with: 01 · JavaScript & TypeScript

    Open topic

    How to use this guide#

    Each tab is one interview topic, written as model answers you can say out loud: concept → how it works under the hood → how you'd build it at Netflix / Spotify / bank / Apple Store scale → a story from your own work → the follow-up questions interviewers ask next.

    Tab map

    #TabWeight for this role
    1JavaScript & TypeScriptVery high — vanilla JS is tested directly
    2HTML & CSSVery high — apple.com is a CSS-heavy, pixel-perfect site
    3React, Next.js & StateVery high
    4Design Systems & Visual CraftHigh
    5PerformanceVery high
    6AccessibilityVery high — Apple treats it as a product value
    7APIs, Data & Real-timeHigh
    8Web SecurityMedium-high (banking-grade thinking, payments in the Store)
    9Generative AI & LLM UIsHigh — explicitly in minimum qualifications
    10Testing, Tooling & CI/CDMedium-high
    11Frontend System DesignVery high — the senior-level separator
    12Machine Coding & DSAVery high
    13Preferred Quals (AEM, micro-frontends, Node, D3, migration)Medium
    14Behavioral & Apple FitVery high
    15Company Playbook (Netflix, Amazon, Uber, Atlassian, Walmart, Target, JPMorgan, Goldman, Tesla)High — how each company interviews, citable facts, company-style questions
    16Output Questions (predict the console output, React render order, input → output transformations)Very high: drill daily. 68 verified predict-output questions (60 JS, 8 React) + 24 tested transformation problems

    Every tab now ends with a "Level up" section: how big companies apply that topic (with sources you can cite), staff-level insights most engineers don't know, and harder questions in the style of Netflix, Amazon, Uber, Atlassian and the banks. Read the core sections first; the Level up sections are what make your answers stand out.

    Every story in this guide is a template. The "Your story" blocks are written around the kind of work you do on PLUS (an arbitration case platform: filings, documents, hearings, payments, strict security and ADA expectations). Before the interview, swap in what you actually built and your real numbers. Apple interviewers dig three or four levels deep; a story you didn't live falls apart on the second follow-up.

    The "How did you implement it?" answer framework#

    Apple interviewers rarely accept the definition. They ask how you did it, why that way, how you knew it worked. Answer every implementation question in this order — call it C-C-D-I-M-L:

    1. Context — product, scale, users. "Our case-filing portal serves ~X attorneys; the dashboard renders up to 5,000 cases."

    2. Constraint — what made it hard. "It had to work on 4-year-old Windows laptops and with JAWS."

    3. Decision — 2–3 options you considered, the trade-off, what you picked. This is the senior signal.

    4. Implementation — concrete detail: the hook, the API, the config, the pattern. Name real APIs (IntersectionObserver, React.lazy, aria-live).

    5. Measurement — before/after numbers and how you measured (Lighthouse, Web Vitals RUM, Profiler, bundle analyzer).

    6. Learning — what you'd do differently or what you'd do at 100× scale.

    Practice saying each story in 90 seconds, then be ready to go 10 minutes deep on any sentence of it.

    Phrases that signal seniority#

    • "The trade-off was…" / "We chose X over Y because…"

    • "We measured it with…" / "p75 LCP went from … to …"

    • "I made it a reusable primitive so the other three teams…"

    • "The failure mode I worried about was…"

    • "At Apple's scale I'd change this by…"

    What the Apple loop tests#

    Apple has no single company-wide format — each team runs its own loop — so ask your recruiter or referrer which rounds you have. Candidate reports for web UI roles in IS&T usually describe 4–6 rounds of 45–60 minutes, each run by a different engineer or manager, often mixing coding and discussion in the same hour.

    Round (typical)What they're really testingTab to drill
    Recruiter / hiring managerMotivation, scope of your past work, communication14
    JavaScript codingClosures, async, DOM, correct core JS (UI parts in React)1, 12
    UI machine codingBuild a component in 45–60 min: clean structure, a11y, edge cases2, 6, 12
    React deep diveRendering model, hooks, state, performance reasoning3, 5
    Frontend system designArchitecture at scale, trade-offs, perf, a11y, security11 (+5, 6, 7, 8)
    Project deep dive"Walk me through the hardest thing you built" — 3–4 levels of why14 + your stories in every tab
    Manager / cross-functionalOwnership, conflict with design/PM, quality bar, Apple values14

    Five things Apple interviewers consistently weigh

    1. Craft and detail. Pixel precision, smooth animation, edge cases (empty, error, long text, RTL, slow network). Mention them before being asked.

    2. Fundamentals over frameworks. Even when you code in React, expect "what does the browser actually do here".

    3. Accessibility as default. If you build a modal and don't trap focus or restore it, that's noticed.

    4. Privacy and security instinct. Apple sells privacy. Mention minimizing data, not logging PII, CSP.

    5. Ownership and clear reasoning. Think out loud, state assumptions, give trade-offs, test your own code.

    Clarify before you code. In every coding or design round, spend the first 3–5 minutes on requirements: inputs, outputs, scale, devices, a11y, offline, browser support. Write them down. This alone separates senior from mid-level.

    14-day plan (Oct 29 – Nov 11)#

    About 4 hours a day on weekdays and 7–8 on weekends. Every day ends with one machine-coding problem (45 min, timed, talking out loud) and one DSA problem from tab 12.

    • Day 1 · Thu Oct 29 — Tab 1 part A: event loop, closures, this, prototypes. Write debounce, throttle, bind from memory.

    • Day 2 · Fri Oct 30 — Tab 1 part B: promises, polyfills, TypeScript. Write Promise.all, allSettled, retry-with-backoff, EventEmitter.

    • Day 3 · Sat Oct 31 — Tab 2: layout, cascade, stacking contexts, responsive images. Build a responsive product grid in plain HTML/CSS.

    • Day 4 · Sun Nov 1 — Tab 3: React rendering, hooks, state, Next.js rendering modes. Re-implement useDebounce, useFetch with abort.

    • Day 5 · Mon Nov 2 — Tab 5: Core Web Vitals, bundle and render optimization. Run Lighthouse + bundle analyzer on your own app; write down 3 real numbers.

    • Day 6 · Tue Nov 3 — Tab 6: WCAG, ARIA patterns, focus management. Build an accessible modal and tabs; test with VoiceOver.

    • Day 7 · Wed Nov 4 — Tab 4 + Tab 10: design systems, Storybook, testing, CI. Write RTL tests for your modal.

    • Day 8 · Thu Nov 5 — Tab 7 + Tab 8: REST/GraphQL, caching, real-time, security. Draw the auth flow from memory.

    • Day 9 · Fri Nov 6 — Tab 9: build the streaming Claude chat demo (Next.js route handler + SSE). This becomes a story.

    • Day 10 · Sat Nov 7 — Tab 11: system design 1–3 (Apple Store product page, Netflix home feed, autocomplete). Draw each on paper in 40 min.

    • Day 11 · Sun Nov 8 — Tab 11: system design 4–6 (banking dashboard, AI support chat, design-system component).

    • Day 12 · Mon Nov 9 — Tab 12: 3 timed machine-coding rounds back to back.

    • Day 13 · Tue Nov 10 — Tab 13 + Tab 14: preferred quals, rehearse all STAR stories out loud (record yourself).

    • Day 14 · Wed Nov 11 — Full mock loop with a friend or with me; re-read the "Interview cheat lines" at the end of each tab; sleep early.

    Day-before and day-of checklist#

    • Confirm round list, interviewer names, and coding tool (CoderPad, shared editor, whiteboard) with the recruiter

    • Test camera, mic, internet backup (phone hotspot), quiet room; close Slack and notifications

    • Have a blank editor and a paper notebook for system-design sketches

    • Re-read your 8 STAR stories (tab 14) and the cheat lines at the end of each tab

    • Look at apple.com/in and the Apple Store product configurator with DevTools open: note one thing that impressed you and one thing you'd improve

    • Prepare 2 questions per interviewer (tab 14)

    • In every round: clarify → plan out loud → code → test with examples → discuss edge cases, a11y, and perf

    • If stuck: say what you know, propose a simpler version, then improve it. Silence is the only real failure.

    Next →01 · JavaScript & TypeScript
    Topic 0119 min read4 diagrams

    1. JavaScript & TypeScript

    1.1 Event loop and async#

    JavaScript runs your code on one main thread. The event loop decides what runs next: finish the current task, drain all microtasks, maybe render a frame, then take the next one task. Getting this order right is the most-asked JS question at Apple-level interviews.

    A long task or a long microtask chain delays step 3, so the screen freezes and input waits.

    The exact order of one loop turn#

    1. Run one task (macrotask) to completion: a script, a setTimeout callback, a click handler, a MessageChannel message.

    2. Drain the microtask queue completely: Promise.then/catch/finally, await continuations, queueMicrotask, MutationObserver. Microtasks queued by microtasks also run now — so an infinite microtask chain freezes the page.

    3. If it's time for a frame (~every 16.7 ms at 60 Hz, 8.3 ms at 120 Hz on ProMotion): run requestAnimationFrame callbacks → style → layout → paint → composite.

    4. If idle time remains: requestIdleCallback callbacks.

    5. Go to 1.

    Classic output question#

    js
    console.log('1');
setTimeout(() => console.log('2'), 0);
Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log('3'));
queueMicrotask(() => console.log('4'));
(async () => {
  console.log('5');
  await null;
  console.log('6');
})();
requestAnimationFrame(() => console.log('7'));
console.log('8');
// 1 5 8 3 4 6 7 2   (7 and 2 may swap: rAF depends on frame timing)

    How to explain it: 1, 5, 8 are synchronous (an async function runs synchronously until its first await). 3, 4, 6 are microtasks in the order they were queued. rAF runs before the next paint; setTimeout(0) is a separate task with a minimum delay (clamped to ≥4 ms after 5 nested calls), so its order versus rAF isn't guaranteed.

    Why it matters in real products#

    SituationWhat goes wrongFix you'd describe
    Filtering 50,000 rows on keypressOne 300 ms task blocks input → bad INPDebounce input, chunk work with scheduler.yield() / setTimeout slices, or move to a Web Worker
    Animating with setTimeoutDrifts and janks against display refreshrequestAnimationFrame or CSS transforms
    Measuring layout after DOM writesForced synchronous layout each loopBatch reads then writes; read in rAF
    Analytics on page loadCompetes with hydrationrequestIdleCallback or after load
    Long Promise.then chains doing heavy workMicrotasks block rendering exactly like sync codeYield back to a task between chunks

    Yielding to the main thread (senior-level detail)#

    js
    // Break a long job into chunks so input and paint can run between them
async function processInChunks(items, work, chunkMs = 8) {
  let i = 0;
  while (i < items.length) {
    const start = performance.now();
    while (i < items.length && performance.now() - start < chunkMs) {
      work(items[i++]);
    }
    // yield: prefer scheduler.yield when available (keeps priority), else a macrotask
    if ('scheduler' in globalThis && 'yield' in scheduler) await scheduler.yield();
    else await new Promise(r => setTimeout(r, 0));
  }
}

    Your story (adapt): "On the PLUS case dashboard, sorting and filtering several thousand cases client-side made typing in the filter box lag. The Performance panel showed 200+ ms tasks on every keystroke. I debounced the input by 200 ms, memoized the derived list, and moved the heavy fuzzy-match into a Web Worker. Long tasks disappeared from the trace and typing stayed under one frame."

    1.2 Closures, scope, this, prototypes, modules#

    Scope and hoisting#

    • var is function-scoped and hoisted as undefined. let/const are block-scoped and hoisted into the temporal dead zone — touching them before the declaration throws ReferenceError.

    • Function declarations are hoisted whole; function expressions and arrow functions are not.

    • Lexical scope: a function sees variables where it was written, not where it is called.

    js
    for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++) setTimeout(() => console.log(i)); // 3 3 3  (one shared i)
for (let j = 0; j < 3; j++) setTimeout(() => console.log(j)); // 0 1 2  (new j per iteration)

    Closures — the definition interviewers want#

    A closure is a function bundled with references to the variables of the scope it was created in. The variables stay alive as long as the function is reachable. That is how JS gets private state, memoization, once, debounce, and every React hook.

    js
    function createRateLimiter(limit, windowMs) {
  const hits = [];                 // private, survives between calls
  return function allow() {
    const now = Date.now();
    while (hits.length && now - hits[0] > windowMs) hits.shift();
    if (hits.length >= limit) return false;
    hits.push(now);
    return true;
  };
}
const canSubmit = createRateLimiter(3, 10_000); // e.g. 3 payment attempts per 10 s

    Real-world closure bugs you should be able to name:

    • Stale closure in React: an effect or interval captures an old count. Fix with the functional updater setCount(c => c + 1), a ref, or correct dependencies.

    • Memory leak: an event listener closure holds a big DOM subtree or dataset after the component unmounts. Fix by removing the listener or using AbortController with addEventListener(..., { signal }).

    this — four binding rules, in priority order#

    RuleExamplethis is
    new bindingnew User()the new object
    Explicitfn.call(obj), apply, bindobj
    Implicitobj.method()obj
    Defaultfn()undefined in strict mode / modules, else globalThis

    Arrow functions have no own this: they take it from the enclosing scope, which is why they fix callback bugs in classes but are wrong as object methods that need this.

    js
    const cart = {
  items: [],
  add(item) { this.items.push(item); },
  addLater(item) { setTimeout(() => this.add(item), 0); }, // arrow keeps cart
};
const { add } = cart;
add('x'); // TypeError in strict mode: this is undefined (lost implicit binding)

    Prototypes and classes#

    Every object has an internal [[Prototype]] link. Property lookup walks the chain until null. class is syntax on top of this: methods live on Class.prototype, shared by all instances (memory-efficient), while fields declared in the class body live on each instance.

    js
    class Product { constructor(n) { this.name = n; } label() { return this.name; } }
const p = new Product('iPhone');
Object.getPrototypeOf(p) === Product.prototype; // true
p.hasOwnProperty('label');                     // false — it's on the prototype

    Private class fields (#token) are truly private (not reachable via reflection), unlike _token convention — a good point to mention for security-sensitive SDK code.

    Equality, copying, immutability#

    • === for everything; Object.is distinguishes NaN and -0 (React uses Object.is to compare state).

    • structuredClone(obj) deep-copies including Map, Set, Date, cyclic refs; it can't copy functions or DOM nodes.

    • Object.freeze is shallow. Immutability in React means new references for changed paths, so === checks are cheap.

    Modules#

    • ES modules are static (imports resolved before execution) → enables tree shaking. CommonJS require is dynamic → bundlers can't safely drop unused exports.

    • import() returns a promise → the basis of code splitting.

    • Module scope is strict mode by default and evaluated once (singletons are natural).

    Generators, iterators, and WeakMap (shows depth)#

    • WeakMap/WeakRef keys don't prevent garbage collection — use them to attach metadata to DOM nodes or objects without leaks.

    • Iterators/generators power lazy pipelines (for...of over pages of an API).

    js
    async function* fetchAllPages(url) {
  let next = url;
  while (next) {
    const res = await fetch(next);
    const { items, nextUrl } = await res.json();
    yield* items;
    next = nextUrl;
  }
}
for await (const order of fetchAllPages('/api/orders')) render(order);

    1.3 Utilities you must write from memory#

    Write each one on paper, then type it in under 5 minutes. In the interview, say the edge cases out loud before they ask.

    Debounce (with leading, cancel, flush)#

    Use for: search-as-you-type, window resize, autosave. Fires after the user stops for wait ms.

    js
    function debounce(fn, wait, { leading = false } = {}) {
  let timer = null, lastArgs, lastThis;
  function debounced(...args) {
    lastArgs = args; lastThis = this;
    const callNow = leading && timer === null;
    clearTimeout(timer);
    timer = setTimeout(() => {
      timer = null;
      if (!leading) fn.apply(lastThis, lastArgs);
    }, wait);
    if (callNow) fn.apply(lastThis, lastArgs);
  }
  debounced.cancel = () => { clearTimeout(timer); timer = null; };
  debounced.flush = () => { if (timer) { clearTimeout(timer); timer = null; fn.apply(lastThis, lastArgs); } };
  return debounced;
}

    Edge cases to mention: preserving this and the latest args; cancel on unmount; in React, create it once (useMemo/useRef) or every render makes a new debouncer.

    Throttle#

    Use for: scroll position tracking, drag, analytics. Fires at most once per wait ms, with a trailing call so the last position is not lost.

    js
    function throttle(fn, wait) {
  let last = 0, timer = null, pendingArgs = null;
  return function throttled(...args) {
    const now = Date.now();
    const remaining = wait - (now - last);
    if (remaining <= 0) {
      clearTimeout(timer); timer = null;
      last = now; fn.apply(this, args);
    } else {
      pendingArgs = args;
      if (!timer) timer = setTimeout(() => {
        last = Date.now(); timer = null; fn.apply(this, pendingArgs);
      }, remaining);
    }
  };
}

    Say this too: for scroll-linked visuals, requestAnimationFrame throttling or IntersectionObserver beats timers.

    Promise.all, allSettled, race, any#

    js
    function promiseAll(iterable) {
  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    const items = Array.from(iterable);
    const results = new Array(items.length);
    let done = 0;
    if (items.length === 0) return resolve([]);
    items.forEach((p, i) => {
      Promise.resolve(p).then(v => {
        results[i] = v;                // keep input order, not finish order
        if (++done === items.length) resolve(results);
      }, reject);                      // first rejection wins
    });
  });
}

function promiseAllSettled(items) {
  return promiseAll(items.map(p => Promise.resolve(p).then(
    value => ({ status: 'fulfilled', value }),
    reason => ({ status: 'rejected', reason }))));
}

    When to use which (real example): an Apple Store page loads price, delivery estimate, and trade-in offer. Price is required → Promise.all for critical data. Recommendations and reviews are optional → allSettled so one failing widget doesn't blank the page. race with a timeout promise caps a slow call. any takes the first healthy mirror/CDN.

    Concurrency-limited task pool#

    Asked often at senior level: "Upload 200 files, but at most 4 at a time."

    js
    async function runPool(tasks, limit = 4) {
  const results = new Array(tasks.length);
  let next = 0;
  async function worker() {
    while (next < tasks.length) {
      const i = next++;
      try { results[i] = { ok: true, value: await tasks[i]() }; }
      catch (e) { results[i] = { ok: false, error: e }; }
    }
  }
  await Promise.all(Array.from({ length: Math.min(limit, tasks.length) }, worker));
  return results;
}

    Retry with exponential backoff and jitter#

    js
    async function retry(fn, { retries = 3, base = 300, signal } = {}) {
  for (let attempt = 0; ; attempt++) {
    try { return await fn(); }
    catch (err) {
      const retriable = !err.status || err.status >= 500 || err.status === 429;
      if (attempt >= retries || !retriable || signal?.aborted) throw err;
      const delay = base * 2 ** attempt * (0.5 + Math.random()); // jitter avoids thundering herd
      await new Promise(r => setTimeout(r, delay));
    }
  }
}

    Say: never auto-retry non-idempotent POSTs (payments) without an idempotency key.

    Function.prototype.bind#

    js
    Function.prototype.myBind = function (ctx, ...preset) {
  const fn = this;
  function bound(...args) {
    // when called with new, ignore ctx (new binding beats explicit binding)
    return fn.apply(this instanceof bound ? this : ctx, [...preset, ...args]);
  }
  bound.prototype = Object.create(fn.prototype);
  return bound;
};

    Event emitter (pub/sub)#

    js
    class Emitter {
  #map = new Map();
  on(evt, fn) {
    if (!this.#map.has(evt)) this.#map.set(evt, new Set());
    this.#map.get(evt).add(fn);
    return () => this.off(evt, fn);          // return unsubscribe
  }
  once(evt, fn) { const off = this.on(evt, (...a) => { off(); fn(...a); }); return off; }
  off(evt, fn) { this.#map.get(evt)?.delete(fn); }
  emit(evt, ...args) { [...(this.#map.get(evt) ?? [])].forEach(fn => fn(...args)); } // copy: safe if a handler unsubscribes
}

    Deep clone (with cycles) and deep equal#

    js
    function deepClone(v, seen = new WeakMap()) {
  if (v === null || typeof v !== 'object') return v;
  if (seen.has(v)) return seen.get(v);
  if (v instanceof Date) return new Date(v);
  if (v instanceof Map) { const m = new Map(); seen.set(v, m); v.forEach((val, k) => m.set(k, deepClone(val, seen))); return m; }
  if (v instanceof Set) { const s = new Set(); seen.set(v, s); v.forEach(val => s.add(deepClone(val, seen))); return s; }
  const out = Array.isArray(v) ? [] : Object.create(Object.getPrototypeOf(v));
  seen.set(v, out);
  for (const k of Reflect.ownKeys(v)) out[k] = deepClone(v[k], seen);
  return out;
}

    Memoize, curry, flatten, once, get#

    js
    const memoize = (fn, key = (...a) => JSON.stringify(a)) => {
  const cache = new Map();
  return (...a) => { const k = key(...a); if (!cache.has(k)) cache.set(k, fn(...a)); return cache.get(k); };
};
const curry = fn => function c(...a) { return a.length >= fn.length ? fn.apply(this, a) : (...b) => c.apply(this, [...a, ...b]); };
const flatten = (arr, depth = Infinity) => depth < 1 ? arr.slice() :
  arr.reduce((acc, x) => acc.concat(Array.isArray(x) ? flatten(x, depth - 1) : x), []);
const once = fn => { let done = false, r; return (...a) => done ? r : (done = true, r = fn(...a)); };
const get = (obj, path, dflt) => path.replace(/\[(\d+)\]/g, '.$1').split('.').reduce((o, k) => o?.[k], obj) ?? dflt;

    DOM-level utilities (Apple likes these)#

    js
    // Event delegation: one listener for 1,000 list items
list.addEventListener('click', e => {
  const btn = e.target.closest('button[data-action]');
  if (!btn || !list.contains(btn)) return;
  handle(btn.dataset.action, btn.closest('li').dataset.id);
});

// getElementsByClassName polyfill via tree walk (tests DOM traversal)
function byClass(root, cls) {
  const out = [];
  (function walk(node) {
    if (node.classList?.contains(cls)) out.push(node);
    for (const child of node.children) walk(child);
  })(root);
  return out;
}

    1.4 TypeScript at senior level#

    The senior message: types are a design tool — they make illegal states unrepresentable and turn runtime bugs into compile errors across hundreds of engineers.

    Make illegal states unrepresentable (discriminated unions)#

    ts
    // Bad: isLoading + error + data can all be set at once
type Bad = { isLoading: boolean; error?: Error; data?: Order[] };

// Good: exactly one state at a time, and the compiler forces you to handle each
type RemoteData<T> =
  | { status: 'idle' }
  | { status: 'loading' }
  | { status: 'error'; error: Error }
  | { status: 'success'; data: T };

function render(s: RemoteData<Order[]>) {
  switch (s.status) {
    case 'success': return list(s.data);      // s.data is typed here only
    case 'error': return errorView(s.error);
    case 'loading': return spinner();
    case 'idle': return null;
    default: { const _never: never = s; return _never; } // exhaustive check
  }
}

    Real example: a checkout step machine (cart → shipping → payment → review → placed) typed as a union means you can't render the payment form with a missing shipping address.

    Generics with constraints#

    ts
    function groupBy<T, K extends PropertyKey>(items: T[], key: (t: T) => K): Record<K, T[]> {
  return items.reduce((acc, item) => {
    const k = key(item);
    (acc[k] ||= []).push(item);
    return acc;
  }, {} as Record<K, T[]>);
}

// keyof + indexed access: a type-safe sortable table column
type Column<T, K extends keyof T = keyof T> = { key: K; header: string; render?: (v: T[K], row: T) => React.ReactNode };

    Utility and advanced types you should explain#

    TypeUse it for
    Partial<T>, Required<T>, Readonly<T>PATCH payloads, config defaults, immutable state
    Pick<T, K>, Omit<T, K>Component props derived from a model
    Record<K, V>Lookup maps (Record<Locale, string>)
    ReturnType<F>, Parameters<F>, Awaited<T>Typing from existing functions without duplication
    NonNullable<T>, Extract, ExcludeNarrowing unions
    Template literal types`` type Token = `color-${'primary'
    Mapped + conditional types{ [K in keyof T as on${Capitalize<string & K>}]: (v: T[K]) => void }
    satisfiesCheck an object matches a type while keeping its literal types
    Branded typestype OrderId = string & { __brand: 'OrderId' } so a UserId can't be passed by mistake

    Narrowing and runtime validation#

    Types vanish at runtime, so API data must be validated at the boundary. Say you use a schema library (Zod/Valibot) at the fetch layer and infer the TS type from the schema — one source of truth.

    ts
    import { z } from 'zod';
const Product = z.object({ id: z.string(), name: z.string(), priceCents: z.number().int().nonnegative() });
type Product = z.infer<typeof Product>;

async function getProduct(id: string): Promise<Product> {
  const res = await fetch(`/api/products/${id}`);
  if (!res.ok) throw new HttpError(res.status);
  return Product.parse(await res.json()); // throws early with a clear error if backend drifts
}

// user-defined type guard
function isApiError(e: unknown): e is { status: number; message: string } {
  return typeof e === 'object' && e !== null && 'status' in e;
}

    Typing React well#

    tsx
    // Props that extend the native element so consumers keep aria-*, onClick, etc.
type ButtonProps = React.ComponentPropsWithoutRef<'button'> & { variant?: 'primary' | 'secondary'; loading?: boolean };

// Polymorphic "as" prop (design-system staple)
type PolyProps<E extends React.ElementType, P = {}> = P & { as?: E } & Omit<React.ComponentPropsWithoutRef<E>, keyof P | 'as'>;
function Text<E extends React.ElementType = 'span'>({ as, ...rest }: PolyProps<E, { size?: 'sm' | 'md' }>) {
  const C = as ?? 'span';
  return <C {...rest} />;
}

// Generic component
function List<T>({ items, renderItem, getKey }: { items: T[]; renderItem: (t: T) => React.ReactNode; getKey: (t: T) => string }) {
  return <ul>{items.map(i => <li key={getKey(i)}>{renderItem(i)}</li>)}</ul>;
}

// Typed hook with a tuple return
function useToggle(initial = false): readonly [boolean, () => void] {
  const [on, set] = React.useState(initial);
  return [on, React.useCallback(() => set(v => !v), [])] as const;
}

    tsconfig decisions you can defend#

    • strict: true, noUncheckedIndexedAccess: true (array/record lookups become T | undefined — catches real bugs), exactOptionalPropertyTypes in libraries.

    • moduleResolution: bundler for Vite/Next; project references for a monorepo so packages build incrementally.

    • any is banned by lint; unknown at boundaries, then narrow.

    Your story (adapt): "When I joined PLUS, case status was a free string and three screens disagreed on spelling. I introduced a CaseStatus union generated from the API's OpenAPI spec, plus an exhaustive switch helper. The next time the backend added a status, the build failed in the four places that needed updating instead of the bug reaching users."

    1.5 Interview questions and model answers#

    Q: What's the difference between microtasks and macrotasks? Give a bug it caused. A: After each task, the engine drains every microtask before rendering. A recursive promise chain that processes a big array keeps the queue non-empty, so the page never paints — it looks frozen even though it's "async". I break the work with a macrotask yield (setTimeout or scheduler.yield()) between chunks.

    Q: Is async/await blocking? A: It doesn't block the thread at the await point — the function suspends and its continuation is a microtask. But everything between awaits is synchronous, so a heavy loop inside an async function blocks just like normal code.

    Q: How does garbage collection decide what to free, and how do frontend apps leak memory? A: Mark-and-sweep from roots (globals, the stack, live closures). Leaks are reachable-but-unused objects: forgotten listeners/intervals, detached DOM nodes held in variables, growing caches without eviction, closures capturing large objects. I find them with DevTools Memory heap snapshots: take one, do the action 5 times, take another, compare, and look for "Detached" nodes.

    Q: == vs ===, null vs undefined? A: === compares without coercion; I use it everywhere except x == null, which intentionally matches both. undefined means "not assigned"; null means "intentionally empty". ?? only falls back on null/undefined, unlike ||, which also replaces 0 and '' — a real pricing bug if a $0 item shows the default price.

    Q: Explain Object.defineProperty, getters, and Proxy. When would you use Proxy? A: Proxy intercepts operations on an object (get/set/has). Vue 3 and MobX use it for reactivity; I'd use it for dev-only warnings on accessing removed config keys, or for a typed API client that builds paths from property access. Cost: slower than plain objects, so not in hot loops.

    Q: What happens when you type a URL and press Enter? (very common) A: DNS → TCP + TLS (or QUIC for HTTP/3) → HTTP request → server/CDN responds with HTML → browser parses HTML and builds the DOM, discovers CSS/JS via the preload scanner → CSSOM built (render-blocking) → scripts run (parser-blocking unless defer/async/module) → render tree → layout → paint → composite. Then I connect each step to an optimization: CDN and preconnect for the network, critical CSS, defer, image priority for LCP.

    Q: Explain fetch vs XHR, and how to cancel a request. A: fetch is promise-based and streams the body; it only rejects on network failure, so I check res.ok. Cancel with AbortController — I pass signal and call abort() on unmount or when a newer request replaces it.

    Q: Write sleep, then a function that runs promises in sequence.

    js
    const sleep = ms => new Promise(r => setTimeout(r, ms));
const sequence = fns => fns.reduce((p, fn) => p.then(acc => fn().then(v => [...acc, v])), Promise.resolve([]));

    Cheat lines#

    • "Async is about when code runs, not where — it's still the main thread."

    • "Microtasks drain fully before paint; that's why a promise loop can freeze the UI."

    • "Closures are how JS does private state; stale closures are the #1 hooks bug."

    • "Validate at the boundary, trust types inside."

    • "I make illegal states unrepresentable with discriminated unions and an exhaustive never check."

    1.6 Level up: JavaScript at big-company scale#

    How top companies apply it#

    • Uber (m.uber): fought dependency bloat by picking single-purpose micro-libraries and moving heavy transformations (date handling, data reshaping) to the server so libraries like Moment never reached the client. They also wrapped every event listener in try/catch, because errors thrown in async callbacks from a CDN-hosted script surface in window.onerror only as an opaque "Script error." without CORS headers (source).

    • Netflix: treated JavaScript cost as the main performance budget. Removing client-side React and libraries like Lodash from one page saved over 200 kB and halved Time-to-Interactive (source).

    • JPMorgan (Perspective): moved heavy computation out of JavaScript altogether — a C++ engine compiled to WebAssembly, running in a Web Worker so the main thread stays free (source).

    Staff-level insights (what 15-year engineers know)#

    • "Script error." and cross-origin scripts. Errors from scripts on another origin are masked unless the <script> has crossorigin="anonymous" and the CDN sends Access-Control-Allow-Origin. Without that, your error monitoring is blind to your own CDN bundles.

    • Engine shapes (hidden classes). V8 optimises objects that always have the same properties added in the same order. Adding properties conditionally or using delete on hot objects makes call sites polymorphic and slower. In hot paths (grids, parsers), create objects with all fields up front and set unused ones to null.

    • Prototype pollution. A naive deep merge(target, JSON.parse(userInput)) can write to __proto__ and change every object in the app — a real class of vulnerability. Block __proto__, constructor and prototype keys, or merge into Object.create(null) or a Map.

    • Strings aren't characters. '👍🏽'.length === 4 (UTF-16 code units). Character counters, truncation and cursor movement must use Intl.Segmenter for grapheme clusters, or emoji, Hindi conjuncts and flags break.

    • Money and numbers. Never use floats for currency (0.1 + 0.2 !== 0.3). Use integer minor units (paise, cents) and Intl.NumberFormat for display; use BigInt beyond 2^53 (IDs from some backends overflow Number silently — parse them as strings).

    • Dates and time zones. new Date('2026-11-01') is UTC midnight but new Date('2026-11-01T00:00') is local; mixing them shifts dates for users west of UTC. Store instants in UTC, display with Intl.DateTimeFormat and an explicit timeZone. The Temporal API is arriving in browsers to fix this — check support before relying on it.

    • Scheduling, done properly. scheduler.postTask(fn, { priority: 'background' }) and scheduler.yield() give real priorities, rather than hoping setTimeout(0) lands at the right time. AbortSignal.timeout() and AbortSignal.any() compose cancellation without hand-written timers.

    • Microtask starvation. A promise loop that keeps queuing microtasks never lets the browser render or handle input. Seniors yield to a macrotask on purpose.

    • Regex denial of service (ReDoS). Nested quantifiers like /(a+)+$/ on user input can hang the main thread for seconds. Validate input length first and avoid catastrophic backtracking patterns.

    Advanced questions (Uber, Amazon, fintech style)#

    Q: Write a deep merge that is safe against prototype pollution.

    js
    const BLOCKED = new Set(['__proto__', 'constructor', 'prototype']);
function safeMerge(target, source) {
  for (const key of Object.keys(source)) {            // own enumerable keys only
    if (BLOCKED.has(key)) continue;
    const val = source[key];
    if (val && typeof val === 'object' && !Array.isArray(val)) {
      if (!target[key] || typeof target[key] !== 'object') target[key] = {};
      safeMerge(target[key], val);
    } else {
      target[key] = val;
    }
  }
  return target;
}

    Q: A character counter shows 2 characters for a single emoji. Fix it.

    js
    const countGraphemes = s => [...new Intl.Segmenter(undefined, { granularity: 'grapheme' }).segment(s)].length;
countGraphemes('👍🏽'); // 1

    Q: Implement a priority task queue that runs at most N tasks at once, with cancellation. Keep a min-heap (or sorted array for small N) of { priority, fn, signal }; a pump() starts tasks while running < N; on finish, decrement and pump again; skip tasks whose signal.aborted is true. Mention that scheduler.postTask already provides priorities in the browser.

    Q: Our error monitoring shows thousands of "Script error." with no stack trace. Why, and what do you do? The bundles are served from a CDN origin without CORS, so the browser hides details from cross-origin scripts. Add crossorigin="anonymous" to the script tags, configure Access-Control-Allow-Origin on the CDN, upload source maps privately to the error tool, and wrap async boundaries so errors carry context.

    Q (fintech): Why can JSON.parse corrupt trade IDs, and how do you prevent it? IDs above Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER (2^53 - 1) lose precision when parsed as numbers. Ask the backend to send them as strings, or use a reviver with a big-number-aware parser; never do arithmetic on IDs.

    ← Previous00 · Overview & PlanNext →02 · HTML & CSS
    Topic 0213 min read3 diagrams

    2. HTML & CSS

    2.1 Semantic HTML, forms and document structure#

    Semantic HTML gives you accessibility, SEO, and correct keyboard behaviour for free. At apple.com scale, every div used as a button is a bug multiplied across 100+ locales.

    The page skeleton you should be able to write cold#

    html
    <!doctype html>
<html lang="en-IN" dir="ltr">
<head>
  <meta charset="utf-8" />
  <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1" />
  <title>iPhone 17 Pro – Buy – Apple (IN)</title>
  <link rel="preconnect" href="https://images.example-cdn.com" />
  <link rel="preload" as="image" href="/hero.avif" fetchpriority="high" />
  <link rel="stylesheet" href="/app.css" />
  <script type="module" src="/app.js"></script>  <!-- modules are deferred by default -->
</head>
<body>
  <a class="skip-link" href="#main">Skip to main content</a>
  <header><nav aria-label="Global">…</nav></header>
  <main id="main">
    <h1>Buy iPhone 17 Pro</h1>
    <section aria-labelledby="model-h"><h2 id="model-h">Model</h2>…</section>
  </main>
  <footer>…</footer>
</body>
</html>

    Rules worth stating in an interview#

    UseNotWhy
    <button> for actions<div onclick>Focusable, Enter/Space work, announced as button
    <a href> for navigation<button> that changes URLMiddle-click, open in new tab, crawlable
    One <h1>, no skipped levelsHeadings chosen for font sizeScreen reader users navigate by headings
    <label for> on every inputPlaceholder as labelPlaceholder disappears; not reliably announced
    <fieldset> + <legend> for radio groupsLoose radiosGroup name announced ("Storage, 256 GB")
    <ul>/<ol> for listsStacked divs"List, 6 items" orients the user
    <table> with <th scope> for tabular dataCSS grid of divsRow/column headers announced
    <dialog> for modalsCustom div overlayNative top layer, Esc, focus handling, ::backdrop
    <details>/<summary> for simple disclosureJS accordionWorks with no JS, accessible by default
    lang on <html> and on foreign phrasesNothingCorrect screen reader pronunciation

    Forms done properly (checkout, filing a case, bank transfer)#

    html
    <form novalidate>
  <label for="email">Email</label>
  <input id="email" name="email" type="email" autocomplete="email" required
         aria-describedby="email-hint email-err" aria-invalid="false" />
  <p id="email-hint">We'll send your receipt here.</p>
  <p id="email-err" class="error" hidden>Enter an email like name@example.com</p>

  <fieldset>
    <legend>Delivery</legend>
    <label><input type="radio" name="ship" value="std" checked /> Standard (free)</label>
    <label><input type="radio" name="ship" value="exp" /> Express</label>
  </fieldset>

  <label for="pin">PIN code</label>
  <input id="pin" inputmode="numeric" autocomplete="postal-code" pattern="[0-9]{6}" />
  <button type="submit">Continue to payment</button>
</form>

    Details that show craft:

    • autocomplete tokens (cc-number, one-time-code, street-address) — big conversion win and required for WCAG 1.3.5.

    • inputmode gives the right mobile keyboard without the quirks of type=number (which strips leading zeros and allows e).

    • Validation timing: validate on blur and on submit, not on every keystroke; on submit, move focus to an error summary that links to each field.

    • Native constraint validation API (input.validity, setCustomValidity) even when you render custom messages.

    • Never block paste in password or OTP fields.

    Metadata and SEO for a commerce site#

    • <title> and meta description per page; canonical URL; hreflang alternates for each locale (apple.com has ~100 storefronts).

    • Structured data (application/ld+json with Product, Offer, BreadcrumbList) for rich results.

    • Open Graph tags for sharing; server-render the content crawlers need.

    Your story (adapt): "Our filing form in PLUS used placeholder text as labels and a custom div dropdown. An accessibility audit flagged it. I rebuilt it with real labels, fieldset/legend, aria-describedby for hints and errors, and an error summary that takes focus on submit. Form completion errors dropped and the audit item closed."

    2.2 Layout mastery#

    The cascade, in the order the browser decides#

    1. Origin and importance — user-agent < author < !important author (< user !important for accessibility overrides).

    2. Cascade layers — @layer reset, base, components, utilities; later layers win regardless of specificity. Unlayered styles beat all layers.

    3. Specificity — (IDs, classes/attributes/pseudo-classes, elements). :where() has zero specificity; :is() takes its most specific argument.

    4. Source order — last one wins.

    Why layers matter at scale: a design system ships in @layer ds, product teams write unlayered or later-layer overrides, and nobody needs !important or ID selectors to win specificity wars.

    css
    @layer reset, tokens, ds, app;
@layer ds { .btn { background: var(--color-accent); } }
@layer app { .checkout .btn { background: var(--color-buy); } }  /* wins by layer, not by specificity */

    Box model and sizing#

    • box-sizing: border-box globally — width includes padding and border.

    • Margins collapse vertically between block siblings and parent/first child; they don't collapse in flex/grid containers.

    • Intrinsic sizing: min-content, max-content, fit-content(); min-width: 0 on flex items is the fix for "text won't truncate / overflows".

    • Logical properties (margin-inline-start, padding-block) make RTL (Arabic, Hebrew storefronts) work without separate stylesheets.

    Flexbox vs Grid — the one-line rule#

    Flexbox is one-dimensional and content-out; Grid is two-dimensional and layout-in. Use flex for a toolbar, a row of chips, centering. Use grid for page layout, product grids, cards that must align across rows.

    css
    /* Responsive product grid with no media queries */
.products {
  display: grid;
  grid-template-columns: repeat(auto-fill, minmax(min(100%, 260px), 1fr));
  gap: clamp(12px, 2vw, 24px);
}

/* Cards whose title, price and button align across a row */
.card { display: grid; grid-template-rows: subgrid; grid-row: span 3; }

/* Holy-grail page layout with named areas */
.page {
  display: grid;
  grid-template: "header header" auto "nav main" 1fr "footer footer" auto / 240px 1fr;
  min-height: 100dvh;
}
@media (max-width: 768px) { .page { grid-template: "header" "main" "footer" / 1fr; } }

    Flex details interviewers probe: flex: 1 = 1 1 0%; flex: auto = 1 1 auto; flex-basis beats width; gap works in flex; margin-left: auto pushes an item to the end.

    Positioning and sticky#

    • relative (offset, creates containing block), absolute (relative to nearest positioned ancestor), fixed (viewport — unless an ancestor has transform, filter or contain, which breaks fixed modals), sticky (sticks within its scroll container; fails if any ancestor has overflow: hidden).

    • Apple's product pages use sticky sub-navs and sticky "Buy" bars — know why sticky silently stops working (overflow ancestor, no top set, parent too short).

    Stacking contexts — the z-index question#

    z-index only competes within the same stacking context. A new context is created by: position + z-index (not auto), opacity < 1, transform, filter, will-change, isolation: isolate, contain: paint, flex/grid children with z-index, and position: fixed/sticky.

    Classic bug: a dropdown with z-index: 9999 hides under a header with z-index: 10 because the dropdown's parent card has transform and its own stacking context. Fixes: render overlays in a portal at the body level, or use the native top layer (<dialog>, popover attribute), which sits above everything without z-index.

    html
    <button popovertarget="menu">Options</button>
<div id="menu" popover>…</div>   <!-- top layer, light-dismiss, Esc closes, no z-index -->

    Plus CSS anchor positioning (anchor-name, position-anchor, position-try) to place tooltips next to their trigger without JS measurement — mention it as the modern direction while noting browser support.

    Modern CSS you should name-drop with substance#

    FeatureReal use
    Container queries @container (min-width: 400px)A product card that changes layout based on its slot, not the viewport — essential for design-system components
    :has()form:has(:invalid) .submit { opacity:.5 }; style a card when its checkbox is checked — no JS
    Custom propertiesTheming, dark mode, design tokens; they cascade and can change at runtime
    clamp(), min(), max()Fluid typography and spacing
    aspect-ratioReserve media space → prevents CLS
    content-visibility: auto + contain-intrinsic-sizeSkip rendering off-screen sections of long pages
    dvh/svh/lvh unitsFull-height heroes that don't jump when mobile Safari's toolbar hides
    Native nestingLess tooling; still keep specificity low
    @supportsProgressive enhancement
    View transitionsSmooth page/state transitions (document.startViewTransition)

    2.3 Responsive design, images, typography, animation#

    Responsive strategy (say it in this order)#

    1. Mobile-first base styles, enhance with min-width media queries at content-driven breakpoints (where the layout breaks, not device names).

    2. Fluid between breakpoints with clamp() so there are fewer breakpoints.

    3. Component-level responsiveness with container queries.

    4. Input-aware: @media (hover: hover) and (pointer: fine) for hover effects; 44×44 pt minimum touch targets (Apple HIG), 24×24 CSS px minimum per WCAG 2.2.

    5. User preferences: prefers-reduced-motion, prefers-color-scheme, prefers-contrast, forced-colors (Windows High Contrast).

    css
    :root {
  --step-0: clamp(1rem, 0.95rem + 0.25vw, 1.125rem);
  --step-4: clamp(2rem, 1.4rem + 3vw, 3.5rem);   /* hero headline grows smoothly */
}
h1 { font-size: var(--step-4); line-height: 1.07; letter-spacing: -0.015em; }

    Responsive images — the LCP lever#

    html
    <picture>
  <source type="image/avif" srcset="hero-800.avif 800w, hero-1600.avif 1600w, hero-2400.avif 2400w" sizes="100vw" />
  <source type="image/webp" srcset="hero-800.webp 800w, hero-1600.webp 1600w, hero-2400.webp 2400w" sizes="100vw" />
  <img src="hero-1600.jpg" alt="iPhone 17 Pro in Cosmic Orange, back view"
       width="2400" height="1350" fetchpriority="high" decoding="async" />
</picture>

<!-- below the fold -->
<img src="card.webp" alt="" loading="lazy" width="600" height="400" />

    • srcset + sizes lets the browser pick by viewport width and device pixel ratio (Retina = 2×/3×).

    • Art direction (different crop on mobile) → <source media="(max-width: 734px)">.

    • width/height (or aspect-ratio) reserve space → no CLS.

    • Never lazy-load the LCP image; give it fetchpriority="high".

    • Decorative images get alt=""; meaningful ones describe the content, not "image of".

    Typography#

    • Variable fonts (one file, many weights) + font-display: swap or optional; preload only the 1–2 critical weights; subset by locale (unicode-range) — a huge win for multi-script sites (Devanagari, CJK).

    • Reduce swap layout shift with size-adjust, ascent-override on the fallback @font-face.

    • Line length 45–75 characters (max-width: 65ch); text-wrap: balance for headlines, text-wrap: pretty for paragraphs.

    • Truncation: text-overflow: ellipsis (single line) or -webkit-line-clamp (multi-line), with the full text available to screen readers.

    Animation at Apple quality#

    Apple product pages are famous for scroll-driven animation. The rules behind smooth motion:

    • Animate only transform and opacity — these run on the compositor thread and skip layout and paint. Animating top, width, height, box-shadow triggers layout/paint every frame.

    • will-change: transform only right before animating, removed after (each layer costs GPU memory).

    • Use CSS scroll-driven animations (animation-timeline: view()/scroll()) or IntersectionObserver instead of scroll listeners.

    • Image-sequence "video scrubbing" (the AirPods-style rotate-on-scroll) draws frames to a <canvas> in requestAnimationFrame, preloading frames progressively.

    • Respect prefers-reduced-motion — swap parallax/zoom for a simple fade.

    • FLIP technique (First, Last, Invert, Play) for layout animations; or the View Transitions API.

    css
    .reveal { animation: fade-up linear both; animation-timeline: view(); animation-range: entry 0% cover 30%; }
@keyframes fade-up { from { opacity: 0; transform: translateY(40px); } }
@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) { .reveal { animation: none; } }

    Dark mode and theming#

    css
    :root { color-scheme: light dark; --bg: #fff; --fg: #1d1d1f; }
@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark) { :root { --bg: #000; --fg: #f5f5f7; } }
[data-theme="dark"] { --bg: #000; --fg: #f5f5f7; }  /* user override */
body { background: var(--bg); color: var(--fg); }

    color-scheme also fixes native controls and scrollbars. Prevent the flash of wrong theme by setting data-theme in a tiny inline script in <head> before first paint.

    Cross-browser (Safari matters at Apple)#

    • Test WebKit specifically — Playwright ships a WebKit engine; real iOS Safari via simulator or device.

    • Common Safari gotchas: 100vh with dynamic toolbars (use dvh), date input styling, position: sticky inside overflow containers, gap in older flex, smooth scrolling differences, autoplay video requiring muted playsinline.

    • Strategy: progressive enhancement with @supports, Browserslist-driven Autoprefixer, and a defined support matrix (e.g. last 2 versions + Safari iOS 16+).

    2.4 Interview questions and model answers#

    Q: Center a div — give me three ways and when you'd use each. A: Grid place-items: center on the parent (simplest, two axes). Flex justify-content + align-items when it's part of a row of items. Absolute inset: 0; margin: auto with known size, or top/left 50% + translate(-50%,-50%) for overlays. In a design system I'd make a Center layout primitive so nobody rewrites it.

    Q: My modal's position: fixed is positioned relative to a card, not the viewport. Why? A: An ancestor has transform, filter, perspective, or contain, which makes it the containing block for fixed descendants. Fix by rendering the modal in a portal at body, or with <dialog>.showModal() which uses the top layer.

    Q: How do you prevent layout shift from images, ads and fonts? A: Reserve space: width/height or aspect-ratio on media, fixed min-height slots for ads or late banners, size-adjust fallback fonts, and never insert content above existing content unless it's in response to a user action. Animate with transform, not properties that move other elements.

    Q: How do you organize CSS for 50 engineers? A: Design tokens as custom properties; @layer ordering (reset, tokens, design system, app); component-scoped styles (CSS Modules or a zero-runtime CSS-in-JS like vanilla-extract) so class names don't collide; Stylelint with rules against IDs, !important, and raw hex values; logical properties for RTL; visual regression tests in CI.

    Q: CSS-in-JS runtime vs CSS Modules vs Tailwind? A: Runtime CSS-in-JS (styled-components) injects styles during render — cost on every render and awkward with React Server Components and streaming. Zero-runtime options (CSS Modules, vanilla-extract, Linaria) or utility CSS (Tailwind) compile to static CSS. For a performance-critical commerce site I'd choose static CSS with tokens; Tailwind if the team wants speed and consistency with a configured token scale.

    Q: Build a pixel-perfect component from a Figma spec — how do you work? A: Inspect tokens (not raw values) in Dev Mode, confirm states (hover, focus, disabled, error, loading, empty, long text, RTL), confirm breakpoints, build with tokens, then compare side-by-side at 1× and 2× with an overlay, and add a visual regression snapshot. Anything the spec doesn't cover, I raise with the designer before coding, not after.

    Q: What is reflow vs repaint vs composite? A: Reflow (layout) recomputes geometry — expensive and can cascade to the whole tree. Repaint redraws pixels without geometry change (color). Composite moves already-painted layers (transform/opacity) on the GPU — cheapest. Goal: keep animations in composite-only properties.

    Cheat lines#

    • "Flex is one-dimensional content-out; grid is two-dimensional layout-in."

    • "z-index only competes within a stacking context — portals or the top layer fix overlay bugs."

    • "Animate only transform and opacity; everything else costs layout or paint."

    • "Container queries make components responsive to their slot, which is what a design system needs."

    • "Never lazy-load the LCP image; always reserve space for media."

    2.5 Level up: HTML and CSS at big-company scale#

    How top companies apply it#

    • Uber: Styletron (used by m.uber and Base Web) generates atomic CSS — one class per unique declaration — so the stylesheet stops growing as features are added and unused CSS is never loaded. m.uber also dropped custom web fonts entirely and hand-tuned SVGs (source).

    • Atlassian: moved to build-time CSS-in-JS (Compiled) so styles become static CSS with no runtime cost, and enforces design tokens with lint rules instead of code review (source). Its CSS API only accepts tokenized values, so padding: 8 won't compile (source).

    • Amazon (Cloudscape): themes plus a "comfortable" and "compact" density mode — the same components serve casual users and dense operations consoles (source).

    Staff-level insights#

    • Atomic vs scoped CSS. Atomic CSS caps total CSS size (rules are reused) but moves weight into HTML class lists and makes debugging harder. CSS Modules keep readable CSS but grow linearly. Big apps with many teams lean atomic or build-time extraction; content sites often stay with scoped CSS.

    • Style recalculation cost grows with (number of elements affected) × (selector complexity). Broad selectors such as .app * or heavy :has() on large lists make every DOM change expensive. In DevTools, look for long "Recalculate Style" entries and the "Selector stats" panel.

    • Containment as a contract. contain: layout paint style tells the browser that a widget's changes can't affect the rest of the page, so layout stays local. content-visibility: auto skips rendering off-screen sections entirely; pair it with contain-intrinsic-size so the scrollbar doesn't jump.

    • Modal scroll-lock jank. Setting overflow: hidden on body removes the scrollbar and shifts the whole page sideways on Windows. Fix with scrollbar-gutter: stable on the root, and overscroll-behavior: contain on the modal so scrolling doesn't chain to the page.

    • Wide-gamut colour. Apple displays support Display P3. Define brand colours in oklch() or color(display-p3 …) with an sRGB fallback, and gate with @media (color-gamut: p3). Use color-mix() to derive hover and disabled states from tokens instead of hard-coding shades.

    • Animatable custom properties. @property --angle { syntax: '<angle>'; inherits: false; initial-value: 0deg; } lets you animate a variable (gradients, progress rings) on the compositor-friendly path, which plain custom properties can't do.

    • WCAG 1.4.12 Text Spacing. Users can override line height, letter and word spacing. Fixed-height containers with overflow: hidden clip text when they do, so design for growth with min-heights rather than fixed heights.

    • Layer explosion on mobile Safari. Every will-change or 3D transform can create a GPU layer. Hundreds of them (a card grid with will-change: transform on every card) exhaust memory and crash tabs on older iPhones. Promote layers only while animating.

    • Internationalisation at the CSS level. Logical properties (margin-inline-start), :dir(rtl), and allowing for roughly 30–40% text expansion when English is translated (German labels are much longer) are what make one stylesheet serve 100+ storefronts.

    Advanced questions#

    Q: Opening our modal makes the page jump horizontally. Why, and how do you fix it? Locking body scroll removes the scrollbar, the viewport gets wider, and centered content shifts. Use scrollbar-gutter: stable on html (or compensate with padding equal to the scrollbar width), overscroll-behavior: contain on the dialog, and the native <dialog> so the background is inert.

    Q: Design the CSS architecture for 40 teams shipping to one site in 100 locales. Tokens as CSS variables (with P3 and dark variants), cascade layers for reset, tokens, design system, and app, build-time or atomic styling so CSS size doesn't grow per team, lint rules banning raw values (the Atlassian model), logical properties for RTL, container queries for components, and visual regression per locale group in CI.

    Q: A Performance trace shows 120 ms "Recalculate Style" on every keystroke in a search box. Diagnose. Likely a class toggled high in the tree (e.g., on body or a list container) that invalidates thousands of descendants, combined with complex selectors. Move the state class to the smallest subtree, simplify selectors (avoid descendant universal selectors and broad :has()), add contain on the results list, and virtualize it.

    ← Previous01 · JavaScript & TypeScriptNext →03 · React, Next.js & State
    Topic 0318 min read4 diagrams

    3. React, Next.js & State Architecture

    3.1 React's rendering model#

    React separates deciding what changed (render phase — pure, interruptible) from applying it to the DOM (commit phase — synchronous, can't be interrupted). Every performance answer you give should map to one of these phases.

    Slow renders are fixed by rendering less (memo, state colocation, transitions); slow commits by touching less DOM (virtualization, fewer layout effects).

    What triggers a render#

    1. State update in the component (setState, useReducer dispatch).

    2. Its parent re-rendered (props don't need to change — this surprises people).

    3. A context it consumes changed value (by Object.is).

    4. A subscribed external store changed (useSyncExternalStore).

    Reconciliation — how React diffs#

    • Different element type at the same position → unmount the whole old subtree, mount a new one (state lost).

    • Same type → keep the DOM node / component instance, update changed props, recurse into children.

    • Lists are matched by key. Using the array index as key breaks when items are inserted, removed, or reordered: state (like an input's text or a checked box) sticks to the wrong row. Use a stable ID from data.

    • Trick worth knowing: changing key on purpose resets a component's state (<CheckoutForm key={productId} />).

    Batching and priorities#

    • React 18+ batches all updates in the same tick, including inside promises and timeouts (automatic batching).

    • Fiber splits rendering into units of work so React can pause, prioritize urgent updates (typing, clicks), and resume.

    • startTransition / useTransition mark an update as non-urgent: typing in a search box stays instant while the filtered list renders in the background and can be discarded if the user types again.

    • useDeferredValue gives you a lagging copy of a value to render the expensive part with.

    tsx
    function ProductSearch({ products }: { products: Product[] }) {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState('');
  const deferredQuery = useDeferredValue(query);              // lags behind while busy
  const results = useMemo(() => filterProducts(products, deferredQuery), [products, deferredQuery]);
  const stale = query !== deferredQuery;
  return (
    <>
      <input value={query} onChange={e => setQuery(e.target.value)} aria-label="Search products" />
      <div style={{ opacity: stale ? 0.6 : 1 }}><ResultList items={results} /></div>
    </>
  );
}

    Suspense and streaming#

    • <Suspense fallback> shows a fallback while children wait for code (React.lazy) or data (a framework/RSC data source or use(promise)).

    • With SSR streaming, the server sends the shell immediately and streams each Suspense boundary's HTML as its data resolves; selective hydration hydrates the part the user interacts with first.

    • Place boundaries around independently-useful regions (reviews, recommendations), not around every component — too many spinners is worse than one.

    React Compiler and memoization#

    The React Compiler (stable since late 2025) auto-memoizes components and values at build time, so manual useMemo/useCallback/memo becomes mostly unnecessary in new code. Know both worlds: how to memoize by hand (interviews still ask) and that the industry direction is compiler-driven. Requirement: your components must follow the Rules of React (pure render, no mutation of props/state).

    Error boundaries#

    Class components with getDerivedStateFromError / componentDidCatch (or react-error-boundary). They catch render errors only — not event handlers or async code. Wrap each independent page region so a broken recommendations widget doesn't blank the checkout button, and log to your monitoring tool with the component stack.

    Your story (adapt): "A case table on PLUS used index as the row key. When a new filing arrived via polling and was inserted at the top, the expanded row and the checked checkbox jumped to a different case — a real risk in a legal product. I switched to the case ID as key and added a test that inserts a row and asserts the selection stays on the same case."

    3.2 Hooks in depth#

    Hooks are stored as a linked list on the component's fiber, read in call order each render. That's why they can't be called conditionally — the order is the identity.

    useState / useReducer#

    • Updates are queued, not applied immediately; reading state right after setState gives the old value.

    • Use the functional updater when the new value depends on the old: setCount(c => c + 1) — safe across batching and stale closures.

    • Lazy initial state for expensive setup: useState(() => readFromStorage()).

    • useReducer when state transitions are complex or several fields change together (a checkout wizard, a form with dependent fields). Reducers are pure and easy to unit test.

    tsx
    type Step = 'cart' | 'shipping' | 'payment' | 'review';
type State = { step: Step; shipping?: Address; payment?: PaymentMethod; error?: string };
type Action = { type: 'SET_SHIPPING'; address: Address } | { type: 'SET_PAYMENT'; method: PaymentMethod } | { type: 'BACK' };

function checkoutReducer(s: State, a: Action): State {
  switch (a.type) {
    case 'SET_SHIPPING': return { ...s, shipping: a.address, step: 'payment', error: undefined };
    case 'SET_PAYMENT':  return { ...s, payment: a.method, step: 'review' };
    case 'BACK':         return { ...s, step: s.step === 'review' ? 'payment' : s.step === 'payment' ? 'shipping' : 'cart' };
  }
}

    useEffect — what it's for and what it's not for#

    Effects synchronize with something outside React (network, subscriptions, DOM APIs, timers). They are not for transforming data for render or reacting to events.

    Anti-patternBetter
    Effect that computes fullName from props into stateCompute during render (or useMemo if expensive)
    Effect that resets state when a prop changeskey on the component
    Effect that POSTs when a button state flipsDo it in the click handler
    Fetching in effects with no cleanupAbort on cleanup, or use React Query / framework data loading
    Chained effects updating each otherOne event handler or a reducer

    Correct fetch-in-effect (when you must):

    tsx
    useEffect(() => {
  const ctrl = new AbortController();
  setStatus('loading');
  fetch(`/api/orders?page=${page}`, { signal: ctrl.signal })
    .then(r => { if (!r.ok) throw new Error(String(r.status)); return r.json(); })
    .then(data => { setOrders(data); setStatus('success'); })
    .catch(e => { if (e.name !== 'AbortError') setStatus('error'); });
  return () => ctrl.abort();   // cancels the stale request → no race condition
}, [page]);

    In dev Strict Mode, React mounts → unmounts → remounts once to prove your cleanup works. If something breaks, the cleanup is wrong.

    useLayoutEffect#

    Runs after DOM mutation, before paint. Use it to measure and reposition (a tooltip that must flip above when near the viewport bottom) without a visible flicker. It blocks paint, so keep it tiny. Not run on the server.

    useRef#

    A mutable box that survives renders and doesn't trigger re-renders. Uses: DOM nodes (focus management), timers, the latest callback (avoid stale closures), previous value, instance flags.

    tsx
    // "latest ref" pattern: stable function identity that always sees fresh props
function useEvent<T extends (...a: any[]) => any>(fn: T) {
  const ref = useRef(fn);
  useLayoutEffect(() => { ref.current = fn; });
  return useCallback((...args: Parameters<T>) => ref.current(...args), []);
}

    useMemo, useCallback, React.memo#

    • memo(Component) skips re-render if props are shallow-equal.

    • It only works if props are stable → useCallback for function props and useMemo for object/array props.

    • Measure first (React Profiler). Memoizing cheap things adds cost. With the React Compiler, most of this is automatic.

    • Better first moves: move state down (colocate) and lift content up (pass expensive children as children so they don't re-render with the parent's state).

    useContext — and why it's not a state manager#

    Every consumer re-renders when the provider's value changes identity. Fixes: memoize the value, split contexts (state vs dispatch), keep fast-changing values out of context, or use a store with selectors (Zustand, Redux, useSyncExternalStore).

    useSyncExternalStore, useId, useImperativeHandle, useOptimistic, useActionState#

    • useSyncExternalStore(subscribe, getSnapshot) — tear-free subscription to external stores (window size, navigator.onLine, a WebSocket store).

    • useId — stable, SSR-safe IDs for aria-labelledby/htmlFor pairs.

    • useImperativeHandle — expose a small API (focus(), open()) from a component; use sparingly.

    • useOptimistic (React 19) — show the optimistic result while an action is in flight; reverts if it fails.

    • useActionState + form action (React 19) — pending/error state for form submissions.

    Custom hooks you should be able to write live#

    tsx
    export function useDebouncedValue<T>(value: T, delay = 300) {
  const [v, setV] = useState(value);
  useEffect(() => { const t = setTimeout(() => setV(value), delay); return () => clearTimeout(t); }, [value, delay]);
  return v;
}

export function useMediaQuery(query: string) {
  return useSyncExternalStore(
    cb => { const m = matchMedia(query); m.addEventListener('change', cb); return () => m.removeEventListener('change', cb); },
    () => matchMedia(query).matches,
    () => false, // server snapshot
  );
}

export function useIntersection<T extends Element>(opts?: IntersectionObserverInit) {
  const ref = useRef<T>(null);
  const [visible, setVisible] = useState(false);
  useEffect(() => {
    const el = ref.current; if (!el) return;
    const io = new IntersectionObserver(([e]) => setVisible(e.isIntersecting), opts);
    io.observe(el);
    return () => io.disconnect();
  }, [opts?.root, opts?.rootMargin, opts?.threshold]);
  return [ref, visible] as const;
}

export function usePrevious<T>(value: T) {
  const ref = useRef<T>();
  useEffect(() => { ref.current = value; }, [value]);
  return ref.current;
}

    3.3 State architecture at scale#

    The senior answer starts by classifying state, then picks a tool per class. Most "Redux vs X" debates disappear once you do this.

    State typeExample (Apple Store)Where it livesTool
    Server / remoteProduct, price, stock, delivery datesServer is source of truth; client caches itTanStack Query, SWR, Apollo, RSC
    URLSelected color, storage, filter, page, sortThe URL — shareable, back-button worksRouter search params (nuqs, Next useSearchParams)
    Global clientBag count, signed-in user, locale, feature flagsA small storeZustand, Redux Toolkit, Context for rarely-changing values
    Local UIAccordion open, hover, input textThe componentuseState / useReducer
    FormCheckout fields, validationForm libraryReact Hook Form + Zod
    DerivedTotal price, filtered listComputed, never storedPlain expressions / selectors / useMemo
    PersistedRecently viewed, draft case filingStorage + synclocalStorage / IndexedDB with a store wrapper

    Rule of thumb you can say: "80% of what teams put in Redux is server cache. Move that to React Query and the remaining global state is small enough for Zustand or Context."

    Server state with TanStack Query (the mechanics to explain)#

    • Cache keyed by query key (['product', id, locale]); staleTime controls when data is considered stale; gcTime when unused data is dropped.

    • Stale-while-revalidate: show cached data immediately, refetch in the background on mount, focus, or reconnect.

    • Request deduplication: 5 components asking for the same key → one request.

    • Mutations with optimistic updates and rollback; invalidateQueries after success.

    • Prefetch on hover/viewport (queryClient.prefetchQuery) for instant navigation.

    tsx
    function useAddToBag() {
  const qc = useQueryClient();
  return useMutation({
    mutationFn: (item: BagItem) => api.post('/bag', item, { headers: { 'Idempotency-Key': item.clientId } }),
    onMutate: async item => {
      await qc.cancelQueries({ queryKey: ['bag'] });
      const prev = qc.getQueryData<Bag>(['bag']);
      qc.setQueryData<Bag>(['bag'], b => ({ ...b!, items: [...b!.items, item] }));   // instant UI
      return { prev };
    },
    onError: (_e, _item, ctx) => qc.setQueryData(['bag'], ctx?.prev),                 // rollback
    onSettled: () => qc.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['bag'] }),                    // reconcile with server
  });
}

    Redux Toolkit vs Zustand vs Context vs signals#

    Redux ToolkitZustandContextJotai / signals
    Mental modelSingle store, actions, reducersHook-based store with selectorsDependency injectionAtomic, fine-grained
    Re-render controlSelectors (useSelector)SelectorsAll consumers re-renderPer-atom
    BoilerplateMedium (RTK reduced it)Very lowLowLow
    DevTools / time travelExcellentVia middlewareNoneSome
    Best forLarge teams wanting strict conventions, complex flows, audit trailMost apps' small global stateTheme, locale, auth userHighly interactive editors

    Pick and justify: "For a big multi-team app with complex workflows like case management, Redux Toolkit's conventions and DevTools help onboarding and debugging. For a commerce front end where most state is server data, React Query + a tiny Zustand store is simpler and faster."

    Avoiding re-render storms#

    • Subscribe with narrow selectors (useBag(s => s.count), not the whole bag).

    • Keep objects immutable so === comparisons work.

    • Split high-frequency state (cursor position, scroll, timers) out of global stores.

    • Normalize collections ({ byId, allIds }) so updating one entity doesn't touch the whole list.

    State machines for complex UI#

    For flows with many states and guards (checkout, payment with 3-D Secure, file upload with retry, a video player), a state machine (XState or a typed reducer) prevents impossible states like "submitting and editing at once". Mention: states, events, guards, and that the chart doubles as documentation for QA and design.

    Your story (adapt): "PLUS had case data copied into Redux from several API calls, and screens went stale after edits. I moved server data to React Query with per-entity keys, kept only UI and session state in Redux, and used invalidateQueries after mutations. That removed a lot of manual sync code and the stale-data bugs stopped."

    3.4 Next.js rendering strategies#

    Pick a rendering mode per route, by how fresh and how personal the data is. That one sentence is the senior answer.

    ModeWhen HTML is builtFreshnessPersonalized?Best forCost
    CSRIn the browser after JS loadsLiveYesLogged-in dashboards behind authSlow first paint, weak SEO, big JS
    SSRPer request on the serverLiveYesAccount pages, search results, cartServer cost per request; TTFB depends on slowest data
    SSGAt build timeAs of last buildNoMarketing, docs, product storiesRebuild to change
    ISR / revalidateBuild, then regenerated in background after N seconds or on demandSeconds–minutes staleNoProduct pages with prices that change occasionallyStale window; cache invalidation design
    Streaming SSR + SuspenseShell immediately, slow parts streamedLiveYesPages mixing fast and slow dataNeeds good boundary design
    Partial prerendering (PPR)Static shell from CDN + dynamic holes streamed in the same responseStatic + livePartlyProduct page: static content, personalized price/stock/bagNewer feature; check maturity
    React Server ComponentsComponents run on the server, send a serialized tree, zero client JS for themPer fetch cacheYesData-heavy, non-interactive partsLearning curve; 'use client' boundaries

    How you'd render an Apple Store product page (great interview example)#

    • Product story, specs, images → static (SSG/ISR, CDN-cached per locale).

    • Price and availability → revalidated frequently (ISR with on-demand revalidation when pricing changes), or streamed.

    • Delivery estimate by PIN code, trade-in value, bag count → dynamic, personalized, streamed in Suspense boundaries or fetched client-side.

    • Configurator (color/storage picker) → client component island; selection mirrored in the URL.

    App Router mechanics to know#

    • Server Components by default; 'use client' marks the boundary — everything imported below it ships to the browser. Keep client islands small and deep in the tree.

    • You can pass Server Components as children into Client Components (composition keeps them server-rendered).

    • Data fetching in Server Components with fetch + caching options (cache, next: { revalidate, tags }), and revalidateTag() / revalidatePath() after mutations.

    • Server Actions ('use server') for mutations from forms — treat them as public HTTP endpoints: validate input and check auth inside every action.

    • loading.tsx = automatic Suspense boundary; error.tsx = error boundary; not-found.tsx.

    • Middleware for locale redirects, A/B bucketing, auth gating at the edge — keep it light.

    • next/image (resizing, AVIF/WebP, lazy by default, priority for LCP), next/font (self-hosted, no layout shift), next/script strategies.

    Hydration — and its errors#

    Hydration attaches event handlers to server-rendered HTML; React expects the first client render to match. Mismatches come from Date.now(), Math.random(), window checks during render, locale-formatted dates differing between server and browser, or invalid HTML nesting (<div> inside <p>). Fixes: render those in an effect, pass server values down, use suppressHydrationWarning only for unavoidable cases like timestamps.

    Hydration cost is the hidden performance tax: SSR gives fast FCP/LCP but the page isn't interactive until JS downloads and hydrates. RSC, islands, and selective hydration all exist to cut this.

    Your story (adapt): "Our public-facing pages were client-rendered and had poor LCP on mobile. I moved the static content to Server Components, kept only the interactive filters as client components, and wrapped the slow 'recent activity' data in a Suspense boundary so the rest streamed first. Client JS for the route dropped substantially and LCP improved."

    3.5 Interview questions and model answers#

    Q: A component re-renders too often. Walk me through how you'd debug it. A: Reproduce, then React DevTools Profiler with "record why each component rendered". Typical causes: parent state changes (fix by colocating state or passing children), new object/function props every render (stabilize or let the compiler do it), context value changing identity (memoize/split), or a store subscription that's too broad (narrow selector). I confirm with a before/after profile, not intuition.

    Q: Controlled vs uncontrolled inputs? A: Controlled: React state is the source of truth, value + onChange — needed for instant validation and formatting (card number spacing). Uncontrolled: the DOM holds the value, read via ref or FormData — fewer renders, good for large forms. React Hook Form uses uncontrolled inputs plus refs for performance.

    Q: Why can't hooks be called in conditions? A: React identifies each hook by its call position in the fiber's hook list. A condition shifts positions between renders, so state from one hook would be read by another.

    Q: How do you share logic between components? A: Custom hooks for stateful logic, composition (children, render props) for UI structure, HOCs only for legacy or cross-cutting wrappers. Headless components (logic + a11y, no styles) for a design system.

    Q: How do portals handle events? A: The DOM node lives elsewhere, but React events bubble through the React tree, so a click in a portaled modal still reaches its React parent's handlers. Focus and aria relationships must be handled manually.

    Q: Explain key beyond lists. A: key is identity. Change it and React remounts — handy for resetting a form when the product changes or restarting an animation.

    Q: Server Components vs SSR? A: SSR renders components to HTML on the server, then the same code ships and hydrates in the browser. Server Components never ship their code to the client at all — their output is a serialized tree. They can be combined: RSC output is itself server-rendered to HTML for the first load.

    Q: How would you migrate a large React app from Pages Router (or CRA) to App Router? A: Incrementally — both routers coexist in Next.js. Move leaf routes first, convert layouts, mark interactive parts 'use client', move data fetching from getServerSideProps into Server Components, add caching tags, and measure Web Vitals per route as you go.

    Cheat lines#

    • "Render is pure and interruptible; commit is synchronous — optimize the phase that's slow."

    • "Classify state first: server, URL, global, local, form, derived."

    • "Effects are for syncing with the outside world, not for deriving data."

    • "Keep client components small and deep; server-render everything else."

    • "Index as key is a correctness bug, not just a performance bug."

    3.6 Level up: React at big-company scale#

    How top companies apply it#
