    • Netflix: every page is server-rendered React, but on the logged-out landing page the client-side React was removed and replaced with a little vanilla JS; React for the sign-up flow is prefetched while the user reads (source). This is the ancestor of today's islands and Server Components thinking.

    • Walmart (Electrode): walmart.com runs on React + Node SSR. Two optimisations worth quoting: render only above-the-fold content on the server and let the client render the rest, and cache server-rendered components that are the same for everyone (header, footer, carousels) instead of re-running renderToString each request (source).

    • Uber (m.uber): swapped React (about 45 kB) for Preact (about 3 kB) to fit a 50 kB budget, used shouldComponentUpdate heavily, and planned to pass only flat primitive props so pure components could skip renders cheaply (source).

    • Spotify: one React + TypeScript codebase now serves both the web player and the desktop app (inside Chromium Embedded Framework), with platform differences behind adapters for playback, auth and offline (source).

    Staff-level insights#

    • The SSR data-leak bug. On a server, module-level variables are shared by every request. A const cache = new Map() at module scope that stores "the current user's cart" will serve user A's data to user B under load. Keep per-request data in request-scoped context (React cache() in Server Components, or a context created per request) — this is a real production incident class.

    • Component SSR caching has a personalisation trap. Cache only components whose output depends on nothing user-specific; include locale, currency, A/B bucket and device class in the cache key, or you'll show Indian prices to US visitors.

    • Hydration is the hidden tax. Server rendering makes content visible fast but not interactive. Measure it with INP and "hydration" long tasks. Fixes, in order of impact: send less client code (Server Components, islands), split hydration with Suspense (selective hydration lets React hydrate what the user clicks first and replays that click), and defer non-critical widgets.

    • Hydration mismatches from Intl. Node's ICU data and the browser's can format dates or numbers differently (narrow no-break spaces in some locales). Format on one side only, or pass the formatted string from the server.

    • Tearing with external stores. In concurrent rendering, reading a mutable store directly can show two different values in one frame. useSyncExternalStore exists to prevent that; Redux, Zustand and others use it internally.

    • Context is not a store. Every consumer re-renders when the provider value changes. At scale, use a store with selectors or split contexts; for a value that changes 60 times a second (playhead position), use a ref plus a subscription and update the DOM directly.

    • Server Components security boundary. Anything passed from a Server Component to a Client Component is serialized into the page. Never pass whole user records or secrets as props — select the exact fields. Server Actions are public endpoints: validate input and check authorization inside every one.

    • React Compiler bail-outs. The compiler skips components that break the Rules of React (mutating props, reading refs during render). Large codebases enable its ESLint rule first and fix violations before turning it on.

    Advanced questions#

    Q: Under load, some users briefly see another user's name in the header. The app is Next.js with SSR. What happened? Almost certainly request-scoped data stored in a module-level variable or a singleton (an API client holding the "current user", a memo cache keyed only by route). Fix: per-request context, no mutable module state, add a test that renders two users concurrently, and review CDN caching (private for personalised HTML).

    Q: Product pages render at 10,000 requests per second and SSR CPU is the bottleneck. What do you do? Cache the full HTML at the CDN for anonymous traffic with stale-while-revalidate, stream the page so the shell isn't blocked on slow data, cache identical sub-trees (Walmart's approach) with correct keys, move personalised fragments to client fetches or edge includes, and profile renderToString hot spots. Scale horizontally last.

    Q: Write a tiny store hook with selectors that doesn't tear.

    tsx
    function createStore<T>(initial: T) {
  let state = initial;
  const listeners = new Set<() => void>();
  return {
    get: () => state,
    set: (fn: (s: T) => T) => { state = fn(state); listeners.forEach(l => l()); },
    subscribe: (l: () => void) => { listeners.add(l); return () => listeners.delete(l); },
  };
}
const bag = createStore({ items: [] as BagItem[] });

export function useBag<S>(selector: (s: { items: BagItem[] }) => S) {
  return useSyncExternalStore(bag.subscribe, () => selector(bag.get()), () => selector(bag.get()));
  // selector must return a stable value (a primitive or memoized object), or React re-renders forever
}
// const count = useBag(s => s.items.length);

    Q: When would you choose not to use React for a page? When the page is mostly static content, its interactivity is small (a menu, a form), and its audience is on slow devices: ship server HTML with a few kilobytes of JavaScript, as Netflix did on its landing page, and load React only for app-like flows.

    ← Previous02 · HTML & CSSNext →04 · Design Systems
    Topic 0413 min read3 diagrams

    4. Design Systems & Visual Craft

    4.1 Design system architecture#

    A design system is a product for other engineers: tokens, components, docs, tooling, and a contribution process. At Apple's Retail and Marcom scale — dozens of teams, 100+ storefronts, light/dark, RTL — it's what keeps every page feeling like one brand.

    Dark mode, a rebrand, or a new storefront changes the token layer; nothing above it is edited.

    The layers (bottom to top)#

    1. Design tokens — named decisions, not values. Three tiers:

      • Primitive (raw palette): blue-600: #0071e3, space-4: 16px

      • Semantic (meaning): color-action-primary → blue-600, color-text-secondary, radius-control

      • Component (optional): button-primary-bg → color-action-primary Components only reference semantic or component tokens, so a rebrand or dark mode changes one mapping, not 400 components.

    2. Foundations — typography scale, spacing scale, grid/breakpoints, elevation, motion curves and durations, iconography.

    3. Primitives — unstyled-ish building blocks: Box, Stack, Inline, Grid, Text, VisuallyHidden, FocusRing.

    4. Components — Button, TextField, Select, Checkbox, Dialog, Tabs, Tooltip, Toast, Popover, Combobox.

    5. Patterns — composed solutions: product card, price block, swatch picker, form layouts, empty states, error summary.

    6. Product pages — teams assemble patterns; anything reused 3+ times is a candidate to move down a layer.

    Token pipeline#

    Tokens live in a source format (JSON, often W3C Design Tokens format) synced from Figma Variables, then Style Dictionary (or similar) generates CSS custom properties, TS constants, iOS/Android values. One source of truth across web and native.

    json
    { "color": { "action": { "primary": { "$value": "{color.blue.600}", "$type": "color" } } } }
    css
    :root            { --color-action-primary: #0071e3; --color-bg: #fff; }
[data-theme=dark]{ --color-action-primary: #2997ff; --color-bg: #000; }
.btn-primary     { background: var(--color-action-primary); border-radius: var(--radius-control); }

    Architecture decisions to discuss (with your recommendation)#

    DecisionOptionsRecommend for Apple-scale web
    StylingRuntime CSS-in-JS, CSS Modules, zero-runtime (vanilla-extract), Tailwind with tokensZero-runtime or CSS Modules + CSS variables: no runtime cost, works with RSC/streaming
    Behaviour + a11yBuild yourself, headless library (React Aria, Radix), full kitHeadless library for complex widgets (combobox, date picker) — a11y is hard; own the styling
    DistributionOne package vs per-component packagesOne versioned package with tree-shakable ESM exports; monorepo with Changesets
    FrameworkReact-only vs Web ComponentsReact for app teams; Web Components if many frameworks or AEM-rendered pages consume it
    ThemingClass swap vs CSS variablesCSS variables at :root / data attribute; supports per-section themes

    What makes a design system succeed (the part seniors talk about)#

    • Adoption is the metric: % of UI built from DS components, number of one-off overrides, time to build a new page.

    • Escape hatches with guardrails (className pass-through, slots) so teams don't fork.

    • Contribution model: RFC → design review → a11y review → build with tests and stories → release notes.

    • Versioning: semver, deprecation warnings in dev, codemods for breaking changes.

    • Quality gates in CI: unit + a11y (axe) + visual regression on every component and theme.

    Your story (adapt): "Across the PLUS apps, buttons, modals and form fields were reimplemented per screen with drifting styles and inconsistent focus states. I led building a shared component library: semantic tokens as CSS variables, accessible primitives, Storybook docs with a11y checks, and a migration plan by page. New screens are now assembled from library components, and design reviews focus on UX instead of spacing nitpicks."

    4.2 Component API design patterns#

    Good DS APIs are hard to misuse: accessible by default, composable, typed, and consistent (same prop names everywhere: size, variant, disabled, onChange).

    1. A production-grade Button#

    tsx
    type ButtonProps = React.ComponentPropsWithoutRef<'button'> & {
  variant?: 'primary' | 'secondary' | 'tertiary' | 'destructive';
  size?: 'sm' | 'md' | 'lg';
  loading?: boolean;
  iconStart?: React.ReactNode;
};

export const Button = React.forwardRef<HTMLButtonElement, ButtonProps>(function Button(
  { variant = 'primary', size = 'md', loading = false, disabled, iconStart, children, className, ...rest }, ref) {
  return (
    <button
      ref={ref}
      type={rest.type ?? 'button'}                 // default avoids accidental form submits
      className={cx(styles.btn, styles[variant], styles[size], className)}
      disabled={disabled}
      aria-disabled={loading || undefined}          // stays focusable while loading
      aria-busy={loading || undefined}
      {...rest}                                     // spread first so the loading guard below wins
      onClick={loading ? e => e.preventDefault() : rest.onClick}
    >
      {loading ? <Spinner aria-hidden /> : iconStart}
      <span>{children}</span>
      {loading && <VisuallyHidden>Loading</VisuallyHidden>}
    </button>
  );
});

    Points to say: spreads native props (consumers keep aria-*, data-*), forwards ref (focus management), default type="button", loading keeps focus instead of disabling, className escape hatch.

    2. Compound components (Tabs, Accordion, Select)#

    The parent owns state; children read it from context. Consumers control structure without prop explosion.

    tsx
    const TabsCtx = React.createContext<{ value: string; setValue(v: string): void; baseId: string } | null>(null);
const useTabs = () => { const c = React.useContext(TabsCtx); if (!c) throw new Error('Tabs.* must be inside <Tabs>'); return c; };

export function Tabs({ value, defaultValue, onValueChange, children }: {
  value?: string; defaultValue?: string; onValueChange?(v: string): void; children: React.ReactNode }) {
  const [inner, setInner] = React.useState(defaultValue ?? '');
  const controlled = value !== undefined;                       // controlled OR uncontrolled
  const current = controlled ? value : inner;
  const setValue = (v: string) => { if (!controlled) setInner(v); onValueChange?.(v); };
  const baseId = React.useId();
  return <TabsCtx.Provider value={{ value: current, setValue, baseId }}>{children}</TabsCtx.Provider>;
}
Tabs.List = function List({ children, label }: { children: React.ReactNode; label: string }) {
  return <div role="tablist" aria-label={label} onKeyDown={rovingKeys}>{children}</div>;
};
Tabs.Tab = function Tab({ value, children }: { value: string; children: React.ReactNode }) {
  const t = useTabs(); const selected = t.value === value;
  return <button role="tab" id={`${t.baseId}-t-${value}`} aria-controls={`${t.baseId}-p-${value}`}
    aria-selected={selected} tabIndex={selected ? 0 : -1} onClick={() => t.setValue(value)}>{children}</button>;
};
Tabs.Panel = function Panel({ value, children }: { value: string; children: React.ReactNode }) {
  const t = useTabs();
  return <div role="tabpanel" id={`${t.baseId}-p-${value}`} aria-labelledby={`${t.baseId}-t-${value}`}
    hidden={t.value !== value} tabIndex={0}>{children}</div>;
};

// usage
<Tabs defaultValue="overview">
  <Tabs.List label="Product details"><Tabs.Tab value="overview">Overview</Tabs.Tab><Tabs.Tab value="specs">Tech Specs</Tabs.Tab></Tabs.List>
  <Tabs.Panel value="overview">…</Tabs.Panel><Tabs.Panel value="specs">…</Tabs.Panel>
</Tabs>

    (rovingKeys handles Arrow/Home/End — full code in tab 6.)

    3. Controlled vs uncontrolled — support both#

    The pattern above (value + onValueChange OR defaultValue) is what Radix/React Aria use. Extract it into a useControllableState hook so every component behaves the same.

    4. Polymorphic as / asChild#

    Lets Button render as a link (<Button as="a" href="/buy">) while keeping styles — or asChild (Radix) to merge props onto the child element. Type it so href is only allowed when as="a" (see tab 1.4).

    5. Headless components and hooks#

    Separate behaviour + accessibility from styling: useCombobox() returns props getters (getInputProps, getListboxProps, getOptionProps) and state; teams render any markup. This is how you support Marcom's bespoke visuals and Store's standard UI with one tested a11y implementation.

    6. Slots and render props for flexibility#

    tsx
    <ProductCard
  media={<ProductImage … />}
  title="MacBook Air 13\""
  price={<Price amount={114900} currency="INR" />}
  badge={<Badge>New</Badge>}
  actions={<Button>Buy</Button>}
/>

    Slots keep layout and spacing owned by the DS while content is owned by the team.

    API review checklist (say this when asked "how do you review a new component?")#

    • Native semantics first; ARIA only where needed; keyboard map documented.

    • Controlled + uncontrolled; forwards ref; spreads rest props; className/style escape hatch.

    • Works in RTL, dark mode, 200% zoom, forced-colors, reduced motion.

    • No layout assumptions about its parent; responsive via container queries.

    • SSR-safe (no window at render), RSC-friendly (server-safe parts don't need 'use client').

    • Stories for every state, unit tests for behaviour, axe + visual snapshots.

    4.3 Storybook, governance, Figma-to-code and pixel-perfect craft#

    Storybook as the workshop, the docs, and the test bed#

    • Component Story Format — each story is one state: default, hover/focus (via play functions), disabled, loading, error, long text, RTL, dark.

    • Controls/args for exploring props; autodocs from TypeScript types and JSDoc.

    • Interaction tests (play functions with Testing Library) run in CI via the Storybook test runner/Vitest addon.

    • a11y addon (axe) flags violations per story.

    • Visual regression (Chromatic, or Playwright screenshots of stories) catches 1px regressions across themes and viewports.

    • Decorators for theme, locale, and RTL toolbars so reviewers check all variants quickly.

    tsx
    const meta = { component: Button, args: { children: 'Add to Bag' } } satisfies Meta<typeof Button>;
export default meta;
export const Primary: StoryObj<typeof meta> = {};
export const Loading: StoryObj<typeof meta> = { args: { loading: true } };
export const KeyboardFocus: StoryObj<typeof meta> = {
  play: async ({ canvasElement }) => {
    const btn = within(canvasElement).getByRole('button', { name: /add to bag/i });
    await userEvent.tab();
    await expect(btn).toHaveFocus();
  },
};

    Versioning and release#

    • Monorepo (pnpm/Turborepo or Nx); Changesets for semver bumps and generated changelogs.

    • Breaking changes: deprecate first (dev-only console warning + lint rule), ship a codemod (jscodeshift), remove in the next major.

    • Canary releases for early adopters; consumers pin and upgrade on a cadence.

    • Track adoption with a script that scans product repos for DS imports vs raw HTML elements.

    Governance model#

    ModelHow it worksTrade-off
    CentralizedA core team builds everythingConsistent, but a bottleneck
    FederatedProduct engineers contribute, core team reviewsScales, needs strong review standards
    Hybrid (recommended)Core owns foundations + complex components; teams contribute patterns via RFCBalance of speed and quality

    Figma-to-code workflow#

    1. Designers use Figma Variables bound to the same token names as code; components in the Figma library mirror code components and props (variants ↔ props).

    2. Engineers inspect in Dev Mode: token names, spacing, typography, not raw hex.

    3. Code Connect (or similar) maps Figma components to real code snippets so Dev Mode shows <Button variant="primary">.

    4. Kick-off review before build: states, breakpoints, motion, content edge cases, a11y annotations (focus order, labels, landmarks).

    5. Design QA after build: side-by-side review in Storybook / a preview deployment, at 1×, 2×, dark mode, RTL.

    Pixel-perfect — what it really means#

    Pixel-perfect isn't copying pixel values; it's matching intent across every size and state.

    • Use the spacing scale and typography tokens; if the design uses an off-scale value, ask whether it's intentional.

    • Optical alignment: icons and text baselines, letter-spacing on large headlines, font-feature-settings for tabular numbers in prices.

    • Sub-pixel issues: odd-width elements centered with transforms blur on 1× screens; use whole pixels or translate3d carefully.

    • Check with an overlay (PerfectPixel extension or Figma export at 50% opacity) and in real devices — Retina and non-Retina.

    • Content resilience: long German product names, Hindi and Tamil scripts, price formats (₹1,14,900.00 uses Indian grouping — always format with Intl.NumberFormat('en-IN', { style: 'currency', currency: 'INR' })).

    Your story (adapt): "Designers and engineers on PLUS argued over spacing in every review. I proposed aligning Figma variables with our CSS token names and added Storybook with a visual regression check in CI. Design review moved to the Storybook preview link on each PR, and late-cycle UI fixes dropped noticeably."

    4.4 Interview questions and model answers#

    Q: How would you start a design system from scratch for 20 teams? A: Audit first — screenshot every button, input, modal across products and count variants. Define tokens with design (color, type, spacing). Build the 10 components that cover ~80% of UI (button, link, text field, select, checkbox, radio, dialog, tabs, toast, tooltip) on an accessible headless base. Ship docs + Storybook + a migration guide. Pilot with one willing team, measure adoption, then expand. Treat it as a product with a roadmap and support channel.

    Q: A team wants a variant you don't have. What do you do? A: Ask about the user need. If it's a one-off, use the escape hatch (className/slot). If two or more teams need it, it goes through the contribution process into the DS. I avoid adding boolean props for every request (isRounded, isLarge2) — that's how components rot.

    Q: How do you handle breaking changes? A: Avoid them with additive APIs. When needed: deprecate with a dev warning and a lint rule, provide a codemod, document in the changelog with before/after, support the old API for one major version, and track remaining usages.

    Q: How do you make a design system fast? A: Zero-runtime CSS, ESM with sideEffects: false so unused components are tree-shaken, per-component entry points, no heavy dependencies in primitives (no lodash, no moment), icons as individual SVG components or a sprite, and a bundle-size budget check in CI per component.

    Q: How do you theme for multiple brands or sections (e.g., Store vs Apple TV+ pages)? A: Semantic tokens with per-theme mappings applied via a data attribute or class on a container; CSS variables cascade, so a section can be themed without re-rendering components.

    Q: Web Components or React for a design system? A: React if every consumer is React — best DX, typed props, SSR. Web Components (Lit) if consumers include AEM-rendered pages, other frameworks, or plain HTML; costs: SSR with declarative shadow DOM, form participation (ElementInternals), and styling through shadow boundaries. A hybrid — tokens + CSS shared by both, React wrappers around WCs — is common at large companies.

    Cheat lines#

    • "Tokens are decisions, not values — components only reference semantic tokens."

    • "A design system is a product; adoption is its metric."

    • "Accessible by default: behaviour from a tested headless layer, styling owned by us."

    • "Pixel-perfect means matching intent across every state, size and language."

    • "Deprecate, codemod, then remove — never surprise consumers."

    4.5 Level up: design systems at big-company scale#

    How top companies run theirs#

    CompanySystemWhat's distinctiveLesson to quote
    SpotifyEncoreAfter disbanding its central team, Spotify counted 22 separate design systems; Encore unified them as a "family of systems": shared foundation plus local systems per product areaCentralised vs federated is a spectrum; match it to your org structure
    NetflixHawkinsOne system across TV, mobile and web, with a separate enterprise variant; job posts describe making it legible to AI agents with guardrailsDesign systems are becoming context for AI code generation
    UberBase WebReact implementation of the Base language; an overrides API exposes every inner element for restyling or replacement; visual regression on every commit (source)Give escape hatches by design, so teams don't fork
    AtlassianAtlassian Design SystemTokens enforced by an ESLint plugin, a token codemod, a Babel plugin that inlines token values, migration to build-time CSSGovernance through tooling, not meetings
    Amazon (AWS)CloudscapePowers the AWS console; light/dark themes and comfortable/compact density modes (source)One component set can serve very different information densities

    Staff-level insights#

    • Adoption is measured, not assumed. Mature teams scan every repository's imports and count design-system components vs raw button, input, hard-coded colours and one-off CSS. That number goes on a dashboard and drives the roadmap.

    • Codemods are part of the product. A breaking change without a codemod (jscodeshift or ts-morph) is a tax on every team. Atlassian ships a codemod to migrate raw values to tokens; that's the standard to quote.

    • Token versioning. Tokens get deprecated too. Rename with an alias period, emit lint warnings on deprecated tokens, and remove in a major version. Never silently change a token's meaning (making color.text.subtle darker changes contrast everywhere).

    • Contrast is validated at the token layer. Check foreground/background token pairs for WCAG contrast in CI, for every theme (light, dark, high-contrast). Then components are accessible by construction.

    • Overrides vs slots vs compound components. Overrides (Uber) give maximum flexibility but turn internal structure into public API — refactoring a component's DOM becomes a breaking change. Slots and compound components expose less. Choose based on how many teams with unusual needs you serve.

    • Multiple React copies. In monorepos and micro-frontends, two copies of the design system or React break context and hooks ("Invalid hook call"). Declare React and the design system as peerDependencies, dedupe in the bundler, and share as singletons in Module Federation.

    • Server-rendered theming. Set the theme attribute on the server from a cookie, or with a tiny blocking script, to avoid a flash of the wrong theme. Atlassian publishes SSR theming utilities for exactly this.

    • Design systems for AI agents. Machine-readable component docs (props, allowed tokens, usage rules), lint rules that run on generated code, and Storybook stories that act as examples. Generated UI that bypasses the system is the new "one-off CSS".

    Advanced questions#

    Q: 2,000 files use hard-coded hex colours. Plan the migration to tokens without freezing feature work. Map each hex to the nearest semantic token with design sign-off, write a codemod for the unambiguous 80%, add a lint rule in warn mode then error mode for new code, add a build-time plugin with fallbacks so tokens work before themes ship, migrate by ownership area with a dashboard, and handle the ambiguous 20% manually with the owning teams.

    Q: Overrides API (Uber Base Web) or compound components (Radix style)? Defend one. For an internal system with hundreds of apps and unusual needs, overrides reduce forks but freeze internal structure. For a consumer brand where consistency matters most (Apple), compound components with a small set of slots keep control. I'd pick compound components plus a documented className/style escape hatch, and track override use to find missing features.

    Q: How would you keep AI-generated UI on the design system? Feed the model the component catalogue and token list as context, require generated code to pass the design-system lint rules and type checks, run visual and accessibility checks on generated screens, and reject raw HTML controls and arbitrary values automatically.

    ← Previous03 · React, Next.js & StateNext →05 · Performance
    Topic 0515 min read5 diagrams

    5. Performance Optimization

    5.1 Critical rendering path and Core Web Vitals#

    For apple.com, performance is revenue and brand: launch-day traffic spikes, heavy imagery, and users on every device from a new iPhone to a 5-year-old Android on 4G in a small town. Always frame performance as user-centric metrics measured on real devices at p75.

    Core Web Vitals (the thresholds you must know)#

    MetricMeasuresGood (p75)PoorBiggest causes
    LCP — Largest Contentful PaintLoading: when the main content is visible≤ 2.5 s> 4 sSlow TTFB, render-blocking CSS/JS, late-discovered or unoptimized hero image, client-side rendering
    INP — Interaction to Next PaintResponsiveness: worst-ish delay from input to next frame across the visit≤ 200 ms> 500 msLong tasks, heavy event handlers, hydration, big re-renders
    CLS — Cumulative Layout ShiftVisual stability≤ 0.1> 0.25Images/ads without size, late banners, web-font swap, inserting content above

    Supporting metrics: TTFB (server + network, aim < 800 ms), FCP (first paint of any content, aim < 1.8 s), TBT (lab proxy for INP: sum of long-task time over 50 ms).

    The critical rendering path#

    1. HTML arrives → parser builds the DOM incrementally; the preload scanner finds CSS, JS, images early.

    2. CSS → CSSOM. CSS is render-blocking: no paint until it's parsed.

    3. Classic <script> is parser-blocking; defer runs after parsing in order; async runs whenever loaded; type=module defers by default.

    4. DOM + CSSOM → render tree → layout (geometry) → paint (pixels in layers) → composite (GPU combines layers).

    Optimizing it, in order of impact:

    • Reduce TTFB: CDN edge caching of HTML (static/ISR), streaming SSR, 103 Early Hints.

    • Inline small critical CSS for above-the-fold, load the rest non-blocking; remove unused CSS.

    • defer all scripts; nothing synchronous in <head> except tiny theme/anti-flicker snippets.

    • Make the LCP resource discoverable in HTML (not injected by JS), with fetchpriority="high" and preload if it's a CSS background.

    • preconnect to the image/font CDN origins.

    LCP broken into its four parts (shows real expertise)#

    LCP = TTFB + resource load delay (time until the browser starts fetching the LCP image) + resource load time + element render delay. Attack whichever part is largest. Typical find: a 1.2 s load delay because the hero image URL only exists after React hydrates — fix by server-rendering the <img> with fetchpriority="high".

    INP broken into three parts#

    INP = input delay (main thread busy when the user clicks) + processing time (your handlers + re-render) + presentation delay (layout/paint of the next frame). Fixes: break long tasks and yield, do less in handlers (show feedback first, defer the rest), avoid huge re-renders (transitions, memoization, virtualization), avoid layout thrash, reduce DOM size.

    In the interview, name the part you measured as largest before naming a fix; that ordering is what signals experience.

    5.2 Loading performance#

    JavaScript is the most expensive byte#

    100 KB of JS costs far more than 100 KB of image: it must be downloaded, parsed, compiled, and executed on the main thread — on a mid-range phone that's several times slower than a MacBook. So the strategy is ship less JS, and ship it later.

    Code splitting — where to split#

    1. Route level — each page is its own chunk (automatic in Next.js).

    2. Component level — heavy, below-the-fold or interaction-only UI: AR viewer, 3D model, compare tool, chat widget, date picker, rich-text editor, charts.

    3. Vendor level — rarely-changing libraries in their own long-cached chunk.

    4. Conditional/feature — code only some users need (admin tools, A/B variant B, a locale's polyfill).

    tsx
    const ARQuickLook = dynamic(() => import('./ARQuickLook'), { ssr: false, loading: () => <Skeleton h={400} /> });
const Chat = lazy(() => import('./SupportChat'));

// load on intent: prefetch on hover/focus, render on click
<button onMouseEnter={() => import('./CompareTool')} onFocus={() => import('./CompareTool')} onClick={openCompare}>Compare models</button>

    Tree shaking and bundle hygiene#

    • ESM everywhere; "sideEffects": false in libraries; import named functions (import debounce from 'lodash-es/debounce', not all of lodash).

    • Replace heavy deps: moment → Intl.DateTimeFormat / date-fns; lodash → native; big icon packs → per-icon imports.

    • npx source-map-explorer / @next/bundle-analyzer / rollup-plugin-visualizer to find what's big.

    • Performance budgets in CI (size-limit / bundlewatch): fail the PR if a route's JS grows past the budget.

    • Ship modern JS only (ES2020+ for evergreen browsers) — no unnecessary polyfills.

    Images (usually the LCP element)#

    • Formats: AVIF (best compression) > WebP > JPEG, via <picture> or CDN content negotiation.

    • Responsive srcset + accurate sizes; DPR caps (3× is rarely visible over 2× for photos).

    • Lazy-load below the fold (loading="lazy"), eager + fetchpriority="high" for the LCP image.

    • Image CDN that resizes on the fly (/img?w=800&q=75&fmt=avif).

    • Low-quality placeholders (blur-up or dominant color) to improve perceived speed without CLS.

    • Video: preload="none" or metadata, poster image, lazy start, HLS for adaptive bitrate on hero films.

    Fonts#

    • Self-host, WOFF2, subset per script with unicode-range, preload 1–2 critical files, font-display: swap (or optional for best CLS), metric-adjusted fallbacks.

    Resource hints#

    HintUse
    preconnectEarly DNS+TCP+TLS to a critical third-party origin (image CDN)
    dns-prefetchCheaper fallback for less-critical origins
    preloadFetch a resource needed this page but discovered late (font, CSS background hero)
    prefetch / Speculation RulesFetch or prerender the next likely page (bag → checkout)
    modulepreloadPreload ES module and its dependency graph
    fetchpriorityRaise/lower priority of a specific image or fetch

    Caching strategy (say this crisply)#

    AssetCache-ControlWhy
    Hashed JS/CSS/fonts (app.3f9a1c.js)public, max-age=31536000, immutableContent hash changes on deploy → safe forever
    HTMLno-cache or short s-maxage + stale-while-revalidate at CDNAlways fresh entry point that references new hashes
    API GET (product data)s-maxage=60, stale-while-revalidate=300 at CDN, private if personalizedAbsorb launch-day spikes; never cache personalized data publicly
    ImagesLong max-age, versioned URLsLarge, rarely change

    Layers: browser cache → service worker → CDN edge → origin. Add ETag/Last-Modified for revalidation, Brotli compression, HTTP/2–3 multiplexing.

    Service workers (when they help)#

    Cache the app shell and static assets (Workbox), offline fallback page, background sync for queued actions. Be careful: a buggy SW can pin users to an old version — always ship a kill-switch and skipWaiting strategy.

    Third-party scripts#

    Analytics, tag managers, chat, A/B testing are frequent INP and LCP killers. Load them async after load or on interaction, use a facade (static chat button that loads the widget on click), audit with the Coverage tab, and set a third-party budget. Consider moving them off the main thread (Partytown-style).

    5.3 Runtime performance#

    Long tasks and the main thread#

    Any task over 50 ms is a long task and delays input. Find them in DevTools Performance (red triangles), the Long Animation Frames API (PerformanceObserver with long-animation-frame) in production, and INP attribution from the web-vitals library.

    Layout thrashing#

    Reading layout (offsetHeight, getBoundingClientRect, scrollTop) after writing styles forces a synchronous layout. In a loop, that's one layout per item.

    js
    // BAD: read-write-read-write → N forced layouts
cards.forEach(c => { c.style.height = c.offsetWidth * 0.75 + 'px'; });

// GOOD: batch reads, then writes
const widths = cards.map(c => c.offsetWidth);
requestAnimationFrame(() => cards.forEach((c, i) => { c.style.height = widths[i] * 0.75 + 'px'; }));
// BEST: CSS aspect-ratio: 4 / 3 — no JS at all

    React rendering performance (in priority order)#

    1. Don't render what isn't visible — virtualization, content-visibility: auto, pagination.

    2. Render less on each update — colocate state, narrow store selectors, split contexts, children-as-props.

    3. Make updates non-urgent — useTransition / useDeferredValue for filtering and tab switches.

    4. Skip unchanged subtrees — memo with stable props, or the React Compiler.

    5. Make each render cheaper — move derivations out, memoize expensive computations.

    Virtualization (windowing)#

    Render only rows in the viewport plus an overscan buffer; keep a spacer with the total height so the scrollbar is right.

    tsx
    function VirtualList<T>({ items, rowHeight, height, render }: { items: T[]; rowHeight: number; height: number; render: (t: T, i: number) => React.ReactNode }) {
  const [scrollTop, setScrollTop] = useState(0);
  const overscan = 5;
  const start = Math.max(0, Math.floor(scrollTop / rowHeight) - overscan);
  const end = Math.min(items.length, Math.ceil((scrollTop + height) / rowHeight) + overscan);
  return (
    <div style={{ height, overflowY: 'auto' }} onScroll={e => setScrollTop(e.currentTarget.scrollTop)} role="list">
      <div style={{ height: items.length * rowHeight, position: 'relative' }}>
        {items.slice(start, end).map((item, k) => (
          <div key={start + k} role="listitem" aria-setsize={items.length} aria-posinset={start + k + 1}
               style={{ position: 'absolute', top: (start + k) * rowHeight, height: rowHeight, width: '100%' }}>
            {render(item, start + k)}
          </div>
        ))}
      </div>
    </div>
  );
}

    Production notes: variable row heights need measurement (TanStack Virtual handles it); accessibility suffers because off-screen rows aren't in the DOM — use aria-setsize/posinset, keep focus on a rendered row, and make browser find-in-page a known trade-off.

    Web Workers#

    Move CPU-heavy, DOM-free work off the main thread: fuzzy search over 50k items, CSV/Excel parsing, image processing, diffing large JSON, encryption. Use Comlink to make the worker feel like an async function. Transfer ArrayBuffers instead of copying. OffscreenCanvas for canvas rendering in a worker.

    Memory#

    • Remove listeners/observers/intervals on unmount; use AbortController signals for listeners.

    • Bound caches (LRU), revoke URL.createObjectURL blobs, close WebSockets.

    • Heap snapshot diffing to find detached DOM trees.

    Animation smoothness#

    Target 60 fps (16.7 ms frames) and 120 fps on ProMotion (8.3 ms). Use transform/opacity, requestAnimationFrame, avoid layout in scroll handlers, use passive listeners ({ passive: true }) for touch/wheel, and check the Rendering panel's paint flashing and layer borders.

    5.4 Measuring, and a performance story you can tell#

    Lab vs field#

    Lab (synthetic)Field (RUM)
    ToolsLighthouse, WebPageTest, DevTools Performance, Lighthouse CIweb-vitals library → your analytics, CrUX, Datadog/New Relic RUM
    Good forDebugging, regression gates in CI, reproducibleTruth: real devices, networks, user flows
    WeaknessOne device and network; no real interactions (INP)Noisy; needs segmentation

    Say this: "I set budgets in the lab and judge success in the field at p75, segmented by device class, connection, country and page type."

    js
    import { onLCP, onINP, onCLS } from 'web-vitals/attribution';
function send(metric) {
  navigator.sendBeacon('/rum', JSON.stringify({
    name: metric.name, value: metric.value, rating: metric.rating, page: location.pathname,
    // attribution tells you WHAT caused it: LCP element, INP target + phase breakdown, shifting nodes
    attribution: metric.attribution,
  }));
}
onLCP(send); onINP(send); onCLS(send);

    Debugging workflow (walk the interviewer through this)#

    1. Field data says which page and metric is bad and for whom (e.g. INP poor on Android mid-tier on the product listing page).

    2. Reproduce in DevTools with CPU 4–6× throttling and network throttling.

    3. Record a Performance profile; find long tasks, layout/recalc style, hydration.

    4. Hypothesize, fix one thing, re-measure.

    5. Guard with a CI budget and a RUM alert so it doesn't regress.

    A complete STAR performance story (template — fill with your real numbers)#

    Situation. "PLUS's case list page was the most-used screen. Field data showed poor LCP and INP for many attorneys, many on older Windows laptops behind corporate VPNs."

    Task. "I owned bringing it into the 'good' band without a rewrite, within two sprints."

    Actions.

    1. Measured: added web-vitals with attribution to our telemetry; LCP was the page header + first rows, INP was the filter dropdown and row-expand.

    2. Bundle: bundle analyzer showed a full charting library and a date library in the main chunk. I lazy-loaded the charts (they're in a collapsed panel), replaced the date lib with Intl, and split routes. Main-route JS dropped substantially.

    3. Data: the page waited on three sequential API calls. I parallelized them, moved filters to the URL, and cached with React Query so returning to the list was instant.

    4. Rendering: virtualized the table, memoized row components with stable callbacks, and wrapped filtering in useTransition.

    5. Guardrails: a size-limit budget in CI and a dashboard on p75 INP/LCP per release.

    Result. "p75 LCP and INP moved into the good range [insert your before → after], and support tickets about 'the slow case list' stopped. The budget caught two regressions in later PRs."

    Learning. "Measure in the field first — my first guess (image weight) wasn't the problem; the sequential API waterfall was."

    How you'd apply it at Apple scale#

    • Launch day: HTML and API responses cached at the edge with stale-while-revalidate; queue pages for the bag/checkout; feature flags to shed non-essential widgets under load.

    • Global audience: per-country RUM, image CDN with regional edges, font subsetting for Indic and CJK scripts.

    • Product pages with scroll animations: preload only the first frames of image sequences, use IntersectionObserver to start loading the next section, respect reduced motion.

    5.5 Interview questions and model answers#

    Q: Our product page has an LCP of 4.5 s on mobile. What do you do? A: First find the LCP element and break LCP into TTFB, load delay, load time, render delay using the Performance panel or web-vitals attribution. If TTFB is high: cache HTML at the edge or stream. If load delay: the image is discovered late — put it in server HTML, add fetchpriority="high", don't lazy-load it. If load time: AVIF, right srcset/sizes, image CDN. If render delay: render-blocking CSS/JS or client-side rendering — inline critical CSS, defer scripts, server-render. Then guard with Lighthouse CI and watch p75 in RUM.

    Q: Clicking "Add to Bag" feels slow. How do you improve INP? A: Profile the interaction. Give visual feedback immediately (pressed state, optimistic bag count), then do the work: move non-urgent updates into a transition, defer analytics with requestIdleCallback/scheduler.postTask, shrink the re-render (narrow selectors), and break any long synchronous code. Check for third-party scripts attaching listeners.

    Q: What's the difference between async and defer? A: Both download in parallel without blocking parsing. async executes as soon as it's downloaded (order not guaranteed, may interrupt parsing) — good for independent scripts like analytics. defer executes after parsing, in order, before DOMContentLoaded — good for app code.

    Q: How does code splitting hurt if done badly? A: Too many tiny chunks create a request waterfall (chunk A loads, then discovers B). Fix with route-level splitting, modulepreload, and prefetching likely next chunks. Splitting the LCP component delays LCP.

    Q: SSR made our FCP great but the page is unresponsive for 3 seconds. Why? A: Hydration. The HTML is visible but JS is still downloading and hydrating the whole tree. Reduce client JS (Server Components, islands), split, use selective hydration with Suspense, and defer non-critical hydration.

    Q: How would you make a page with 10,000 rows fast? A: Paginate or virtualize; server-side sort/filter for large data; memoized row components; stable keys; Web Worker for client-side search; content-visibility for long non-tabular content.

    Q: How do you prevent performance regressions in a team of 40? A: Budgets in CI (bundle size, Lighthouse CI thresholds), RUM dashboards with alerts per release, performance review in the PR template for heavy features, and a culture of showing before/after numbers.

    Cheat lines#

    • "I measure in the field at p75, debug in the lab, and guard in CI."

    • "JS is the most expensive byte: parse, compile, execute on the main thread."

    • "LCP has four parts — fix the biggest one."

    • "For INP: feedback first, work later, and never block the main thread over 50 ms."

    • "Hashed assets are immutable for a year; HTML is always fresh."

    5.6 Level up: performance at big-company scale#

    How top companies apply it#

    CompanyWhat they didResult they published
    NetflixKept React on the server, removed it (and Lodash) from the landing page's client bundle; prefetched the sign-up flow's code while users readOver 200 kB less JS, TTI down more than 50%; next-page TTI down about 30% (source)
    Uber (m.uber)50 kB core, Preact, SSR, code splitting, service worker, SVG, no web fonts; measured time to first interaction with the Performance API and skipped the map on slow devicesAbout 3 s to interactive on 2G (source)
    WalmartMoved walmart.com to React + Node SSR (Electrode), cached SSR components, server-rendered only above the foldPages moved from client to server rendering got up to 30% faster (source); each 1 s faster brought up to 2% more conversions (source)
    Amazon—The widely cited "100 ms of latency costs 1% of sales" (source)

    Use the Walmart and Amazon numbers to justify performance work to product managers; use Netflix and Uber to show you know how it's done.

    Staff-level insights#

    • Adaptive loading. Serve different experiences by device capability, not just screen size: navigator.deviceMemory, navigator.hardwareConcurrency and navigator.connection.effectiveType (Chromium-only) or a measured interaction time, as Uber did. Low-end devices get static images instead of video, no live map, fewer animations.

    • The back/forward cache (bfcache) restores pages instantly on Back. Pages are excluded if they use unload handlers, and historically if they send Cache-Control: no-store. Replace unload with pagehide, and check eligibility in DevTools → Application → Back/forward cache. This is one of the cheapest big wins most teams miss.

    • Speculation Rules (<script type="speculationrules">) let Chromium prefetch or fully prerender the likely next page (product → bag), making navigation feel instant. Use moderate eagerness (on hover) to limit waste, and never prerender pages with side effects.

    • 103 Early Hints. The CDN can send preload hints for CSS and fonts while the server is still rendering HTML, which turns server "think time" into download time.

    • INP debugging in production. The Long Animation Frames API (PerformanceObserver with type long-animation-frame) attributes slow frames to specific scripts, including third parties. web-vitals/attribution gives the INP target element and the input-delay / processing / presentation split.

    • Percentiles and segmentation. p75 is the Core Web Vitals bar, but outages hide in p95/p99 and in segments (one country, one Android model, one carrier). Senior engineers always segment before concluding.

    • Third-party governance. Tag managers let marketing add scripts without engineering review. Big companies keep an allowlist, a performance budget for third parties, load them after load or on interaction, and review each tag's cost quarterly.

    • Performance regressions are organisational. A CI budget catches size growth, but most regressions come from data (bigger images uploaded to a CMS, a new A/B test). Add RUM alerts per page type and tie them to release markers.

    • The cost of server rendering moves to the server. SSR shifts CPU cost from users' devices to your fleet; at Black Friday scale you need caching and streaming, not just more servers.

    Advanced questions#

    Q: Users in India on mid-range Android and 4G have p75 LCP of 5.8 s; US desktop is 1.4 s. Plan. Segment RUM by country, device and connection to confirm. Break LCP into its four parts for that segment: TTFB (regional CDN edge and origin distance), load delay (is the hero image discoverable without JS?), load time (image weight; serve smaller AVIF to small, high-DPR screens), render delay (JS blocking). Apply adaptive loading for low-memory devices, and set a per-segment target.

    Q: Product wants a hero video on the home page. What do you insist on? A poster image as the LCP element, preload="none" and start after load or on visibility, muted autoplay with playsinline, a pause control (WCAG 2.2.2), no video for prefers-reduced-motion or saveData users, adaptive bitrate streaming, and an A/B test that measures conversion as well as engagement.

    Q: How do you make our pages eligible for the back/forward cache? Remove unload listeners (use pagehide and visibilitychange), close open connections such as WebSockets on pagehide and reopen on pageshow, avoid Cache-Control: no-store on pages that don't need it, and verify with DevTools and the notRestoredReasons API.

    Q: Convince a PM to spend a sprint on performance. Tie it to money with their numbers and published ones: Walmart's "each second faster, up to 2% more conversions". Show our own RUM curve (conversion by LCP bucket), propose a measurable target, and run it as an experiment so the result is attributable.

    ← Previous04 · Design SystemsNext →06 · Accessibility
    Topic 0615 min read3 diagrams

    6. Accessibility & Web Standards

    6.1 WCAG 2.2, POUR, and the accessibility tree#

    Apple builds VoiceOver, Switch Control, and Voice Control into every device and treats accessibility as a core value. Expect an interviewer to test your component with a keyboard and ask how VoiceOver announces it. Speak about accessibility as a default you build in, not an audit you pass.

    POUR — the four principles#

    PrincipleMeaningExamples of criteria (level AA)
    PerceivableUsers can perceive contentText alternatives (1.1.1); captions (1.2.2); contrast 4.5:1 text, 3:1 large text and UI parts (1.4.3, 1.4.11); reflow at 320 px / 400% zoom (1.4.10); text spacing (1.4.12)
    OperableUsers can operate UIKeyboard for everything (2.1.1); no keyboard trap (2.1.2); skip links (2.4.1); visible focus (2.4.7); focus not obscured by sticky headers (2.4.11, new in 2.2); target size ≥ 24×24 px (2.5.8, new); dragging has a single-pointer alternative (2.5.7, new)
    UnderstandableUsers understand content and behaviourPage language (3.1.1); no surprise context change on focus/input (3.2.1–2); error identification and suggestions (3.3.1, 3.3.3); consistent help (3.2.6, new); redundant entry avoided (3.3.7, new); accessible authentication — no cognitive tests like puzzles, allow paste/password managers (3.3.8, new)
    RobustWorks with assistive techName, role, value exposed for every control (4.1.2); status messages announced without focus (4.1.3)

    Legal context to mention: ADA (US), European Accessibility Act (in force since June 2025), and India's RPwD Act all reference WCAG — relevant for a global store and for PLUS (an American dispute-resolution provider).

    The accessibility tree#

    The browser builds an accessibility tree from the DOM: each node has a role (button, link, checkbox), an accessible name (from content, <label>, aria-labelledby, aria-label, alt, title — in that rough priority), a state/value (checked, expanded, selected, disabled, invalid), and a description (aria-describedby). Screen readers read this tree, not your CSS. Inspect it in Chrome DevTools → Accessibility pane, or Safari's Accessibility inspector.

    The first rule of ARIA#

    If a native element exists, use it. ARIA changes semantics only — role="button" on a div gives no focus, no Enter/Space, no disabled behaviour; you must add all of that by hand. Bad ARIA is worse than none (WebAIM's annual survey of the top million homepages consistently finds pages with ARIA have more detected errors).

    Names, descriptions, and hidden content#

    html
    <!-- icon-only button needs a name -->
<button aria-label="Remove AirPods Pro from Bag"><svg aria-hidden="true" focusable="false">…</svg></button>

<!-- visually hidden but read by screen readers -->
<span class="visually-hidden">(opens in a new tab)</span>
<style>.visually-hidden{position:absolute;width:1px;height:1px;margin:-1px;overflow:hidden;clip:rect(0 0 0 0);white-space:nowrap;border:0}</style>

<!-- hide decorative content from AT -->
<img src="swirl.svg" alt="" />

<!-- inert: hide AND disable everything behind a modal -->
<main inert>…</main>

    • display:none / hidden / visibility:hidden → hidden from everyone.

    • aria-hidden="true" → hidden from AT only (never on focusable elements).

    • .visually-hidden → hidden visually only.

    • Repeated "Buy" buttons need unique names: "Buy iPhone 17", "Buy iPhone Air" (visually hidden suffix or aria-label).

    Color, contrast, and motion#

    • Don't use color alone (in-stock green / out-of-stock red needs text or an icon).

    • Check contrast of text on images and of focus indicators (3:1 against adjacent colors).

    • prefers-reduced-motion; no flashing more than 3 times per second; auto-playing carousels need pause controls (2.2.2).

    • Support forced-colors: active (Windows High Contrast): don't rely on background images for meaning; use currentColor in SVG icons.

    6.2 ARIA patterns and focus management#

    All patterns follow the WAI-ARIA Authoring Practices Guide (APG). Know the keyboard map for each — interviewers will press keys.

    WidgetRoles / attributesKeyboard
    Modal dialog<dialog> or role=dialog + aria-modal=true + aria-labelledbyTab cycles inside; Esc closes; focus returns to trigger
    Tabstablist, tab (aria-selected, aria-controls), tabpanel←/→ move, Home/End, roving tabindex; Tab goes to panel
    Accordion<button aria-expanded aria-controls> inside a headingEnter/Space toggle
    Menu buttonaria-haspopup=menu, aria-expanded; menu/menuitem↓ opens, arrows move, Esc closes and returns focus
    Combobox (autocomplete)input role=combobox, aria-expanded, aria-controls, aria-activedescendant; listbox/option↓/↑ move, Enter selects, Esc closes
    Disclosure (nav dropdown)button[aria-expanded]Enter/Space
    Carouselregion aria-roledescription=carousel, slide group with "3 of 5"Prev/next buttons, pause control
    Toast / statusrole=status (polite) or role=alert (assertive)No focus change
    Switch<button role=switch aria-checked>Space toggles

    Modal: complete focus management#

    The native way (recommended): <dialog> + showModal() gives top layer, Esc, background inert, and focus moved inside. You still restore focus and label it.

    tsx
    export function Modal({ open, onClose, title, children }: { open: boolean; onClose(): void; title: string; children: React.ReactNode }) {
  const ref = useRef<HTMLDialogElement>(null);
  const returnTo = useRef<HTMLElement | null>(null);
  const titleId = useId();

  useEffect(() => {
    const d = ref.current!;
    if (open && !d.open) {
      returnTo.current = document.activeElement as HTMLElement;   // remember trigger
      d.showModal();                                               // top layer + inert background
      d.querySelector<HTMLElement>('[autofocus]')?.focus();
    } else if (!open && d.open) {
      d.close();
    }
  }, [open]);

  return (
    <dialog ref={ref} aria-labelledby={titleId}
      onClose={() => { onClose(); returnTo.current?.focus(); }}     // Esc + close() both land here
      onClick={e => { if (e.target === ref.current) ref.current!.close(); }}  // backdrop click
    >
      <h2 id={titleId}>{title}</h2>
      {children}
      <button onClick={() => ref.current!.close()}>Close</button>
    </dialog>
  );
}

    If you must build it by hand (older browser support or custom portal): set aria-modal, put inert on siblings of the portal, trap Tab/Shift+Tab between first and last focusable elements, lock body scroll, close on Esc, restore focus.

    tsx
    // Reusable focus-trap hook for a custom (non-<dialog>) modal rendered in a portal
export function useFocusTrap<T extends HTMLElement>(active: boolean) {
  const ref = useRef<T>(null);
  useEffect(() => {
    const container = ref.current;
    if (!active || !container) return;
    const previous = document.activeElement as HTMLElement | null;      // remember the trigger
    const selector = 'a[href],button:not([disabled]),input:not([disabled]),select:not([disabled]),textarea:not([disabled]),[tabindex]:not([tabindex="-1"])';
    const focusables = () => Array.from(container.querySelectorAll<HTMLElement>(selector));
    focusables()[0]?.focus();

    const onKeyDown = (e: KeyboardEvent) => {
      if (e.key !== 'Tab') return;
      const f = focusables(); if (f.length === 0) return;
      const first = f[0], last = f[f.length - 1];
      if (e.shiftKey && document.activeElement === first) { e.preventDefault(); last.focus(); }
      else if (!e.shiftKey && document.activeElement === last) { e.preventDefault(); first.focus(); }
    };
    container.addEventListener('keydown', onKeyDown);
    return () => { container.removeEventListener('keydown', onKeyDown); previous?.focus(); }; // restore focus on close
  }, [active]);
  return ref;
}

// usage
function CustomModal({ open, onClose, title, children }: { open: boolean; onClose(): void; title: string; children: React.ReactNode }) {
  const ref = useFocusTrap<HTMLDivElement>(open);
  const titleId = useId();
  if (!open) return null;
  return createPortal(
    <div className="backdrop" onClick={onClose}>
      <div ref={ref} role="dialog" aria-modal="true" aria-labelledby={titleId}
           onClick={e => e.stopPropagation()} onKeyDown={e => e.key === 'Escape' && onClose()}>
        <h2 id={titleId}>{title}</h2>
        {children}
      </div>
    </div>,
    document.body,
  );
}

    Roving tabindex (tabs, toolbars, radio-like groups)#

    Only one item in the group has tabIndex=0; the rest are -1. Arrow keys move focus and update which one is 0. Tab leaves the group in one press — which is what screen reader and keyboard users expect.

    ts
    function rovingKeys(e: React.KeyboardEvent<HTMLElement>) {
  const tabs = Array.from(e.currentTarget.querySelectorAll<HTMLElement>('[role="tab"]:not([disabled])'));
  const i = tabs.indexOf(document.activeElement as HTMLElement);
  const isRtl = getComputedStyle(e.currentTarget).direction === 'rtl';
  const next = { ArrowRight: isRtl ? i - 1 : i + 1, ArrowLeft: isRtl ? i + 1 : i - 1, Home: 0, End: tabs.length - 1 }[e.key];
  if (next === undefined) return;
  e.preventDefault();
  const target = tabs.at((next + tabs.length) % tabs.length)!;
  target.focus();
  target.click();          // automatic activation; omit for manual activation (Enter/Space)
}

    Combobox with aria-activedescendant#

    Focus stays in the input; the highlighted option is referenced by ID — so typing never loses focus.

    html
    <label for="q">Search Apple Store</label>
<input id="q" role="combobox" aria-autocomplete="list" aria-expanded="true"
       aria-controls="q-list" aria-activedescendant="q-opt-2" />
<ul id="q-list" role="listbox">
  <li id="q-opt-1" role="option" aria-selected="false">iPhone 17</li>
  <li id="q-opt-2" role="option" aria-selected="true">iPhone 17 Pro</li>
</ul>
<div role="status" class="visually-hidden">5 results available</div>

    Live regions — announcing changes without moving focus#

    tsx
    // Render the region ONCE on page load (empty), then change its text — AT ignores regions inserted with content already in them
<div role="status" aria-live="polite" className="visually-hidden">{bagMessage}</div>
// setBagMessage('AirPods Pro added to Bag. Bag has 2 items.')

    Use polite for confirmations and result counts, assertive/role=alert only for urgent errors. Don't announce every keystroke — debounce result-count announcements.

    Focus management in SPAs#

    • On route change: move focus to the new page's <h1> (with tabIndex=-1) or a wrapper, and update document.title — otherwise screen reader users don't know the page changed.

    • After deleting an item: move focus to the next item or the list heading, never to body.

    • After form submit errors: focus the error summary.

    • Visible focus always: :focus-visible { outline: 2px solid var(--focus); outline-offset: 2px; } — never outline: none without a replacement.

    • Ensure sticky headers don't hide the focused element (scroll-padding-top).

    6.3 Testing accessibility and building it into the process#

    Automated tools catch only part of the problem: Deque reports roughly half of real-world issues by volume, and only a minority of WCAG criteria can be fully verified by a machine. The rest needs keyboard and screen reader testing. A senior answer covers all three layers plus process.

    Layer 1 — automated, every PR#

    • eslint-plugin-jsx-a11y in the editor.

    • jest-axe / vitest-axe on component tests; @axe-core/playwright on key pages; Storybook a11y addon.

    • Lighthouse CI accessibility score as a gate (necessary, not sufficient).

    • Testing Library queries by role and name (getByRole('button', { name: /add to bag/i })) — if the test can't find it by role, a screen reader can't either.

    ts
    import { axe } from 'jest-axe';
test('modal has no detectable a11y violations', async () => {
  const { container } = render(<Modal open title="Confirm" onClose={() => {}}>Body</Modal>);
  expect(await axe(container)).toHaveNoViolations();
});

// Playwright: full keyboard flow
test('checkout is keyboard operable', async ({ page }) => {
  await page.goto('/bag');
  await page.keyboard.press('Tab');
  await expect(page.getByRole('link', { name: 'Skip to main content' })).toBeFocused();
  // …Tab to Check Out, press Enter, assert focus lands on the checkout h1
});

    Layer 2 — manual keyboard pass (5 minutes per feature)#

    Unplug the mouse: Tab order follows visual order, every control reachable and operable, focus always visible, no traps, Esc closes overlays, focus restored after close.

    Layer 3 — screen readers#

    PlatformScreen readerBrowser
    macOS / iOSVoiceOver (Cmd+F5; rotor with VO+U)Safari
    WindowsNVDA (free), JAWSChrome, Firefox, Edge
    AndroidTalkBackChrome

    Plus 200%/400% zoom and reflow, Windows High Contrast, reduced motion, and voice control ("Click Add to Bag" must match the visible label — WCAG 2.5.3 Label in Name).

    Process — how you make it stick at scale#

    • Shift left: design annotations for headings, landmarks, focus order, and alt text in Figma before build.

    • Design system components accessible by default, so product teams inherit it.

    • Definition of done includes keyboard + VoiceOver check and axe clean.

    • Accessibility champions per team; periodic expert audits; testing sessions with users with disabilities.

    • Track a11y bugs by severity with SLAs (blockers fixed before release).

    Accessibility story (template — fill with your real details)#

    Situation. "PLUS serves attorneys and parties in disputes, and the client required WCAG 2.1 AA for ADA compliance. An external audit returned a long list of issues, with the case-filing wizard and document viewer worst."

    Task. "I led the frontend remediation for my modules and made sure new work didn't regress."

    Actions. "I grouped findings by root cause instead of fixing tickets one by one: missing labels and error associations (fixed once in our form field component), custom dropdowns without keyboard support (replaced with an accessible combobox), modals that didn't trap or return focus (one shared dialog), and route changes that weren't announced (focus to h1 + title update). I added jest-axe to component tests and an axe check on key flows in Playwright, and ran VoiceOver and NVDA passes before each release."

    Result. "The re-audit closed the critical and serious items for those modules [use your numbers], and because the fixes lived in shared components, other teams got them for free."

    Learning. "Fixing root causes in components beats fixing screens. And automated checks are a floor — the VoiceOver pass found the issues that mattered most to real users."

    6.4 Interview questions and model answers#

    Q: Make this custom dropdown accessible. A: First ask: is it a select (pick a value) or a menu (actions)? For a value, a native <select> is the best answer if the design allows. Otherwise the APG listbox/combobox pattern: a button with aria-haspopup="listbox" and aria-expanded, a listbox with options and aria-selected, arrow keys + type-ahead, Esc to close and return focus, Enter to select, and the selected value in the button's accessible name. Then test with VoiceOver.

    Q: How does a screen reader user know an item was added to the bag? A: A polite live region that exists from page load announces "AirPods Pro added. Bag has 2 items." Focus stays where it was, or moves to a confirmation dialog if one opens.

    Q: What's wrong with <div onClick>? A: Not focusable, no Enter/Space activation, no role, no disabled semantics, not announced as interactive, and voice control can't target it. Use <button>.

    Q: aria-label vs aria-labelledby vs aria-describedby? A: aria-labelledby points to visible text to form the name (preferred — stays in sync and gets translated). aria-label sets a name string when there's no visible text (icon buttons). aria-describedby adds supplementary info read after the name (hints, error messages).

    Q: How do you make an image carousel accessible? A: Pause/play control, no auto-advance or pause on hover/focus, prev/next buttons with names, slide position announced ("Slide 3 of 5"), inactive slides hidden from AT (inert or aria-hidden plus no focusable content), respect reduced motion, swipe has button alternatives.

    Q: How do you handle accessibility for data visualizations? A: Provide a text summary of the insight, an accessible data table alternative, don't rely on color alone (patterns/labels), keyboard-focusable data points with names, and role="img" with a label on the SVG for simple charts.

    Q: How do you get a team to care about accessibility? A: Make it easy (accessible components by default), make it visible (axe in CI, a11y in the PR checklist), make it human (watch a screen reader user try the product), and make it part of done, not a separate phase.

    Cheat lines#

    • "Native HTML first; ARIA only fills gaps — bad ARIA is worse than none."

    • "Every control has a name, role, and state; I check it in the accessibility tree."

    • "Focus is the cursor for keyboard and screen reader users — I always know where it goes next."

    • "Automated checks are a floor; VoiceOver and keyboard passes are the real test."

    • "I fix accessibility in shared components so every team inherits the fix."

    6.5 Level up: accessibility at big-company scale#

    Why big companies treat it as non-negotiable: the cases everyone cites#

    CaseWhat happenedWhy it matters in your answer
    NFB v. Target (2006–2008)Blind users couldn't use Target.com (missing alt text, mouse-only controls). The court held the ADA can reach a retailer's website; Target settled with a $6 million fund, NFB certification and ongoing monitoring of the siteThe precedent behind "e-commerce sites must be accessible" (summary)
    NAD v. Netflix (2011–2012)Deaf users sued over missing captions. The court held the ADA applies even to a web-only business; Netflix agreed to caption 100% of content within two years and make captioned titles easy to find (source)Accessibility covers media and product features, not just HTML
    Robles v. Domino's (2019)An appeals court held Domino's website and app had to be usable with a screen reader; the US Supreme Court declined to hear Domino's appealApps and sites tied to physical stores are in scope (relevant to Apple Store pickup, Walmart, Target)
    European Accessibility Act (in force June 2025)E-commerce, banking and other services sold in the EU must meet accessibility requirements, aligned with EN 301 549 / WCAGGlobal companies now have regulatory, not just legal, exposure

    How top companies build it in#

    • Netflix: beyond captions (from the 2012 settlement), the product includes audio description tracks, subtitle styling, and TV interfaces navigated entirely by remote — so focus visibility and predictable spatial navigation are core requirements, not polish.

    • Uber (Base Web): the design system states keyboard navigation works reliably with screen readers, and drag-and-drop lists were built with explicit ARIA support because browsers give little help there (source).

    • Atlassian: enforces accessible icon labels with lint rules, and standardised drag-and-drop on one library so accessibility is solved once (source). The accessibility answer for dragging cards is an alternative path — a "Move to…" menu — rather than trying to make the drag gesture itself usable for everyone.

    • Amazon (Cloudscape): lists accessibility as a core feature next to responsiveness and browser coverage, so every AWS console team inherits it (source).

    • Apple: VoiceOver, Voice Control and Switch Control ship on every device; web teams test with them as a matter of course.

    Staff-level insights#

    • Enterprise sales need paperwork. Companies selling to governments and large enterprises (Atlassian, AWS, banks' vendors) publish an Accessibility Conformance Report (a VPAT) per product. Engineers are asked to fill in conformance per WCAG criterion — knowing that exists signals seniority.

    • Screen reader + browser pairs behave differently. VoiceOver is best with Safari, NVDA and JAWS with Chrome and Firefox. A pattern that works in one pair can be silent in another; test the pairs your users actually use.

    • Live region quirks. A region added to the DOM with text already in it is often ignored; it must exist first, then change. VoiceOver can drop rapid successive updates, so debounce and combine messages ("3 items added"). Don't announce on every keystroke.

    • Virtualized grids. Off-screen rows don't exist, so screen readers can't count them. Use role="grid" with aria-rowcount on the grid and aria-rowindex on each rendered row, keep focus on a rendered cell, and handle Page Up/Down yourself.

    • Accessible video players need captions (closed and SDH), audio description, keyboard shortcuts that don't fight screen readers, focus kept visible in fullscreen, labelled controls that change name ("Play"/"Pause"), and a seek slider with aria-valuetext in words.

    • 10-foot (TV) interfaces. Spatial navigation with remembered focus per row, very large and high-contrast focus indicators, and text-to-speech of the focused item on platforms that support it.

    • Cognitive accessibility is the next frontier: plain language, no time limits without extension, consistent placement of help (WCAG 2.2's 3.2.6), and avoiding re-entry of information (3.3.7).

    • Shift-left economics. Fixing an accessibility bug in design costs minutes; after launch it can mean a lawsuit. Mature companies put a11y annotations in Figma, accessible primitives in the design system, linting in the editor, and expert audits before major launches.

    Advanced questions#

    Q: Make a Jira-style drag-and-drop board accessible. Keyboard pickup and drop (Space to lift, arrows to move, Space to drop, Esc to cancel) with live-region announcements ("Card 'Fix login' moved to In Progress, position 2 of 5"), plus a non-drag alternative: a "Move to…" menu on each card. Keep focus on the moved card after it lands. Test with VoiceOver and NVDA.

    Q: Our product grid is virtualized with 50,000 items. A screen reader user says it's unusable. Switch to role="grid" with aria-rowcount/aria-rowindex, give each card a concise accessible name, implement arrow-key navigation that scrolls and focuses, add filters and search so users don't need to browse 50k items, and offer a non-virtualized paginated view.

    Q: How would you run accessibility across 5,000 engineers? Accessible-by-default design-system components, lint rules that block common errors, axe checks in CI as a gate, a11y champions per team, annotated designs, expert audits per launch, bug severity SLAs, and a conformance report per product kept up to date. Measure open a11y bugs by severity and time-to-fix.

    Q: Design the accessibility of a video player for a streaming service. Use the list above: captions and audio description tracks selectable by keyboard, remembered preferences, focus management in fullscreen, labelled controls, announcements for state changes, reduced motion for previews, and testing on TV platforms with their screen readers.

    ← Previous05 · PerformanceNext →07 · APIs, Data & Real-time
    Topic 0714 min read4 diagrams

    7. APIs, Data Handling & Real-time

    7.1 REST vs GraphQL, API contracts, and the BFF#

    REST — what "good" looks like from the frontend#

    • Resource URLs, correct verbs (GET safe and cacheable; PUT/DELETE idempotent; POST not), meaningful status codes (400 validation, 401 unauthenticated, 403 forbidden, 404, 409 conflict, 422, 429 rate-limited, 5xx).

    • Pagination: cursor-based (?after=abc&limit=50) for feeds and large lists — stable under inserts; offset only for small, static lists.

    • Error shape consistent across services (RFC 9457 Problem Details: type, title, status, detail, field errors).

    • HTTP caching works naturally: ETag, Cache-Control, CDN.

    • Versioning via URL or header; additive changes only.

    GraphQL — strengths and costs#

    RESTGraphQL
    Over/under-fetchingCommon; multiple round tripsClient asks for exactly the fields needed, one request
    CachingHTTP/CDN caching is easyNeeds a normalized client cache (Apollo, urql, Relay); CDN caching via persisted GET queries
    TypingOpenAPI → generated typesSchema is the contract; codegen for TS types and hooks
    EvolutionVersioningDeprecate fields, add new ones; no versions
    RisksChatty clientsExpensive nested queries (need depth/complexity limits), N+1 on server (DataLoader), harder error semantics (200 with errors)
    Best forPublic APIs, simple resources, file uploadsMany clients with different data needs; aggregating many backend services
    graphql
    query ProductPage($slug: String!, $locale: Locale!) {
  product(slug: $slug, locale: $locale) {
    id name
    price { amount currency formatted }
    variants(first: 20) { edges { node { id color storage inStock } } }
    ...ProductGallery_product            # colocated fragment owned by the gallery component
  }
}

    Fragment colocation (Relay-style): each component declares the fields it needs; the page query composes fragments. Removing a component removes its fields — no over-fetching drift.

    Backend-for-Frontend (BFF)#

    A thin server layer owned by the frontend team (Node/Next route handlers) that sits between the browser and many microservices.

    Why it's the right answer for Apple Store–like systems:

    • Aggregation: one call returns product + price + availability + trade-in instead of 5 browser round trips over mobile networks.

    • Shaping: returns exactly the view model the UI needs.

    • Security: tokens and API keys stay server-side; session cookie to the browser (see tab 8).

    • Resilience: timeouts, fallbacks, partial responses when a non-critical service fails.

    • Caching per field-freshness: product copy cached long, price short, availability very short.

    Contracts between frontend and backend#

    • OpenAPI or GraphQL schema as the source of truth; generate TS types and clients.

    • Validate responses at runtime at the boundary (Zod) in critical paths.

    • Mock Service Worker (MSW) to build UI before the API exists and to run deterministic tests.

    • Contract tests (Pact) or schema checks in CI to catch breaking backend changes.

    Your story (adapt): "The PLUS case detail page made five sequential REST calls from the browser. I proposed a BFF endpoint that aggregates them server-side in parallel and returns a view model; the frontend went from five round trips to one, error handling was centralized, and we stopped leaking internal service fields to the client."

    7.2 Async data flows: cancellation, races, retries, optimistic updates#

    The race condition every interviewer asks about#

    User types "ip" then "iph". The "ip" request returns after "iph" → results for the wrong query are shown. Three fixes, best first:

    1. Cancel the old request with AbortController when a new one starts.

    2. Ignore stale responses — track a request id and only apply the latest.

    3. Use a library that keys by query (React Query) so each query's data is stored under its own key.

    ts
    function createLatestFetcher<T>() {
  let ctrl: AbortController | null = null;
  let seq = 0;
  return async (url: string): Promise<T | undefined> => {
    ctrl?.abort();                       // cancel the in-flight request
    ctrl = new AbortController();
    const id = ++seq;
    try {
      const res = await fetch(url, { signal: ctrl.signal });
      if (!res.ok) throw new HttpError(res.status, await res.text());
      const data = (await res.json()) as T;
      return id === seq ? data : undefined; // belt and braces: drop stale
    } catch (e) {
      if ((e as DOMException).name === 'AbortError') return undefined;
      throw e;
    }
  };
}

    Timeouts#

    ts
    const res = await fetch(url, { signal: AbortSignal.any([userSignal, AbortSignal.timeout(8000)]) });

    A resilient fetch layer (what you'd build once for the whole app)#

    • Base URL, auth (cookies via BFF), JSON parsing, typed errors (HttpError with status and body).

    • Timeout per call class; retry only idempotent requests on network errors, 5xx, 429 (respect Retry-After) with exponential backoff + jitter.

    • Idempotency keys for POSTs that matter (place order, pay, submit filing) so a retry or double-click doesn't create two orders.

    • Correlation/request IDs in headers for tracing across services.

    • Central handling of 401 (session expired → refresh or redirect to sign-in, preserving the return URL).

    • Response validation in critical paths.

    Optimistic updates (with rollback)#

    Use for low-risk, high-frequency, likely-to-succeed actions: favorite, add to bag, mark as read, reorder. Don't use for payments, placing orders, or legal submissions — show a clear pending state and confirm from the server.

    Steps: snapshot → apply locally → send → on error roll back + toast with retry → on settle refetch to reconcile. (React Query example in tab 3.3; React 19's useOptimistic for form actions.)

    Double-submit and duplicate actions#

    • Disable or mark the button aria-disabled + aria-busy while pending (keep focus).

    • Idempotency key generated when the form mounts, reused on retry.

    • Server dedupes by key; the client treats a 409 "already processed" as success.

    Request waterfalls#

    Sequential awaits that don't depend on each other are the most common avoidable latency.

    ts
    // waterfall: ~ t1 + t2 + t3
const user = await getUser(); const cases = await getCases(); const notices = await getNotices();
// parallel: ~ max(t1, t2, t3)
const [user, cases, notices] = await Promise.all([getUser(), getCases(), getNotices()]);

    Also watch for component waterfalls: parent fetches, renders child, child fetches. Fix by hoisting fetches to the route level, prefetching, or server components fetching in parallel.

    Offline and flaky networks#

    • navigator.onLine + online/offline events for a banner (not fully reliable — treat failed fetches as the truth).

    • Queue mutations while offline (IndexedDB + Background Sync) for draft-type data — e.g. a case note draft — never for payments.

    • Persist drafts locally so a lost connection doesn't lose a long form.

    7.3 Client caching and large datasets#

    Caching layers on the client#

    LayerLifetimeUse
    Component stateUntil unmountEphemeral UI
    Query cache (React Query/Apollo)Session, with stale/gc timesServer data, dedupe, background refresh
    HTTP cache / service workerAcross sessionsStatic assets, cacheable GETs
    localStoragePersistent, sync, ~5 MB, stringsSmall preferences (theme, recently viewed IDs) — never tokens or PII
    IndexedDBPersistent, async, largeOffline data, drafts, large datasets
    sessionStorageTab lifetimeMulti-step form progress in one tab

    Invalidation strategies: time-based (staleTime), event-based (invalidate after a mutation), push-based (WebSocket message invalidates a key), and version-based (cache key includes a schema/app version).

    Normalized cache (Apollo/Relay/Redux entity adapter): store each entity once by ID, so updating the price of product 42 updates every list showing it.

    Handling large datasets in the browser#

    Decision tree:
