    • content-visibility: auto on rows as a cheaper first step.

    • Fixed card sizes per breakpoint → zero layout shift and simple virtualization math.

    Images

    • srcset per card size; lazy-load off-screen; prioritize the first row; low-quality placeholders or dominant-color backgrounds; decode off the main thread (decoding="async").

    • Preconnect to the image CDN.

    Hover/focus preview

    • Start loading the trailer only after a 300–500 ms dwell (intent), cancel if the user moves on; only one preview player instance reused and moved between cards; respect prefers-reduced-motion and data-saver (no autoplay).

    • The enlarged card uses transform: scale() (compositor only), rendered in a portal so it isn't clipped by the row's overflow.

    Navigation and focus (TV and keyboard)

    • Spatial navigation: arrow keys move focus within a row and between rows; remember the last focused index per row.

    • Each row is a labelled region (aria-labelledby the row title) containing a list; cards are links with full names ("The Crown, Season 6").

    • Roving tabindex per row so Tab moves between rows, arrows move within.

    Low-end devices

    • Small JS budget, avoid heavy animation libraries, throttle preview, reduce concurrency of image loads; measure on real TV hardware.

    Resilience: if a personalized row fails, drop it silently or show a generic row; never blank the page. Cache the last successful browse response to show instantly on next open, then refresh (stale-while-revalidate).

    Observability: impressions (which cards were actually visible — via IntersectionObserver), time to first row, preview start latency, playback start rate.

    11.4 Design 3 — Autocomplete / search typeahead#

    Prompt: "Design the search box on apple.com: as you type, show suggestions (products, accessories, support articles, quick links)." Asked everywhere because it touches performance, networking, caching, and accessibility in one small component.

    R — Requirements#

    • Suggestions after 1–2 characters, grouped (Products, Support, Quick Links); recent searches when empty; keyboard and mouse selection; Enter goes to full results.

    • p95 suggestion latency feels instant (< 100–150 ms after typing pause); works on mobile; localized; WCAG AA.

    A — Architecture#

    Input component → debounced query → client cache lookup → request to suggestions API (edge-cached for popular prefixes) → render grouped listbox. Optional: a small static prefix index shipped for the top few thousand queries for zero-latency suggestions offline.

    Key decisions and the code you'd write#

    tsx
    function useSuggestions(query: string) {
  const debounced = useDebouncedValue(query.trim().toLowerCase(), 150);
  return useQuery({
    queryKey: ['suggest', debounced, locale],
    queryFn: ({ signal }) => api.get(`/suggest?q=${encodeURIComponent(debounced)}&locale=${locale}`, { signal }), // auto-abort on change
    enabled: debounced.length >= 2,
    staleTime: 5 * 60_000,              // same prefix again → instant from cache
    placeholderData: keepPreviousData,  // keep old list while loading: no flicker
  });
}
    ConcernDecision
    Request volumeDebounce 120–200 ms; min length 2; cancel stale requests; cache by normalized prefix (LRU, e.g. 100 entries)
    Race conditionsAbort previous request; keyed cache prevents wrong results
    LatencyCDN caches popular prefixes (s-maxage); prefetch on focus (recent/popular); HTTP/2 connection already warm
    RankingServer-side; client may re-rank recent items to the top
    HighlightingBold the matched part via string splitting — never innerHTML with user input
    Empty / error"No results for 'xyz'" with suggestions; on error, keep the input usable and allow Enter to full search
    MobileFull-screen overlay, inputmode="search", enterkeyhint="search", large touch targets
    AnalyticsLog query, selected position, zero-result queries (privacy-reviewed, no PII)

    Accessibility (the part they'll probe)#

    • input role="combobox" with aria-expanded, aria-controls, aria-autocomplete="list", aria-activedescendant pointing to the highlighted option; focus never leaves the input.

    • Results container role="listbox" with groups labelled by their heading, options with aria-selected.

    • ↓/↑ move, Enter selects, Esc clears/closes, Tab leaves; mouse hover updates the active option.

    • A polite status region: "8 suggestions available" (debounced), "No suggestions".

    • Visible label (or aria-label="Search apple.com") and a clear button with a name.

    Scale-up discussion#

    For millions of users: edge-computed suggestions near the user, a tiered cache (browser → CDN → search service), and graceful degradation (show recent searches if the API is slow). Measure with time-to-first-suggestion and suggestion click-through rate.

    11.5 Design 4 — Banking dashboard with high security#

    Prompt: "Design the web dashboard for a bank: accounts overview, transactions with search and filters, transfer money, statements download." (The same structure fits PLUS: case overview, filings, payments, documents.)

    R — Requirements#

    • Accounts and balances; transactions list (years of history, search, filter by date/amount/category); transfers to saved payees; add payee; download statements (PDF/CSV).

    • Security first: regulatory requirements, session timeouts, step-up auth, audit trail, no data leakage on shared devices.

    • Accessibility AA (regulated); works on older desktops; data correctness over speed.

    A — Architecture#

    • Rendering: authenticated app — SSR shell or CSR SPA behind login; no SEO need. Prefer SSR/RSC for first data to avoid loading spinners, with Cache-Control: no-store, private everywhere.

    • BFF with server-side sessions (HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Strict cookie); tokens never in the browser; CSRF tokens on all mutations.

    • Strict CSP with nonces, Trusted Types, no third-party scripts on authenticated pages (analytics first-party and scrubbed).

    • Separate micro-app or route boundary for high-risk flows (transfers) with its own stricter policies.

    D — Data model and state#

    • Server state per entity: accounts, transactions (cursor paginated by date+id), payees.

    • Filters in the URL — but never PII or account numbers; use opaque IDs.

    • Money as integer minor units + currency code; format only at render with Intl.

    • No persistence to localStorage; on sign-out, clear the query cache and broadcast logout to other tabs (BroadcastChannel).

    I — Interfaces#

    • GET /accounts, GET /accounts/:id/transactions?cursor&from&to&q&category.

    • POST /transfers { fromAccount, payeeId, amountMinor, currency, reference, idempotencyKey } → 202 { status: 'pending_auth', challengeId } → step-up → POST /transfers/:id/confirm.

    • POST /statements → async job → GET /statements/:jobId (poll or SSE) → signed, short-lived download URL.

    O — Optimizations#

    Transfer flow (show the UX and security together)

    1. Form with strict validation (amount > 0, ≤ balance and limits, payee exists) — but the server is the authority.

    2. Review screen: payee name, masked account, amount in words and numbers, fees, date — the user must see exactly what they sign.

    3. Step-up auth: passkey/OTP in a modal with focus management; OTP input with autocomplete="one-time-code".

    4. Submit with idempotency key; button pending state; no optimistic UI for money.

    5. Result screen with reference number; transaction appears as "Pending" until settled (real-time event or polling).

    Transactions list: server-side search and filters; cursor pagination; virtualized table with proper <table> semantics (or role=grid only if cells are interactive); sticky header; export done server-side.

    Session security: idle timeout warning dialog at e.g. 4 of 5 minutes with an accessible "Stay signed in" button (WCAG 2.2.1), absolute lifetime, re-auth for sensitive views, mask balances optionally ("Hide balances" toggle for shoulder-surfing).

    Data leakage: no-store on all pages and APIs, no PII in URLs or logs, Referrer-Policy: no-referrer on authenticated pages, disable autocomplete on sensitive one-time fields, clear clipboard hints.

    Accessibility: tables with captions and header scopes, amounts read correctly ("minus ₹2,500" not "dash 2500" — use visually hidden text for sign), charts with table alternatives, errors announced.

    Observability and audit: every sensitive action logged server-side with correlation IDs; client logs scrubbed; anomaly alerts on unusual CSP violations.

    Testing: E2E for transfer happy path + failure paths (insufficient funds, OTP failure, timeout, double-click), security tests (CSP enforced, cookies flags), contract tests with backend.

    11.6 Design 5 — AI support chat assistant, and Design 6 — Spotify-style persistent player#

    Design 5: AI support chat on apple.com/support#

    Reuse tab 9 and walk RADIO quickly:

    • R: answer product/support questions grounded in support articles; check order status for signed-in users; hand off to a human advisor; streaming; WCAG AA; no PII to the model without policy; works on mobile.

    • A: chat widget (lazy-loaded on click via a facade button so it costs nothing on page load) → BFF /api/assistant (auth, rate limit, redaction, RAG over support index, tools: getOrderStatus, createCase) → model provider; SSE back; conversation store server-side.

    • D: Conversation { id, messages: Message[], status: 'active' | 'handoff' | 'closed' }; Message { id, role, parts: (Text | Citation | Card | ToolProgress)[] } — typed parts so the UI renders design-system components, not HTML.

    • I: POST /assistant (stream), POST /assistant/:id/feedback, POST /assistant/:id/handoff.

    • O: time-to-first-token, batching renders per frame, virtualization for long threads, role=log with completion announcements, citations, handoff with transcript, guardrails, eval suite, cost controls, and a kill switch flag.

    Design 6: Spotify-style persistent player (music keeps playing while you navigate)#

    R: audio continues across page navigation; mini-player bar and full-screen view; queue, shuffle, repeat, seek; keyboard and media keys; lock-screen controls on mobile web; works offline for downloaded content (optional).

    A — the key insight: the <audio> element and player state must live outside the route tree so navigation never unmounts it.

    • SPA/App Router: player provider in the root layout; pages render inside it; client-side navigation keeps the layout mounted.

    • If the site does full page loads (MPA), options: make the shell an SPA, use View Transitions + a persistent element pattern, or move playback to a dedicated window/tab — and explain the trade-off.

    tsx
    // root layout (client part)
const PlayerCtx = createContext<PlayerApi | null>(null);
export function PlayerProvider({ children }: { children: React.ReactNode }) {
  const audioRef = useRef<HTMLAudioElement>(null);
  const store = usePlayerStore();              // Zustand: queue, index, status, position
  useMediaSession(store, audioRef);             // lock-screen + hardware keys
  return (
    <PlayerCtx.Provider value={store.api}>
      {children}
      <audio ref={audioRef} preload="metadata" onTimeUpdate={store.onTime} onEnded={store.next} />
      <MiniPlayer />                            {/* fixed bar, region labelled "Now playing" */}
    </PlayerCtx.Provider>
  );
}

function useMediaSession(store: PlayerStore, audio: React.RefObject<HTMLAudioElement>) {
  useEffect(() => {
    if (!('mediaSession' in navigator) || !store.current) return;
    navigator.mediaSession.metadata = new MediaMetadata({ title: store.current.title, artist: store.current.artist, artwork: store.current.art });
    navigator.mediaSession.setActionHandler('play', () => audio.current?.play());
    navigator.mediaSession.setActionHandler('pause', () => audio.current?.pause());
    navigator.mediaSession.setActionHandler('nexttrack', store.next);
    navigator.mediaSession.setActionHandler('previoustrack', store.prev);
  }, [store.current]);
}

    O — details that impress

    • Fast track switching: preload the next track's first segment; adaptive streaming (HLS/DASH via MSE) for long content.

    • Progress updates: timeupdate fires ~4×/s — update a ref and render the progress bar with rAF or CSS, not global state re-renders across the app.

    • Multiple tabs: BroadcastChannel so starting playback in one tab pauses the other.

    • Accessibility: real buttons with names that change ("Play"/"Pause" or aria-pressed), the seek bar as <input type="range"> with aria-valuetext="1 minute 32 seconds of 3 minutes 40 seconds", keyboard shortcuts that don't conflict with screen readers, no autoplay with sound.

    • Offline: cache encrypted segments in Cache Storage/IndexedDB via a service worker for downloaded playlists (licensing permitting).

    • Analytics: play started, 30-second listens, skips, buffering ratio — batched with sendBeacon.

    11.7 Level up: company-flavoured system design#

    At staff level, interviewers expect you to bring numbers, failure modes and organisational reality into the design. Here are the moves that separate a strong answer, then five designs in each company's style.

    Senior moves in any design round#

    • Back-of-envelope numbers. "50,000 rows × 200 bytes = 10 MB of JSON, so we can't send it all" or "10 updates per second per driver × 20 drivers visible = 200 messages per second, so coalesce per frame." Numbers justify decisions.

    • Failure modes per region of the page. For each box on your diagram, say what the user sees when it's slow, down or wrong.

    • Ownership. Which team owns which part, and how they ship independently (Netflix federated GraphQL, Walmart micro-frontends, Spotify's family of design systems).

    • Rollout and measurement. Feature flags, A/B test with guardrails, RUM per region, kill switch.

    • Security and privacy per data flow. What's personal, where it's cached, who can see it.

    Uber-style: live trip tracking on the web#

    Driver location every ~4 s, map with ETA, status changes (arriving, on trip), works on weak networks. Push channel with heartbeat and reconnect (RAMEN-style: detect silence within seconds, resume from last sequence number); interpolate the car's movement between updates with requestAnimationFrame so it glides instead of jumping; render the map tiles lazily and allow a no-map mode on slow devices (as m.uber did); keep the last known status in local storage for instant re-open; never trust device time for ETAs. Failure mode: show "Reconnecting…" and the last known position with its age.

    Atlassian-style: collaborative document editor#

    Multiple people editing the same page. Choose a sync model: OT (server transforms concurrent operations; mature, needs a central server) or CRDTs (e.g., Yjs; merge without a central authority, works offline, more memory). Show remote cursors and presence over WebSocket, debounce presence updates, persist snapshots plus an operation log, and handle permissions changes mid-session. Rich-text editors at this scale are built on structured document models (such as ProseMirror) rather than contenteditable HTML strings, so content is schema-validated and safe to render.

    Amazon-style: product page assembled by many teams#

    The page is composed of widgets owned by different teams (buy box, reviews, recommendations, Q&A, an AI assistant panel). Each widget gets a latency budget and timeout; the buy box is critical and server-rendered first; the rest stream in and fail independently (a broken recommendations widget disappears instead of breaking the page). Contracts between widgets are props and events, not shared state. Performance budget per widget, with ownership on a dashboard.

    Netflix-style: TV home screen on low-power devices#

    D-pad navigation with remembered focus per row; aggressive node recycling (a fixed pool of card elements reused as you scroll, since TVs have little memory); image sizes chosen per device class; preview video only after focus dwells and only one player instance; frame-time budgets measured on the cheapest supported device, not a laptop.

    Bank-style (JPMorgan / Goldman): client portfolio dashboard#

    Positions, P&L updating in real time, statements, transfers. Entitlements decide which accounts each user sees; real-time prices coalesced per frame in a worker; every number shows its "as of" time; no optimistic UI for money; step-up auth for transfers; strict CSP and no third-party scripts; audit events for every sensitive view and action; session timeout with an accessible warning; export generated server-side.

    How to answer "How would this change at 100× scale?"#

    Cache more at the edge, move work off the main thread and off the client, split ownership by domain, add backpressure and degradation paths, and invest in observability before features. Saying this unprompted at the end of a design round is a strong close.

    ← Previous10 · Testing, Tooling & CI/CDNext →12 · Machine Coding & DSA
    Topic 1218 min read4 diagrams

    12. Machine Coding & DSA

    12.1 How to run a machine-coding round#

    You have 45–60 minutes to build a working, clean, accessible component, which you'll write in React (confirm React is allowed in the first minute). They grade process as much as output.

    The 6-step script#

    StepTimeWhat you do and say
    1. Clarify3–5 minInputs, outputs, interactions, keyboard, mobile, edge cases, framework allowed? "I'll assume modern browsers and no libraries — OK?"
    2. Plan3 minSketch the component tree, props and state. Name the pieces: <Autocomplete>, useSuggestions(), onKeyDown, choose(item)
    3. Skeleton10 minSemantic HTML, minimal CSS, a working happy path end to end
    4. Behaviour15 minEvent handlers, state updates, re-render, keyboard support
    5. Edge cases + a11y10 minEmpty, overflow, rapid clicks, focus, ARIA, reduced motion
    6. Test + discuss5 minWalk through scenarios; mention perf, tests you'd write, how it would scale/become a reusable component

    Habits that earn points#

    • Minimal, well-placed state: one source of truth per value, derive everything else during render (selected item, filtered list, disabled options) instead of syncing extra state with effects.

    • Controlled + uncontrolled support (value/onChange or defaultValue) when it's a reusable component — interviewers notice.

    • Accessibility without being asked: native elements, useId for label/ARIA pairs, keyboard handlers, focus management with refs.

    • Effects only for the outside world (fetch, timers, observers, subscriptions), always with cleanup — AbortController for fetches, disconnect() for observers, clearInterval for timers.

    • Stable keys and handlers: real IDs as key; useCallback for handlers passed to memoized children; functional updates (setX(x => …)) to avoid stale closures.

    • Narrate decisions and trade-offs; if stuck, simplify and say so. Leave 5 minutes to test edge cases in front of them.

    Practice list (most-reported for UI roles)#

    Star rating · Tabs · Accordion · Modal · Toast system · Autocomplete · Infinite scroll · Image carousel · Nested comments · File explorer tree · Progress bar(s) with concurrency limit · Countdown/stopwatch · Pagination · Data table with sort/filter · Drag-and-drop list · Kanban board · Tic-tac-toe / Connect Four · Traffic light · Like button with optimistic update · Form with validation · Color/size product swatch selector · Typeahead with debounce.

    12.2 Solved components, part 1 (React)#

    All solutions are React + TypeScript function components, the way you'll write them in the round. Each one uses native elements first, keeps state minimal, and handles keyboard and screen readers without being asked.

    Star rating (accessible, keyboard, hover preview)#

    Key insight: a star rating is a radio group. Use native radios inside a fieldset and arrow keys plus VoiceOver ("3 stars, 3 of 5") work for free.

    tsx
    type StarRatingProps = { max?: number; value?: number; defaultValue?: number; onChange?(v: number): void; label?: string };

export function StarRating({ max = 5, value, defaultValue = 0, onChange, label = 'Rating' }: StarRatingProps) {
  const [inner, setInner] = useState(defaultValue);
  const [hover, setHover] = useState(0);
  const current = value ?? inner;              // controlled or uncontrolled
  const name = useId();
  const shown = hover || current;

  const select = (n: number) => { if (value === undefined) setInner(n); onChange?.(n); };

  return (
    <fieldset className="rating" onMouseLeave={() => setHover(0)}>
      <legend>{label}</legend>
      {Array.from({ length: max }, (_, i) => {
        const n = i + 1;
        const id = `${name}-${n}`;
        return (
          <Fragment key={n}>
            <input type="radio" className="visually-hidden" id={id} name={name} value={n}
                   checked={current === n} onChange={() => select(n)} />
            <label htmlFor={id} className={n <= shown ? 'star filled' : 'star'} onMouseEnter={() => setHover(n)}>
              <span aria-hidden="true">★</span>
              <span className="visually-hidden">{n} star{n > 1 ? 's' : ''}</span>
            </label>
          </Fragment>
        );
      })}
    </fieldset>
  );
}

    CSS: .star.filled { color: gold }, visible focus ring on input:focus-visible + label. Mention: half stars (two radios per star, or a read-only display with clip), read-only mode (<span role="img" aria-label="4.5 out of 5 stars">), and why useId matters (two ratings on one page must not share a radio name).

    Accessible tabs (data-driven, roving tabindex)#

    tsx
    type TabItem = { id: string; label: string; content: React.ReactNode };

export function Tabs({ tabs, label, defaultId }: { tabs: TabItem[]; label: string; defaultId?: string }) {
  const [active, setActive] = useState(defaultId ?? tabs[0].id);
  const tabRefs = useRef<(HTMLButtonElement | null)[]>([]);
  const base = useId();

  const onKeyDown = (e: React.KeyboardEvent) => {
    const i = tabs.findIndex(t => t.id === active);
    const map: Record<string, number> = { ArrowRight: i + 1, ArrowLeft: i - 1, Home: 0, End: tabs.length - 1 };
    if (!(e.key in map)) return;
    e.preventDefault();
    const next = (map[e.key] + tabs.length) % tabs.length;
    setActive(tabs[next].id);            // automatic activation
    tabRefs.current[next]?.focus();
  };

  return (
    <div>
      <div role="tablist" aria-label={label} onKeyDown={onKeyDown}>
        {tabs.map((t, i) => {
          const selected = t.id === active;
          return (
            <button key={t.id} ref={el => { tabRefs.current[i] = el; }} type="button" role="tab"
                    id={`${base}-tab-${t.id}`} aria-controls={`${base}-panel-${t.id}`}
                    aria-selected={selected} tabIndex={selected ? 0 : -1} onClick={() => setActive(t.id)}>
              {t.label}
            </button>
          );
        })}
      </div>
      {tabs.map(t => (
        <div key={t.id} role="tabpanel" id={`${base}-panel-${t.id}`} aria-labelledby={`${base}-tab-${t.id}`}
             hidden={t.id !== active} tabIndex={0}>
          {t.content}
        </div>
      ))}
    </div>
  );
}

    Follow-ups to expect: RTL (swap arrow directions), lazy-render inactive panels vs keep them mounted (keeping state), and the compound-component version in tab 4.2.

    Modal (React + native <dialog>)#

    Use the Modal component from tab 6.2 (showModal(), Esc, backdrop click, focus return). In the round, show it in use:

    tsx
    function DeleteDraftButton({ onDelete }: { onDelete(): void }) {
  const [open, setOpen] = useState(false);
  return (
    <>
      <button type="button" onClick={() => setOpen(true)}>Delete draft</button>
      <Modal open={open} onClose={() => setOpen(false)} title="Delete this draft?">
        <p>This can't be undone.</p>
        <button type="button" autoFocus onClick={() => setOpen(false)}>Cancel</button>
        <button type="button" onClick={() => { onDelete(); setOpen(false); }}>Delete</button>
      </Modal>
    </>
  );
}

    If asked to build it without <dialog>: createPortal to document.body, role="dialog" aria-modal="true" aria-labelledby, the useFocusTrap hook from tab 6.2, Esc handler, body scroll lock, and inert on the app root while open. Put the safe action (Cancel) first for destructive dialogs.

    Autocomplete (debounced, cancellable, accessible)#

    tsx
    type Item = { id: string; label: string };
type Props = { label: string; fetchSuggestions(q: string, signal: AbortSignal): Promise<Item[]>; onSelect(item: Item): void };

export function Autocomplete({ label, fetchSuggestions, onSelect }: Props) {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState('');
  const [items, setItems] = useState<Item[]>([]);
  const [active, setActive] = useState(-1);
  const [open, setOpen] = useState(false);
  const [status, setStatus] = useState('');
  const justSelected = useRef(false);
  const debounced = useDebouncedValue(query.trim(), 200);   // hook from tab 3.2
  const inputId = useId();
  const listId = `${inputId}-list`;

  useEffect(() => {
    if (justSelected.current) { justSelected.current = false; return; }   // don't reopen after a pick
    if (debounced.length < 2) { setItems([]); setOpen(false); return; }
    const ctrl = new AbortController();
    fetchSuggestions(debounced, ctrl.signal)
      .then(res => {
        setItems(res); setActive(-1); setOpen(true);
        setStatus(res.length ? `${res.length} suggestions available` : 'No suggestions');
      })
      .catch(e => { if (e.name !== 'AbortError') { setItems([]); setStatus('Suggestions unavailable'); } });
    return () => ctrl.abort();                                // cancel stale request → no race condition
  }, [debounced, fetchSuggestions]);

  const choose = (item: Item) => {
    justSelected.current = true;
    setQuery(item.label); setOpen(false); setItems([]); setActive(-1);
    onSelect(item);
  };

  const onKeyDown = (e: React.KeyboardEvent<HTMLInputElement>) => {
    if (e.key === 'Escape') { setOpen(false); return; }
    if (!open || items.length === 0) return;
    if (e.key === 'ArrowDown') { e.preventDefault(); setActive(a => Math.min(a + 1, items.length - 1)); }
    else if (e.key === 'ArrowUp') { e.preventDefault(); setActive(a => Math.max(a - 1, 0)); }
    else if (e.key === 'Enter' && active >= 0) { e.preventDefault(); choose(items[active]); }
  };

  return (
    <div className="autocomplete">
      <label htmlFor={inputId}>{label}</label>
      <input id={inputId} role="combobox" aria-autocomplete="list" aria-expanded={open} aria-controls={listId}
             aria-activedescendant={active >= 0 ? `${listId}-${active}` : undefined}
             value={query} onChange={e => setQuery(e.target.value)} onKeyDown={onKeyDown}
             onBlur={() => setOpen(false)} autoComplete="off" />
      {open && items.length > 0 && (
        <ul id={listId} role="listbox">
          {items.map((it, i) => (
            <li key={it.id} id={`${listId}-${i}`} role="option" aria-selected={i === active}
                onMouseDown={e => { e.preventDefault(); choose(it); }}   // mousedown + preventDefault keeps input focus
                onMouseEnter={() => setActive(i)}>
              <Highlight text={it.label} match={debounced} />
            </li>
          ))}
        </ul>
      )}
      <div role="status" className="visually-hidden">{status}</div>
    </div>
  );
}

// Bold the matched part safely — no dangerouslySetInnerHTML with user input
function Highlight({ text, match }: { text: string; match: string }) {
  const i = text.toLowerCase().indexOf(match.toLowerCase());
  if (!match || i < 0) return <>{text}</>;
  return <>{text.slice(0, i)}<strong>{text.slice(i, i + match.length)}</strong>{text.slice(i + match.length)}</>;
}

    Points to say: the parent must pass a stable fetchSuggestions (useCallback) or the effect re-runs every render; the effect cleanup aborts the previous request so out-of-order responses can't win; aria-activedescendant keeps focus in the input; at scale you'd swap the effect for React Query keyed by the query (tab 11.4).

    File explorer tree (React, recursive, accessible tree)#

    tsx
    type Node = { id: string; name: string; children?: Node[] };
function Tree({ nodes, level = 1 }: { nodes: Node[]; level?: number }) {
  return (
    <ul role={level === 1 ? 'tree' : 'group'} aria-label={level === 1 ? 'Files' : undefined}>
      {nodes.map(n => <TreeItem key={n.id} node={n} level={level} />)}
    </ul>
  );
}
function TreeItem({ node, level }: { node: Node; level: number }) {
  const [open, setOpen] = useState(false);
  const isFolder = !!node.children;
  return (
    <li role="treeitem" aria-level={level} aria-expanded={isFolder ? open : undefined} tabIndex={-1}
        onKeyDown={e => { if (e.key === 'ArrowRight' && isFolder) setOpen(true); if (e.key === 'ArrowLeft') setOpen(false); }}>
      <span onClick={() => isFolder && setOpen(o => !o)}>{isFolder ? (open ? '▾ ' : '▸ ') : ''}{node.name}</span>
      {isFolder && open && <Tree nodes={node.children!} level={level + 1} />}
    </li>
  );
}

    Mention the full APG tree pattern (roving tabindex, ↑/↓ across visible nodes, Home/End, type-ahead) and lazy-loading children for big trees.

    12.3 Solved components, part 2 (React)#

    Infinite scroll (IntersectionObserver + cursor + dedupe)#

    tsx
    type Page<T> = { items: T[]; next: string | null };

export function InfiniteList<T extends { id: string }>({ loadPage, renderItem }: {
  loadPage(cursor: string | null): Promise<Page<T>>; renderItem(item: T): React.ReactNode }) {
  const [items, setItems] = useState<T[]>([]);
  const [cursor, setCursor] = useState<string | null>(null);
  const [status, setStatus] = useState<'idle' | 'loading' | 'error' | 'done'>('idle');
  const loadingRef = useRef(false);                 // blocks double-trigger before state updates land
  const sentinelRef = useRef<HTMLDivElement>(null);

  const loadMore = useCallback(async () => {
    if (loadingRef.current || status === 'done') return;
    loadingRef.current = true; setStatus('loading');
    try {
      const { items: page, next } = await loadPage(cursor);
      setItems(prev => {
        const seen = new Set(prev.map(p => p.id));
        return [...prev, ...page.filter(p => !seen.has(p.id))];   // dedupe across pages
      });
      setCursor(next);
      setStatus(next ? 'idle' : 'done');
    } catch {
      setStatus('error');
    } finally {
      loadingRef.current = false;
    }
  }, [cursor, status, loadPage]);

  useEffect(() => {
    const el = sentinelRef.current;
    if (!el || status === 'done' || status === 'error') return;
    const io = new IntersectionObserver(([e]) => { if (e.isIntersecting) loadMore(); }, { rootMargin: '600px' });
    io.observe(el);
    return () => io.disconnect();
  }, [loadMore, status]);

  return (
    <>
      <ul>{items.map(it => <li key={it.id}>{renderItem(it)}</li>)}</ul>
      <div ref={sentinelRef}>
        {status === 'loading' && <p role="status">Loading more…</p>}
        {status === 'error' && <button type="button" onClick={loadMore}>Couldn't load more. Retry</button>}
        {status === 'idle' && <button type="button" onClick={loadMore}>Load more</button>}
        {status === 'done' && <p>You've reached the end</p>}
      </div>
    </>
  );
}

    Discuss: virtualization past ~1,000 items (TanStack Virtual); restoring scroll on back (keep pages in React Query's useInfiniteQuery cache); the "Load more" button so keyboard users can still reach the footer.

    Carousel (accessible, no auto-advance surprises)#

    tsx
    type Slide = { id: string; content: React.ReactNode };

export function Carousel({ slides, label, autoPlayMs }: { slides: Slide[]; label: string; autoPlayMs?: number }) {
  const [index, setIndex] = useState(0);
  const [playing, setPlaying] = useState(!!autoPlayMs);
  const [paused, setPaused] = useState(false);       // hover/focus pause
  const count = slides.length;
  const go = useCallback((n: number) => setIndex((n + count) % count), [count]);

  useEffect(() => {
    if (!autoPlayMs || !playing || paused) return;
    const reduce = matchMedia('(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)').matches;
    if (reduce) return;
    const t = setInterval(() => setIndex(i => (i + 1) % count), autoPlayMs);
    return () => clearInterval(t);
  }, [autoPlayMs, playing, paused, count]);

  return (
    <section aria-roledescription="carousel" aria-label={label} className="carousel"
             onMouseEnter={() => setPaused(true)} onMouseLeave={() => setPaused(false)}
             onFocus={() => setPaused(true)} onBlur={() => setPaused(false)}>
      <div className="controls">
        {autoPlayMs && <button type="button" onClick={() => setPlaying(p => !p)}>{playing ? 'Pause' : 'Play'}</button>}
        <button type="button" aria-label="Previous slide" onClick={() => go(index - 1)}>‹</button>
        <button type="button" aria-label="Next slide" onClick={() => go(index + 1)}>›</button>
      </div>
      <div className="viewport">
        <div className="track" style={{ transform: `translateX(${-100 * index}%)` }}>   {/* compositor-only */}
          {slides.map((s, i) => (
            <div key={s.id} role="group" aria-roledescription="slide" aria-label={`${i + 1} of ${count}`}
                 inert={i !== index} className="slide">   {/* inactive slides out of tab order + AT */}
              {s.content}
            </div>
          ))}
        </div>
      </div>
      <div aria-live={playing && !paused ? 'off' : 'polite'} className="visually-hidden">Slide {index + 1} of {count}</div>
    </section>
  );
}
// CSS: .viewport { overflow: hidden } .track { display: flex; transition: transform .4s ease } .slide { flex: 0 0 100% }
// @media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) { .track { transition: none } }

    Notes to say: inert is a boolean prop in React 19 (on React 18 pass inert="" or set it via a ref); the live region is silenced during auto-play so it doesn't chatter; alternative is CSS scroll-snap-type: x mandatory with native scrolling — great on touch, minimal JS.

    Nested comments (recursive components + immutable reply)#

    tsx
    type Comment = { id: string; text: string; replies: Comment[] };

function addReply(tree: Comment[], parentId: string, reply: Comment): Comment[] {
  return tree.map(c => c.id === parentId
    ? { ...c, replies: [...c.replies, reply] }
    : { ...c, replies: addReply(c.replies, parentId, reply) });
}

export function CommentThread({ initial }: { initial: Comment[] }) {
  const [comments, setComments] = useState(initial);
  const onReply = useCallback((parentId: string, text: string) =>
    setComments(t => addReply(t, parentId, { id: crypto.randomUUID(), text, replies: [] })), []);
  return <CommentList comments={comments} onReply={onReply} />;
}

function CommentList({ comments, onReply }: { comments: Comment[]; onReply(id: string, text: string): void }) {
  return <ul>{comments.map(c => <CommentItem key={c.id} comment={c} onReply={onReply} />)}</ul>;
}

const CommentItem = memo(function CommentItem({ comment, onReply }: { comment: Comment; onReply(id: string, text: string): void }) {
  const [replying, setReplying] = useState(false);
  const [draft, setDraft] = useState('');
  const fieldId = useId();
  return (
    <li>
      <p>{comment.text}</p>   {/* React escapes text → XSS-safe */}
      <button type="button" aria-expanded={replying} onClick={() => setReplying(r => !r)}>Reply</button>
      {replying && (
        <form onSubmit={e => {
          e.preventDefault();
          if (!draft.trim()) return;
          onReply(comment.id, draft.trim()); setDraft(''); setReplying(false);
        }}>
          <label htmlFor={fieldId} className="visually-hidden">Reply to comment</label>
          <textarea id={fieldId} value={draft} onChange={e => setDraft(e.target.value)} autoFocus />
          <button type="submit">Post reply</button>
        </form>
      )}
      {comment.replies.length > 0 && <CommentList comments={comment.replies} onReply={onReply} />}
    </li>
  );
});

    Talk about: memo + immutable updates mean only the changed branch re-renders; at scale, normalize ({ byId, childIds }) for O(1) updates; collapse deep threads and lazy-load replies; nested <ul>s give screen readers "list, level 2" for free.

    Progress bars with a concurrency limit (classic)#

    "Add a progress bar each time a button is clicked; each fills in 2 s; at most 3 fill at once."

    tsx
    const LIMIT = 3;
type Bar = { id: string; state: 'queued' | 'running' | 'done' };

export function ProgressBars() {
  const [bars, setBars] = useState<Bar[]>([]);

  // Whenever something finishes or is added, start queued bars up to the limit
  useEffect(() => {
    const running = bars.filter(b => b.state === 'running').length;
    const next = bars.find(b => b.state === 'queued');
    if (running < LIMIT && next) setBars(bs => bs.map(b => (b.id === next.id ? { ...b, state: 'running' } : b)));
  }, [bars]);

  const finish = (id: string) => setBars(bs => bs.map(b => (b.id === id ? { ...b, state: 'done' } : b)));

  return (
    <>
      <button type="button" onClick={() => setBars(bs => [...bs, { id: crypto.randomUUID(), state: 'queued' }])}>Add</button>
      {bars.map((b, i) => (
        <div key={b.id} className="track" role="progressbar" aria-label={`Task ${i + 1}`}
             aria-valuemin={0} aria-valuemax={100} aria-valuenow={b.state === 'done' ? 100 : b.state === 'queued' ? 0 : undefined}>
          <div className={b.state === 'queued' ? 'fill' : 'fill go'}
               onTransitionEnd={() => b.state === 'running' && finish(b.id)} />
        </div>
      ))}
    </>
  );
}
// CSS: .fill { height: 8px; transform: scaleX(0); transform-origin: left; transition: transform 2s linear }
//      .fill.go { transform: scaleX(1) }   ← compositor-only animation

    Follow-ups: pause/resume (store elapsed time and switch to rAF-driven progress), cancel, and showing a real percentage for uploads (XMLHttpRequest.upload.onprogress — fetch has no upload progress).

    12.4 DSA for frontend interviews#

    UI-engineer DSA rounds are usually easy–medium, often with a DOM or UI flavour. Always state time and space complexity.

    Patterns to know cold#

    PatternTypical frontend usePractice problems
    Hash map / setDedupe, grouping, counting, cachesTwo Sum, Group Anagrams, First Unique Character
    Two pointers / sliding windowStrings, rangesLongest Substring Without Repeating Characters, Valid Palindrome
    StackBracket matching, undo/redo, HTML tag validationValid Parentheses, Min Stack, Decode String
    Queue / BFSTree level order, DOM traversal, shortest path in gridLevel Order Traversal, Number of Islands
    DFS / recursionDOM tree, nested comments, JSON flattenMax Depth, Path Sum, Flatten Nested Object
    IntervalsCalendars, meeting rooms, merging selectionsMerge Intervals, Meeting Rooms II
    Binary searchVirtualized list index from scroll offset (variable heights)Search Insert Position
    Heap / priorityTop-K, schedulingKth Largest, Top K Frequent
    LRU (map + order)CachesLRU Cache

    Solved: LRU cache (asked a lot — it's a real frontend cache)#

    js
    class LRUCache {
  constructor(capacity) { this.cap = capacity; this.map = new Map(); } // Map keeps insertion order
  get(key) {
    if (!this.map.has(key)) return -1;
    const v = this.map.get(key); this.map.delete(key); this.map.set(key, v); // move to most-recent
    return v;
  }
  put(key, value) {
    if (this.map.has(key)) this.map.delete(key);
    this.map.set(key, value);
    if (this.map.size > this.cap) this.map.delete(this.map.keys().next().value); // evict least-recent
  }
}
// O(1) get/put

    Solved: merge intervals (calendar / hearing slots)#

    js
    function merge(intervals) {
  intervals.sort((a, b) => a[0] - b[0]);
  const out = [];
  for (const [s, e] of intervals) {
    if (out.length && s <= out.at(-1)[1]) out.at(-1)[1] = Math.max(out.at(-1)[1], e);
    else out.push([s, e]);
  }
  return out;
} // O(n log n)

    Solved: flatten a nested object (form state, analytics payloads)#

    js
    function flattenObject(obj, prefix = '', out = {}) {
  for (const [k, v] of Object.entries(obj)) {
    const key = prefix ? `${prefix}.${k}` : k;
    if (v && typeof v === 'object' && !Array.isArray(v)) flattenObject(v, key, out);
    else out[key] = v;
  }
  return out;
}
// { a: { b: 1, c: { d: 2 } } } → { 'a.b': 1, 'a.c.d': 2 }

    Solved: find the nearest common ancestor of two DOM nodes#

    js
    function commonAncestor(a, b) {
  const seen = new Set();
  for (let n = a; n; n = n.parentNode) seen.add(n);
  for (let n = b; n; n = n.parentNode) if (seen.has(n)) return n;
  return null;
} // O(depth); native: a.contains(b), Range.commonAncestorContainer

    Solved: identical DOM tree — find the corresponding node#

    "Given two identical DOM trees A and B and a node in A, return the same-position node in B."

    js
    function findCorresponding(rootA, rootB, target) {
  const path = [];
  for (let n = target; n !== rootA; n = n.parentNode) path.push([...n.parentNode.children].indexOf(n));
  return path.reverse().reduce((node, i) => node.children[i], rootB);
}

    Solved: valid parentheses / HTML tag balance#

    js
    function isValid(s) {
  const pairs = { ')': '(', ']': '[', '}': '{' }, st = [];
  for (const ch of s) {
    if ('([{'.includes(ch)) st.push(ch);
    else if (ch in pairs && st.pop() !== pairs[ch]) return false;
  }
  return st.length === 0;
}

    Solved: binary search for a virtualized list with variable row heights#

    js
    // offsets[i] = top position of row i (prefix sums of heights). Find first visible row for scrollTop.
function firstVisible(offsets, scrollTop) {
  let lo = 0, hi = offsets.length - 1;
  while (lo < hi) { const mid = (lo + hi + 1) >> 1; if (offsets[mid] <= scrollTop) lo = mid; else hi = mid - 1; }
  return lo;
} // O(log n) — connect this to a real virtualization problem when you explain it

    Daily practice plan#

    One easy + one medium per day from the table above, timed (20 + 30 min). Say the brute force first, then optimize; write tests with 3 inputs including an edge case (empty, one element, duplicates).

    12.5 Level up: company-reported problems, solved in React#

    Public interview-prep sites list questions candidates report from these companies. Uber's reported list includes Grid Lights, Map Async, Map Async Limit and Memoize (source); Amazon's includes Accordion, Contact Form and Signup Form (source). One caution: Amazon's front-end guide says Vanilla JavaScript is the preferred language, so ask your recruiter whether React is allowed before each round.

    Grid Lights (reported at Uber)#

    Cells light up when clicked; once all are lit, they switch off one by one in reverse order of activation.

    tsx
    const LAYOUT = [
  [1, 1, 1],
  [1, 0, 1],      // 0 = empty slot
  [1, 1, 1],
];

export function GridLights({ delayMs = 300 }: { delayMs?: number }) {
  const cells = LAYOUT.flat();
  const total = cells.filter(Boolean).length;
  const [order, setOrder] = useState<number[]>([]);   // a stack of activated indices
  const [unwinding, setUnwinding] = useState(false);

  useEffect(() => {
    if (!unwinding) return;
    if (order.length === 0) { setUnwinding(false); return; }
    const t = setTimeout(() => setOrder(o => o.slice(0, -1)), delayMs);   // pop the most recent
    return () => clearTimeout(t);                                          // cleanup on unmount or re-run
  }, [unwinding, order, delayMs]);

  const activate = (i: number) => {
    if (unwinding || order.includes(i)) return;
    const next = [...order, i];
    setOrder(next);
    if (next.length === total) setUnwinding(true);
  };

  return (
    <div role="group" aria-label="Grid lights" className="grid"
         style={{ display: 'grid', gridTemplateColumns: `repeat(${LAYOUT[0].length}, 64px)`, gap: 8 }}>
      {cells.map((slot, i) => slot ? (
        <button key={i} type="button" aria-label={`Light ${i + 1}`} aria-pressed={order.includes(i)}
                aria-disabled={unwinding || undefined} onClick={() => activate(i)}
                className={order.includes(i) ? 'cell lit' : 'cell'} />
      ) : <span key={i} aria-hidden="true" />)}
    </div>
  );
}

    Points to say: the order is a stack, not a set; the timer chain lives in an effect with cleanup so unmounting mid-unwind doesn't update a dead component; aria-pressed gives toggle semantics; aria-disabled (not disabled) keeps focus on the button while unwinding.

    Nested checkboxes with tri-state parents (common at Atlassian, Amazon, banks)#

    A tree of permissions or filters: checking a parent checks all children; a parent with some children checked shows "mixed".

    tsx
    type Node = { id: string; label: string; children?: Node[] };
type Tri = 'on' | 'off' | 'mixed';

const leaves = (n: Node): string[] => (n.children?.length ? n.children.flatMap(leaves) : [n.id]);

function stateOf(n: Node, checked: Set<string>): Tri {
  const ls = leaves(n);
  const count = ls.filter(id => checked.has(id)).length;
  return count === 0 ? 'off' : count === ls.length ? 'on' : 'mixed';
}

export function CheckboxTree({ nodes }: { nodes: Node[] }) {
  const [checked, setChecked] = useState<Set<string>>(new Set());  // store leaf ids only; parents are derived
  const toggle = (n: Node) => setChecked(prev => {
    const next = new Set(prev);
    const turnOn = stateOf(n, prev) !== 'on';
    leaves(n).forEach(id => (turnOn ? next.add(id) : next.delete(id)));
    return next;
  });
  const renderNodes = (list: Node[]) => (
    <ul>
      {list.map(n => (
        <li key={n.id}>
          <TriCheckbox label={n.label} state={stateOf(n, checked)} onChange={() => toggle(n)} />
          {n.children?.length ? renderNodes(n.children) : null}
        </li>
      ))}
    </ul>
  );
  return renderNodes(nodes);
}

function TriCheckbox({ label, state, onChange }: { label: string; state: Tri; onChange(): void }) {
  const ref = useRef<HTMLInputElement>(null);
  useEffect(() => { if (ref.current) ref.current.indeterminate = state === 'mixed'; }, [state]); // only settable via JS
  return <label><input ref={ref} type="checkbox" checked={state === 'on'} onChange={onChange} /> {label}</label>;
}

    Points to say: storing only leaves makes illegal states impossible (a parent can't disagree with its children); indeterminate is announced as "mixed" by screen readers; for thousands of nodes, memoize leaf lists per node and virtualize.

    Accordion and forms (reported at Amazon)#

    The bar is in the details, so say them while you build: each header is a <button aria-expanded aria-controls> inside an <h3>; panels use hidden; support "allow multiple open" via a Set of open IDs; forms use real labels, aria-describedby for hints and errors, validation on blur and submit, focus moved to an error summary, and a disabled-while-submitting button that keeps focus.

    Kanban card move (Atlassian style)#

    State as { columns: Record<ColumnId, CardId[]> } with cards normalized by ID. Moving a card is a pure function move(state, cardId, toColumn, toIndex) you can unit test. Drag-and-drop calls it on drop; the keyboard path (Space to lift, arrows to choose, Space to drop) and a "Move to…" menu call the same function, with a live-region announcement. Optimistic update with rollback if the server rejects the move.

    ← Previous11 · Frontend System DesignNext →13 · Preferred Qualifications
    Topic 1311 min read5 diagrams

    13. Preferred Qualifications

    13.1 AEM and content management#

    You don't need to be an AEM developer; you need to talk confidently about how a frontend team works with a CMS that marketing teams control — exactly the Retail and Marcom reality.

    AEM in one minute#

    Adobe Experience Manager is an enterprise CMS (Java/OSGi on Apache Sling and a JCR content repository). Authors build pages in a visual editor from components; content goes through workflows (draft → review → publish) and is replicated from the Author instance to Publish instances, cached by the Dispatcher (an Apache-based cache/load balancer) and a CDN.

    AEM conceptWhat it means for a frontend engineer
    ComponentsA server template (HTL/Sightly) + dialog (author fields) + client-side JS/CSS. Your design-system component often maps 1:1
    Templates & policiesWhich components authors may use on which page types, and with which style options
    Client libraries (clientlibs)AEM's way to bundle/serve CSS/JS; modern setups build with webpack/Vite and output a clientlib
    Content Fragments / Experience FragmentsStructured, reusable content (product copy, banners) — exposed headlessly via GraphQL
    Dispatcher cacheCached HTML at the edge; invalidated on publish — affects how you handle personalization (do it client-side or via ESI/edge)

    Headless vs hybrid#

    • Traditional: AEM renders HTML with HTL; frontend ships JS enhancements.

    • Headless: AEM provides content via GraphQL/JSON; a Next.js app renders. Faster frontend iteration, modern tooling, but authors lose in-context editing unless you use the Universal Editor / SPA Editor.

    • Hybrid (common at large companies): marketing pages authored in AEM, app-like flows (configurator, bag, checkout) in a React app; shared design system across both — often why Web Components or framework-agnostic CSS matter.

    Frontend concerns with any CMS#

    • Authors can break layouts: design components for any content length, empty fields, missing images, 3 vs 30 items; validate in dialogs; preview in all locales.

    • Rich text safety: sanitize author HTML, restrict allowed tags.

    • Performance: components loaded only when on the page (per-component JS), image renditions from the DAM, lazy-loading by default.

    • Localization: content per locale with fallbacks; translation workflows; RTL.

    • Preview and publishing: author preview environments, cache invalidation on publish, scheduled launches (product launch at a set time across time zones).

    If you lack AEM experience, say: "I haven't shipped on AEM directly, but I've worked with CMS-driven pages in [project] and with JSON-driven component rendering. The concepts — components with author dialogs, templates, publish workflows, dispatcher caching — map closely to how I'd design a component-driven page builder, and I'd ramp up on HTL and clientlibs quickly." Honest + transferable is the right tone.

    13.2 Micro-frontends and modular architecture#

    Micro-frontends split one product into independently built and deployed frontend apps owned by different teams, composed into one experience. They solve an organizational problem (many teams blocking each other on one deploy), not a technical one.

    Each MFE can use a different composition method; what keeps them one product is the shared foundation and narrow contracts.

    Composition approaches#

    ApproachHowProsCons
    Build-time packagesEach team publishes an npm package; the shell installs themSimple, type-safe, one bundleNot independently deployable — shell must rebuild
    Server-side / edge compositionServer or edge stitches HTML fragments (SSI/ESI, route-based)Fast first paint, SEO-friendly, tech-agnosticShared state and client navigation are harder
    Route-based (multi-zone)Each team owns full routes (/shop, /support) — e.g. Next.js Multi-ZonesStrong isolation, simplest runtimeFull page loads between zones; shared header duplicated
    Runtime JS — Module FederationHost loads remote modules at runtime, sharing React as a singletonIndependent deploys, shared deps, SPA feelVersion skew, runtime failures, harder debugging
    Web Components / iframesEach MFE is a custom element or iframeFramework-agnostic, strong isolation (iframes)Styling/SSR complexity; iframes hurt a11y/perf

    Rules that keep them healthy#

    • Split by business domain, not by component type (Product, Bag, Checkout, Support — not "Header team").

    • Shared design system + tokens so it looks like one product; enforce versions with a compatibility range.

    • Thin shell: routing, auth/session, global layout, error boundaries per MFE, feature flags.

    • Contracts between MFEs: URL, custom events (window.dispatchEvent(new CustomEvent('bag:updated', { detail }))), or a tiny pub/sub — avoid shared global stores.

    • Performance budgets per MFE; share React and the design system as singletons; avoid loading two copies of libraries.

    • Each MFE handles its own failure (fallback UI) so one broken team doesn't take down the page.

    When NOT to use them#

    One or two teams, or a small app: a modular monolith (monorepo with clear package boundaries, CODEOWNERS, lint rules for import boundaries, independent test/build caching) gives 80% of the benefit with none of the runtime cost. Say this — it's the senior answer.

    Your story (adapt): "In the PLUS ecosystem, several apps share the same header, auth, and case components. We kept them as separate deployables per domain but shared a versioned design-system package and an auth/session package, with a common shell layout. That gave teams independent releases without the runtime complexity of Module Federation."

    13.3 Node.js, BFF, cloud and CI/CD for frontend engineers#

    Node.js essentials to discuss#

    • Event loop on the server: same single thread for JS, but libuv handles I/O and a thread pool (fs, crypto, DNS). CPU-heavy work (PDF generation, image resizing) blocks every request → move to worker threads or a separate service.

    • Streams: pipe large responses (CSV exports, SSE) instead of buffering in memory; handle backpressure.

    • Error handling: async errors must be caught; unhandled rejections crash the process; use a framework error middleware and graceful shutdown (stop accepting, finish in-flight, close DB).

    • Security: validate input (Zod), set headers (helmet), rate limit, never trust client data, secrets from env/secret manager.

    A minimal BFF endpoint (Next.js route handler or Express)#

    ts
    // GET /api/case/:id — aggregate 3 services in parallel, with timeouts and partial failure
export async function GET(req: Request, { params }: { params: { id: string } }) {
  const session = await requireSession(req);
  const t = () => AbortSignal.timeout(2000);
  const [caseRes, docsRes, hearingsRes] = await Promise.allSettled([
    casesApi.get(params.id, { signal: t(), token: session.token }),
    docsApi.list(params.id, { signal: t(), token: session.token }),
    hearingsApi.list(params.id, { signal: t(), token: session.token }),
  ]);
  if (caseRes.status === 'rejected') return Response.json({ error: 'Case unavailable' }, { status: 502 });
  return Response.json({
    case: toCaseView(caseRes.value),                                  // shape for the UI, drop internal fields
    documents: docsRes.status === 'fulfilled' ? docsRes.value.map(toDocView) : null,  // null = show "couldn't load"
    hearings: hearingsRes.status === 'fulfilled' ? hearingsRes.value : null,
  }, { headers: { 'Cache-Control': 'private, no-store' } });
}

    Cloud for frontend engineers#

    ConceptWhat to say
    Static hosting + CDNS3/Cloud Storage + CloudFront/Akamai; immutable hashed assets; invalidate HTML only
    SSR hostingContainers (Kubernetes/ECS) or serverless functions; cold starts vs always-on cost
    Edge functionsLocale redirect, A/B bucketing, auth checks close to users; tiny runtimes, no heavy deps
    ObservabilityLogs, metrics, traces (OpenTelemetry); RUM on the client; alerts on SLOs
    EnvironmentsPreview per PR, staging, production; config via environment variables and secrets manager
    ScalingHorizontal autoscaling for SSR; cache aggressively so scaling is rarely needed

    (CI/CD pipeline details are in tab 10.3.)

    13.4 Data visualization with D3#

    D3's model in one paragraph#

    D3 is a toolkit, not a chart library: scales map data to pixels (scaleLinear, scaleTime, scaleBand), shapes generate SVG paths (line, area, arc), axes draw ticks, and the data join (selection.data().join()) creates, updates, and removes elements to match data. Transitions animate between states.

    D3 with React — the pattern to describe#

    Let React own the DOM and use D3 only for math (scales, shapes, layouts). This keeps rendering declarative, SSR-friendly, and testable. Use D3's DOM manipulation only for things React does poorly (zoom/brush behaviours, complex transitions) inside a ref.

    tsx
    function PriceHistory({ data, width = 640, height = 240 }: { data: { date: Date; price: number }[]; width?: number; height?: number }) {
  const m = { top: 16, right: 16, bottom: 28, left: 56 };
  const x = d3.scaleTime().domain(d3.extent(data, d => d.date) as [Date, Date]).range([m.left, width - m.right]);
  const y = d3.scaleLinear().domain([0, d3.max(data, d => d.price)!]).nice().range([height - m.bottom, m.top]);
  const path = d3.line<typeof data[number]>().x(d => x(d.date)).y(d => y(d.price))(data)!;
  const fmt = new Intl.NumberFormat('en-IN', { style: 'currency', currency: 'INR', maximumFractionDigits: 0 });
  return (
    <figure>
      <svg viewBox={`0 0 ${width} ${height}`} role="img" aria-labelledby="ph-title ph-desc">
        <title id="ph-title">Price over the last 12 months</title>
        <desc id="ph-desc">Price fell from {fmt.format(data[0].price)} to {fmt.format(data.at(-1)!.price)}.</desc>
        {y.ticks(4).map(t => <g key={t}><line x1={m.left} x2={width - m.right} y1={y(t)} y2={y(t)} stroke="currentColor" opacity={0.1} /><text x={m.left - 8} y={y(t)} textAnchor="end" dy="0.32em">{fmt.format(t)}</text></g>)}
        <path d={path} fill="none" stroke="var(--color-accent)" strokeWidth={2} />
      </svg>
      <figcaption>Data table available below</figcaption>
    </figure>
  );
}

    What to mention for production charts#

    • Responsive: viewBox + container width via ResizeObserver.

    • Large data: SVG is fine up to a few thousand elements; beyond that use Canvas (or WebGL) and keep an invisible accessible layer; downsample (LTTB) for time series.

    • Accessibility: title/desc, a data table alternative, keyboard-focusable points with labels for interactive charts, patterns not just color, sufficient contrast.

    • Performance: compute scales with useMemo, throttle tooltips to rAF, run heavy aggregation in a Web Worker.

    • Libraries: Visx (D3 + React primitives), Recharts/Nivo for standard charts; D3 directly for bespoke visuals.

    13.5 Modernizing legacy applications#

    The senior principle: never a big-bang rewrite. Migrate incrementally while shipping features, with the old and new running side by side.

    The strangler fig pattern#

    1. Put a routing layer in front (reverse proxy, edge, or the new app's router) so each URL can be served by old or new.

    2. Pick a seam: start with a low-risk, high-value route (e.g., a read-only page) to prove the stack, build the shared foundation (design system, auth, API client), then move high-traffic routes.

    3. Migrate route by route; the legacy app shrinks until it can be deleted.

    4. Share session and look-and-feel across both during the transition (same cookie domain, shared CSS tokens or a Web Component header).

    Common migrations and the tactics#

    From → ToTactics
    jQuery / server templates → ReactMount React islands into existing pages (createRoot on a div); replace widgets one at a time; communicate via custom events
    AngularJS → React/AngularHybrid bootstrapping or route-level split behind a proxy; shared services via an adapter
    CRA / webpack → Vite or Next.jsMove build config first, keep code; then adopt routing/SSR route by route
    Next.js Pages → App RouterBoth routers coexist; move leaf routes first; data fetching into Server Components
    JS → TypeScriptallowJs, rename files gradually starting at leaves and shared utils, strict per folder, types for API boundaries first
    Class components → hooksOnly when touching the code; codemods for mechanical parts
    Redux everywhere → React Query + small storeMove server data first, one entity at a time

    Risk control#

    • Characterization tests (E2E snapshots of current behaviour) before touching legacy code.

    • Feature flags and canary per migrated route; compare Web Vitals and error rates old vs new.

    • Track migration progress as a metric (% of routes/components migrated) and remove dead code continuously.

    • Plan for SEO (same URLs, redirects, canonical tags) and analytics parity.

    Your story (adapt): "At Accenture we had a large legacy frontend with class components and a homegrown build. Instead of a rewrite, we introduced TypeScript with allowJs, migrated the build to a modern bundler, and moved feature areas to hooks and a shared component library as we touched them. Build times dropped, and new features shipped on the modern stack from week one."

    13.6 Level up: how big companies did migrations, micro-frontends and data viz#

    Migrations#

    • Walmart: moved walmart.com (about two dozen applications) from Handlebars, Backbone and Java to React and Node.js app by app, while the site kept running at 80 million monthly visitors. Server-rendered pages that had been client-rendered got up to 30% faster, and JS bundles shrank about 20% (source). Lesson: a platform (Electrode) plus app-by-app migration, not a rewrite.

    • Spotify: unified desktop and web by growing the web player's codebase to desktop-grade features inside the desktop container, rewriting in TypeScript "bit by bit" and proving feasibility with a small virtual team first (source).

    • Netflix: migrated consumer APIs to federated GraphQL domain by domain, investing in developer experience (tooling, tracing) to onboard dozens of teams (source).

    Micro-frontends#

    • Spotify (historic): each squad's feature ran in its own iframe inside the desktop client, so squads could choose tools independently. It maximised autonomy but cost consistency and performance, which is part of why they later converged on one codebase (source).

    • Walmart: Electrode's open-source README lists "large scale micro-frontend architecture" as a core focus (source).

    • Lesson for interviews: micro-frontends trade consistency and performance for team independence. Spotify's arc — iframes for autonomy, then convergence for quality — is the best story to tell when asked "would you use micro-frontends?"

    Data visualization at scale#

    • JPMorgan Perspective: streaming pivots and charts over large data via WebAssembly in a Web Worker, with charts built on D3FC (a D3 component library from Scott Logic) (source).

    • Amazon Cloudscape: ships chart components (line, bar, area, pie) as part of the design system so AWS console teams get consistent, accessible charts (source).

    Staff-level insights#

    • Migration success metric is percent of traffic (or routes) on the new stack plus no regression in Core Web Vitals and conversion, reported weekly. Walmart's tracking of traffic share through the new platform is the model.

    • The last 10% of a migration is the hardest. Rarely used pages with odd behaviour. Budget for it explicitly, or two stacks live forever.

    • For charts beyond ~10k points, move from SVG to Canvas or WebGL, downsample on the server or in a worker, and keep an accessible table and summary alongside.

    Advanced questions#

    Q: We have 30 teams on one React monolith with weekly releases blocked by each other. Do we split into micro-frontends? First try a modular monolith: domain packages with enforced boundaries, independent CI per package, feature flags and trunk-based releases. Split only domains that truly need independent deploys, by route, sharing one design system and React as singletons. Cite Spotify's iframe era as the cost of going too far.

    Q: How do you migrate a revenue-critical checkout from a legacy stack with zero downtime? Proxy routing, rebuild checkout behind a flag, shadow-run with internal users, ramp 1% → 100% by region while comparing conversion and error rates, keep the old path warm for instant rollback, and delete it only after a full peak season on the new one.

    14. Behavioral & Apple Fit

    14.1 Eight STAR stories to adapt and rehearse#

    Eight stories cover nearly every behavioural question if you can bend them. Write yours in this format, keep each to 90 seconds spoken, and make sure every number is real. The prompts below suggest where each story probably comes from in your career — replace them with what actually happened.

    #ThemeQuestions it answersSuggested source
    1Performance win"Hardest technical problem", "something you're proud of", "impact with data"PLUS case list / dashboard (tab 5.4)
    2Accessibility remediation"Quality bar", "user empathy", "fixing root causes"PLUS ADA audit (tab 6.3)
    3Design system / reuse"Influence without authority", "scaling a team"Shared component library (tab 4)
    4Disagreement with design or product"Conflict", "pushing back", "collaboration"A spec that hurt a11y or perf
    5Production incident"Ownership", "pressure", "debugging"A bug in prod you diagnosed and fixed
    6Mentoring / leading"Leadership", "raising the bar", "code reviews"Tech-lead work at Zensar/Accenture
    7Mistake or failure"Biggest mistake", "what would you do differently"Something you shipped that went wrong
    8AI-assisted delivery or learning fast"Learning something new quickly", "using AI tools"Claude/Copilot workflow, Python/FastAPI learning

    Story 4 — Disagreement with design (worked example)#

    • Situation: "Design proposed a custom animated dropdown for the case-type selector on PLUS, replacing the native select."

    • Task: "I owned the implementation and was concerned about keyboard and screen-reader support, plus mobile behaviour."

    • Action: "Instead of saying no, I built two quick prototypes in Storybook — the custom version and a styled native select — and ran both through VoiceOver and keyboard with the designer watching. I showed what it would cost to make the custom one fully accessible. We agreed on a styled native select for mobile and an accessible combobox for desktop where search was valuable."

    • Result: "Shipped on time, passed the accessibility review, and the designer started inviting me to early design crits."

    • Learning: "Show, don't argue. Bringing evidence to the person turned a disagreement into a shared decision."

    Story 5 — Production incident (worked example)#

    • Situation: "After a release, some users couldn't submit a filing — the button spun forever."

    • Task: "I was the on-call frontend engineer."

    • Action: "Error monitoring showed a spike in a validation error only on Safari. I reproduced it with WebKit and found a date-parsing difference: we parsed a non-ISO date string that Chrome accepted and Safari rejected. I hot-fixed by parsing with an explicit format, added a WebKit project to our Playwright suite, and wrote a short post-mortem."

    • Result: "Fixed within hours; no repeat since, and WebKit runs on every PR now."

    • Learning: "Cross-browser testing is non-negotiable, and errors should never fail silently — the button now shows an error state."

    (These two are examples of shape and detail level. Use them only if they're true for you; otherwise write your own in the same format.)

    Story 7 — Mistake (structure that works)#

    Own it plainly ("I shipped X without Y"), the impact, what you did immediately, what you changed so it can't happen again (process or tooling, not just "I'll be careful"), and what it taught you. Never pick a fake weakness.

    14.2 Apple-specific questions with model answers#

    Q: Why Apple? Why this team? A (shape): one sentence on craft — "I care about the details users feel but rarely notice: a focus ring, a 100 ms faster tap response, a screen reader announcement that makes sense." One on scale and impact — "Retail and Marcom engineering is where Apple's product story meets every customer; the Store and product pages are used by people on every kind of device, in every language." One on fit — "My work on accessible, performance-focused React apps and design systems, plus building with LLMs, maps directly to what this role asks for." Avoid generic "I love Apple products" as the main reason.

    Q: Walk me through your current project. A: Two minutes: what PLUS is (an arbitration and dispute-resolution platform for the American Arbitration Association, built through Zensar), who uses it, your scope (modules you own, tech lead duties), the architecture in one breath (React/Next.js + TypeScript, API layer, design system), and one hard problem you solved. Then stop and let them pick where to dig. Respect client confidentiality — describe, don't disclose.

    Q: Tell me about something you built that you're proud of. A: Story 1 or 3 — with numbers and a clear personal contribution ("I", not "we").

    Q: How do you handle a deadline that would compromise quality? A: Make the trade-off visible: what can ship on time at full quality (reduce scope), what risk the shortcut carries (e.g., a11y or security debt), and a recommendation. Accessibility and security are not the scope I cut; secondary features are.

    Q: How do you work with designers? A: Early and often: kick-off on states and edge cases, prototype quickly in Storybook, design QA before merge, shared tokens so we speak the same language. Disagreements resolved with prototypes and user evidence (Story 4).

    Q: How do you stay current? A: Specific sources and recent practice: release notes and RFCs (React, Next.js, TC39, WCAG), web.dev, WebKit blog (relevant for Safari), building small projects (your Claude streaming demo; Python/FastAPI learning). Mention one recent thing you learned and applied.

    Q: Describe a time you raised the quality bar for your team. A: Story 2, 3, or the CI improvements in tab 10.3 — something that changed the system, not a one-off fix.

    Q: What's a product experience on apple.com you'd improve? A: Prepare one thoughtful, respectful observation after using the site with DevTools and VoiceOver (e.g., a configurator interaction, a performance detail on mobile, or an announcement that could be clearer), plus how you'd measure the improvement. Frame it as curiosity, not criticism.

    Q: Why are you leaving your current role so soon? A (if asked, given you joined Zensar this year): be honest and forward-looking — "PLUS has been great for deepening React and accessibility work; this role is the kind of product-company, craft-focused, large-scale consumer work I've been building toward, and it's a rare opportunity." Never criticize current or past employers.

    Q: Where do you see yourself in a few years? A: Growing into a technical leader for web experiences — owning architecture for a product area, mentoring, and shaping how AI-powered experiences are built responsibly. Connect it to the role.

    Apple culture notes#

    • Apple is known for secrecy — teams often can't share what they work on; don't push for details, and show you respect confidentiality (don't over-share client details either).

    • Collaboration across functions (design, product, QA, legal, localization) and attention to detail come up constantly.

    • Ownership: "directly responsible individual" culture — talk about things you personally owned end to end.

    14.3 Questions to ask your interviewers#

    Ask two per interviewer, tailored to their role. Good questions show you're already thinking like a team member.

    To engineers

    • "What does the frontend stack look like for the Store and product pages today — and what's the biggest technical challenge you're working on?"

    • "How do you balance pixel-perfect, animation-heavy experiences with performance on lower-end devices?"

    • "How does a change go from PR to production here — testing, review, rollout?"

    • "How are AI tools being used in the team's workflow, and are there AI-powered customer experiences on the roadmap?"

    To the hiring manager

    • "What would success look like for this role in the first 90 days and the first year?"

    • "What distinguishes the engineers who do best on this team?"

    • "How does the team work with design, Marcom, and localization partners day to day?"

    To design / cross-functional interviewers

    • "How do design and engineering review work together before launch?"

    • "How is accessibility handled — who owns it and when does it get reviewed?"

    Avoid: compensation in technical rounds, anything that's on the careers page, and asking about confidential unreleased products.

    Closing line for each round#

    "Thanks — I really enjoyed digging into [specific topic from the round]. It's exactly the kind of problem I want to be working on."

    14.4 Level up: behavioural rounds company by company#

    The same eight stories (14.1) can serve every company if you know which values each one listens for. Re-tell the same story with a different emphasis.

    Amazon: the 16 Leadership Principles and the Bar Raiser#

    Amazon builds every interview round around its Leadership Principles, and one interviewer is a trained Bar Raiser from outside the hiring team who can block the hire (source). Expect one or two principle questions even in technical rounds. Map your stories:

    Your story (14.1)Principles it provesEmphasis to add for Amazon
    1 · Performance winCustomer Obsession, Dive Deep, Deliver ResultsThe customer metric first ("attorneys waited X seconds"), then the data you dug into
    2 · Accessibility remediationInsist on the Highest Standards, OwnershipYou fixed root causes in shared components, beyond your ticket
    3 · Design systemInvent and Simplify, Think BigThe leverage: one change, many teams
    4 · Disagreement with designHave Backbone; Disagree and CommitSay how you committed fully once the decision was made
    5 · Production incidentOwnership, Bias for Action, Dive DeepSpeed of response, then the permanent fix
    6 · Mentoring / leadingHire and Develop the Best, Earn TrustA person who grew, specifically
    7 · MistakeEarn Trust, Learn and Be CuriousYou raised it yourself, early
    8 · AI-assisted deliveryLearn and Be Curious, FrugalityResults with fewer resources

    Amazon follow-ups go deep: "What exactly did you do?", "What data did you look at?", "What would you do differently?". Use "I", have numbers, and keep each story under two minutes before the follow-ups.

    Atlassian: the Values interview#

    Atlassian runs a separate Values interview that weighs as much as the technical rounds (source). Its five values are: Open company, no bullshit; Build with heart and balance; Don't #@!% the customer; Play, as a team; Be the change you seek. Prepare one story per value — stories 4 (open disagreement), 2 (customer impact), 6 (team play) and 3 (being the change) map cleanly.

    Netflix: judgment, candour, and "freedom and responsibility"#

    Netflix's culture memo centres on high performance with freedom and responsibility, context rather than control, and candid feedback. Expect: "Tell me about a decision you made without approval", "What's the hardest feedback you gave or received?", "What would you stop doing on your current team?" Answer with direct, specific judgment calls and what you learned; avoid stories where you waited for permission.

    Apple#

    Apple listens for craft, ownership and respect for confidentiality: stories about caring for details others missed, owning something end to end, and collaborating with design. Don't over-share client details from PLUS — describing your work while protecting confidentiality is itself a signal.

    Banks (JPMorgan, Goldman Sachs)#

    Expect controls-minded questions: "Tell me about a time you found a risk others missed", "How do you handle a production change in a regulated system?", "When did you push back on a deadline for quality or safety?" Your security remediation and accessibility compliance stories (ADA for AAA) fit well; mention audit trails, approvals and how you documented decisions.

    Uber, Walmart, Target, Tesla#

    These loops lean towards execution and impact: "the biggest thing you shipped", "a time you had to move fast under pressure", "a time you made a trade-off between speed and quality". Lead with outcome and scale, then the trade-off you made and how you protected users.

    Five questions that appear at almost every big company#

    1. Tell me about the most technically complex project you led. (Story 1 or 3, with architecture detail.)

    2. Tell me about a time you disagreed with a senior person. (Story 4.)

    3. Tell me about a failure. (Story 7 — own it, show the systemic fix.)

    4. How do you decide what not to build? (Prioritisation by customer impact and cost; give an example.)

    5. How do you raise the bar for your team? (Story 2, 3 or 6: something that changed how everyone works.)

    15. Company Playbook — how Netflix, Amazon, Uber, Atlassian, Walmart, Target, JPMorgan, Goldman and Tesla build and interview

    15.1 How top companies interview frontend engineers#

    Every company in this list hires senior UI engineers with the same core loop (coding, UI build, system design, behavioural), but each one weighs something different. Knowing the weighting tells you what to over-prepare.

    CompanyLoop shape (as reported)What they weigh mostWhat to over-prepare
    AppleTeam-run loops, 4–6 rounds, often coding + discussion in one hourCraft, fundamentals, accessibility, privacy instinctVanilla/React UI build with full a11y; "what does the browser do here"
    AmazonOnline assessment → phone screen → loop with a Bar Raiser who can veto16 Leadership Principles in every round, plus fundamentals2 LP stories per round; Amazon's front-end guide prefers Vanilla JavaScript in a LiveCode-style editor — confirm with your recruiter
    NetflixSmall number of deep rounds, heavy on judgment and past impactSenior-level independence, candour, "freedom and responsibility" cultureTrade-off reasoning; stories where you made a call without being told
    UberTwo technical rounds + front-end system designAsync JavaScript, UI components, performance on weak devices/networksPromise utilities with concurrency limits; real-time UI design
    AtlassianCraft rounds + a Values interview that carries real weightCollaboration, customer focus, its five valuesStories mapped to each value; complex UI like boards and drag-and-drop
    Walmart / TargetPractical JS/React coding + commerce system designPerformance and SEO at retail scale, peak-traffic resilience, accessibilityProduct page, cart and checkout design; SSR caching
    JPMorgan / Goldman SachsOnline assessment, technical rounds, "superday"Correctness, security, real-time data UIs, governanceTrading blotter / data-grid design, security answers, controls mindset
    TeslaLittle is published; candidates report practical, hands-on roundsSpeed of execution, fundamentalsClean, fast, working code; be ready to build without a framework

    Be honest where information is thin: little about Tesla's or Netflix's front-end loops is public, so prepare fundamentals deeply rather than memorising rumoured questions.

    15.2 Engineering facts you can cite, per company#
