    These are public, sourced facts. Quote the idea and the number; don't embellish. If asked "how do you know?", say "their engineering blog" or "it's a well-known case".

    Netflix#

    • Performance: Netflix removed client-side React from the logged-out landing page, keeping React on the server. Over 200 kB less JavaScript and more than a 50% drop in Time-to-Interactive; prefetching the sign-up flow's resources cut TTI on the next page by about 30%. Lesson: server-render, then hydrate only what needs interaction.

    • APIs: Netflix moved to federated GraphQL — domain teams own subgraphs ("Domain Graph Services") behind one gateway. It started with the Studio API and now powers the consumer apps. Their DGS framework is open source.

    • Design system: Hawkins is the design system across TV, mobile and web. Recent job posts describe building guardrails so AI agents build inside the system.

    • Testing: Netflix open-sourced Polly.js, which records real HTTP traffic and replays it in tests (stored as HAR files) so fixtures don't drift from the real API.

    • Accessibility: In NAD v. Netflix (2012 settlement), Netflix agreed to caption 100% of streaming content within two years and make captioned titles easy to find. The court had held the ADA applies to a web-only business.

    Uber#

    • Performance on weak networks: m.uber shipped a 50 kB (gzipped) core ride-request app, about 3 s to interactive on 2G. It used Preact instead of React, server rendering, service-worker caching, SVG instead of PNG (logo 7.4 kB → 500 bytes), no custom fonts, and loaded the map only when the device could handle it.

    • Real-time: Uber's push platform RAMEN moved from Server-Sent Events to gRPC bidirectional streaming over QUIC. The old design used a 4 s heartbeat, reconnected after 7 s of silence and batched acknowledgements every 30 s — too slow for 30-second driver offers. Bidirectional streams let acks arrive instantly.

    • Design system: Base Web is Uber's React implementation of its Base design language. Its "overrides" API lets teams restyle or replace any inner element; it uses Styletron atomic CSS and runs visual regression on every commit.

    • Security lesson: In 2022 an attacker with a contractor's stolen password spammed MFA push prompts until one was accepted. The case for phishing-resistant MFA (passkeys) and number matching.

    Spotify#

    • Design system: After the central team was disbanded, Spotify found 22 separate design systems. Encore replaced them as a "family of systems": one shared foundation of tokens, with local systems per product area.

    • Micro-frontends and migration: The old desktop client ran each squad's feature in its own iframe inside Chromium Embedded Framework. Spotify then converged desktop and web on the web player's codebase, rewriting it in TypeScript incrementally to avoid a big-bang rewrite.

    Atlassian#

    • Styling at scale: Atlassian standardised on Compiled, a build-time CSS-in-JS library that emits static CSS, and migrated its design-system components in late 2024. Reasons: less runtime JavaScript and compatibility with concurrent rendering and streaming SSR.

    • Governance by tooling: Its ESLint plugin enforces design-token use instead of hard-coded values. It also bans native drag-and-drop in favour of Pragmatic drag and drop, and enforces icon labels.

    • AI: Rovo (generally available October 2024) combines search, chat and roughly 20 agents across Jira and Confluence.

    Walmart#

    • Platform: Electrode, Walmart's React + Node.js platform, has powered walmart.com since 2017, with server rendering and a large-scale micro-frontend architecture. Two SSR tricks: render only above-the-fold content on the server, and cache rendered components like header and footer.

    • Business case for speed: Walmart found that each 1 second of improvement brought up to 2% more conversions, and each 100 ms up to 1% incremental revenue.

    • AI: Sparky (June 2025) is a generative-AI shopping assistant in the app that summarises reviews and recommends products.

    Amazon#

    • Design system: Cloudscape powers the AWS Management Console. Built in 2016 and open-sourced in 2022, it offers React components with light/dark themes and comfortable/compact density modes.

    • AI: Rufus (February 2024) is a shopping assistant grounded in the catalog, reviews and community Q&A, available on product pages.

    • Speed: "Every 100 ms of latency cost 1% in sales" is a widely cited Amazon finding. Cite it as widely cited, not as a current figure.

    Target#

    • Accessibility precedent: NFB v. Target (filed 2006, settled 2008) established that a retailer's website is covered by the ADA. Target set up a $6 million fund, and the NFB certified and monitored the site.

    • Security precedent: The 2013 breach began with credentials stolen from an HVAC vendor and exposed about 40 million cards — a lesson in third-party access and network segmentation.

    JPMorgan and Goldman Sachs#

    • Real-time data UIs: JPMorgan built Perspective for its trading business and open-sourced it through FINOS. It is a C++ streaming query engine compiled to WebAssembly, running in a Web Worker with Apache Arrow and a Web Component UI.

    • AI with controls: JPMorgan first restricted ChatGPT, then rolled out its own LLM Suite to about 200,000 employees. It stays cautious about customer-facing use. Goldman Sachs rolled out GS AI Assistant to about 10,000 employees in 2025.

    A security case every interviewer knows: British Airways#

    In 2018 attackers added 22 lines of skimming code to a Modernizr file on BA's site, sending payment data to a look-alike domain. About 429,000 people were affected and the UK regulator fined BA £20 million. This is why payment pages need script inventories, integrity checks and change detection.

    15.3 How to drop a company example into an answer#

    A company example is evidence, not decoration. Use this three-beat shape so it supports your answer instead of replacing it:

    1. Principle — "Ship less JavaScript on the first page; hydrate only what's interactive."

    2. Proof at scale — "Netflix did exactly this on its logged-out landing page: kept React on the server, dropped it on the client, and Time-to-Interactive fell by more than half."

    3. Your application — "On our case portal I'd apply the same idea: server-render the case list and lazy-load the editor only when someone opens a case."

    Rules that keep you credible

    • One example per answer, two at most. Three sounds rehearsed.

    • State numbers the way the source states them ("over 50%", "up to 2%"). Never round up.

    • Say "they published" or "it's a well-known case", not "I know they currently do X" — companies change stacks.

    • At Apple, prefer principles and public standards over naming competitors too often. One well-chosen industry example is enough.

    • If the interviewer worked at that company, they may correct you. Respond with curiosity: "Interesting — how did it evolve after that?"

    15.4 Company-style questions with model answers#

    Some of these are publicly reported for that company (marked reported); the rest are written in that company's style, built from what they publish about their engineering.

    Uber#

    Reported: implement mapAsyncLimit — map items through an async function with at most N in flight, results in input order. (source)

    js
    async function mapAsyncLimit(items, fn, limit = Infinity) {
  const results = new Array(items.length);
  let next = 0;
  async function worker() {
    while (next < items.length) {
      const i = next++;                       // claim an index synchronously: no two workers take the same one
      results[i] = await fn(items[i], i);     // first rejection rejects the whole call (like Promise.all)
    }
  }
  const workers = Array.from({ length: Math.min(limit, items.length) }, worker);
  await Promise.all(workers);
  return results;
}

    Follow-ups to pre-empt: cancellation (pass an AbortSignal and stop claiming new indices), "settle all" mode (catch per item), and progress callbacks for a UI.

    Reported: Grid Lights — a 3×3 grid where clicked cells light up; when all are lit, they turn off in reverse order of activation. Solution in React is in tab 12.5. The trick: keep the click order in an array (a stack), not just a set; deactivate with a timer popping one at a time; disable input while unwinding; clear timers on unmount.

    Style: "Design the rider web app for low-end Android phones on 2G." Answer with the m.uber playbook: server-render the request screen, a tiny core bundle (Preact-class runtime), code-split everything not needed to request a ride, service-worker cache for repeat visits, a feature switch that skips the live map on slow devices, and a push channel (SSE or WebSocket) for driver location with heartbeats and reconnect.

    Amazon#

    Reported: build an Accordion, a contact form, a signup form. (source) They are simple on purpose; the bar is the details: button[aria-expanded] inside headings, aria-controls, labels, error messages linked with aria-describedby, focus moved to an error summary on submit, no layout shift. Amazon's guide says Vanilla JavaScript is preferred, so be able to do both (vanilla in tab 1.3 and the DOM examples; React in tab 12).

    Every round adds a Leadership Principle question. Typical pairings: "Tell me about a time you had to deliver with incomplete data" (Bias for Action), "a time you disagreed with your manager" (Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit), "a time you raised the bar on quality" (Insist on the Highest Standards). Tab 14.4 maps your stories to the principles.

    Atlassian#

    Style: "Design a Jira board with drag-and-drop for 5,000 issues and live updates from teammates." Key points: virtualized columns, optimistic moves with server reconciliation and conflict handling (the card was moved by someone else), real-time updates via WebSocket with per-card versioning, accessible drag-and-drop with a keyboard and menu alternative ("Move to → In Progress"), and design-system primitives. Mention Atlassian chose Pragmatic drag and drop and enforces it with lint rules.

    Values round: Expect "Tell me about a time you put the customer first even though it cost the team" and "a time you gave hard feedback openly". Atlassian's values are blunt by design — answer with real stakes and real mistakes.

    Netflix#

    Style: "When would you remove a framework from a page?" When the page is mostly content and the framework's cost (download, parse, hydrate) delays the first interaction more than its benefit. Netflix's landing page is the canonical example: server-render with React, ship near-zero client JS, then prefetch the framework for the next step.

    Style: "Design the TV browse experience." 10-foot UI: D-pad spatial navigation with remembered focus per row, very low memory and CPU budgets, image-heavy rows with aggressive virtualization and recycled nodes, preview playback started only after focus dwells, and accessibility (focus must be obvious from across the room, screen-reader support on TV platforms).

    JPMorgan / Goldman Sachs#

    Style: "Design a trading blotter that receives 10,000 price updates per second." You can't render 10k updates a second. Parse and aggregate in a Web Worker (binary or columnar format, like Perspective uses Apache Arrow), coalesce to the latest value per instrument, and push one batch per animation frame. Use a virtualized grid (canvas for extreme row counts), flash changes with CSS classes not React state per cell, and freeze updates while the user is hovering or selecting. Add entitlements (who can see which books), audit logging, and session timeouts.

    Style: "How would you roll out an internal AI assistant to bankers?" Entitlement-aware retrieval (users only see what they're allowed to), no customer data sent to external models without approval, full audit trails, PII redaction, and human review for anything client-facing — the same caution JPMorgan describes publicly.

    Walmart / Target#

    Style: "Black Friday: the product page must survive 20× traffic." Static shell and product content cached at the CDN with stale-while-revalidate, personalised parts (price for member, store pickup, cart) fetched separately, SSR component caching for repeated fragments (Walmart's Electrode approach), graceful degradation via feature flags (turn off recommendations first), a waiting room for checkout, and RUM alerts per region.

    Tesla#

    Style: "Build a vehicle configurator: model, paint, wheels, interior, with live price and a 3D or image preview." It's the Apple Store configurator problem (tab 11.2): selection in the URL, SKU derived from options, invalid combinations disabled with explanations, prices in integer minor units, and heavy preview assets loaded progressively with the first view prioritised.

    ← Previous14 · Behavioral & Apple FitNext →16 · Output Questions
    Topic 1655 min read20 diagrams

    16. Output Questions — predict the output, and input → output transformations

    16.1 How to use this tab#

    Two kinds of output questions come up in senior front-end loops:

    1. Predict the output — a snippet, and "what gets logged, in what order?" (16.2–16.7). These test whether you can run the engine in your head: the event loop, scope, this, coercion, and React's render cycle.

    2. Input → output — "given this object, write a function that returns that shape" (16.8–16.9). These test clean data manipulation under time pressure, with edge cases.

    Every answer here was executed, not guessed. JavaScript outputs come from Node.js 22 (V8, the same engine as Chrome; the behaviour shown is defined by the ECMAScript spec, so Safari and Firefox match unless noted). React outputs come from React 19.3 in development mode. Every transformation solution passed an automated test against the example shown.

    How to answer a predict-the-output question out loud#

    1. Cover the answer and write your prediction first. Then compare. The questions you get wrong are your study list.

    2. Narrate in phases, not line by line: "Synchronous code first: start, async start, end. Then the microtask queue in the order things were queued: p1, the await continuation, the queueMicrotask callback, then p2, which was queued by p1. Then the timer."

    3. Name the rule you're applying ("an async function runs synchronously until its first await", "let creates a new binding per loop iteration"). Interviewers grade the rule more than the final string.

    4. Flag environment differences when they exist (sloppy vs strict mode, Node vs browser, StrictMode in development). Saying "in a browser classic script this would differ because…" is a senior signal.

    5. Connect it to a real bug in one sentence: "This is why forEach with async callbacks doesn't wait — I've seen it fire 200 parallel API calls."

    How to answer an input → output question#

    1. Restate the input and output shape, and ask about edge cases: empty input, nulls, arrays inside objects, duplicate keys, ordering, mutation of the input.

    2. Say the complexity you're aiming for (usually one pass, O(n), with a Map or object index).

    3. Write a pure function that doesn't mutate the input, then run the example by hand.

    4. Mention production concerns: prototype pollution (__proto__ keys), very deep nesting (recursion limits), Dates and class instances, and stable key ordering.

    The same five beats every time: for a snippet, run the engine in phases and name the rule; for a transformation, pin the shape before writing a line.

    16.2 Event loop, promises and async/await#

    The one rule behind every question here: run all synchronous code, then drain the entire microtask queue (promise callbacks, await continuations, queueMicrotask) in the order tasks were queued, then take one macrotask (timer, I/O), then drain microtasks again. A microtask queued during the drain runs in the same drain.

    Keep this loop in your head for every question in this section: anything in the microtask box beats anything in the macrotask box, and a microtask queued during the drain still runs before the next timer.

    E1 · Mixed sync, promises, async function, timer ★★#

    js
    console.log('start');
setTimeout(() => console.log('timeout'), 0);
Promise.resolve()
  .then(() => console.log('p1'))
  .then(() => console.log('p2'));
(async () => {
  console.log('async start');
  await null;
  console.log('after await');
})();
queueMicrotask(() => console.log('micro'));
console.log('end');
    text
    start
async start
end
p1
after await
micro
p2
timeout

    Why: an async function runs synchronously up to its first await. Microtasks are queued in this order: p1, the await continuation, micro. p2 is only queued when p1 finishes, so it comes after micro. The timer is a macrotask and waits for the microtask queue to empty.

    E2 · The classic async1 / async2 question ★★#

    js
    async function async1() {
  console.log('async1 start');
  await async2();
  console.log('async1 end');
}
async function async2() {
  console.log('async2');
}
console.log('script start');
setTimeout(() => console.log('setTimeout'), 0);
async1();
new Promise(resolve => {
  console.log('promise1');
  resolve();
}).then(() => console.log('promise2'));
console.log('script end');
    text
    script start
async1 start
async2
promise1
script end
async1 end
promise2
setTimeout

    Why: async2() runs synchronously and returns an already-resolved promise; awaiting a native promise takes one tick, so async1 end is queued before promise2. The Promise executor (promise1) is synchronous. Older engines (before 2019) printed promise2 first — mention it if asked about "await optimisations".

    E3 · Returning a promise from an async function costs two extra ticks ★★★★#

    js
    async function a() { return Promise.resolve('a'); }
async function b() { return 'b'; }
a().then(console.log);
b().then(console.log);
Promise.resolve()
  .then(() => console.log('c'))
  .then(() => console.log('d'))
  .then(() => console.log('e'))
  .then(() => console.log('f'));
    text
    b
c
d
a
e
f

    Why: b resolves its promise with a plain value, so its .then fires on the first tick. a resolves its promise with another promise, which the spec handles via a PromiseResolveThenableJob (one tick) that then calls .then on the inner promise (another tick). So a lands two ticks later than b. The same applies to returning a promise from a .then callback. This is a staff-level question — very few candidates get it.

    E4 · A promise settles once; the executor keeps running ★★#

    js
    const p = new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
  console.log(1);
  resolve('ok');
  console.log(2);
  reject('no');
  throw new Error('ignored?');
});
p.then(v => console.log('then', v)).catch(e => console.log('catch', e));
console.log(3);
    text
    1
2
3
then ok

    Why: resolve doesn't stop execution. Once settled, later reject calls and even a thrown error are ignored (the throw is swallowed because the promise is already resolved).

    E5 · Non-function arguments to .then, and recovery ★★★#

    js
    Promise.resolve(1)
  .then(2)
  .then(Promise.resolve(3))
  .then(v => { console.log(v); return Promise.reject(new Error('boom')); })
  .then(() => console.log('skipped'))
  .catch(e => { console.log('caught', e.message); return 'recovered'; })
  .then(v => console.log(v));
    text
    1
caught boom
recovered

    Why: .then ignores arguments that aren't functions and passes the value through, so 1 survives two .thens. A rejection skips fulfilment handlers until a catch; a catch that returns a value puts the chain back into the fulfilled state.

    E6 · finally ordering ★★★★#

    js
    Promise.resolve('A')
  .finally(() => 'B')
  .then(v => console.log('1:', v));
Promise.reject('E')
  .finally(() => console.log('2: finally'))
  .catch(e => console.log('2: caught', e));
Promise.resolve('X')
  .finally(() => { throw 'F'; })
  .catch(e => console.log('3: caught', e));
    text
    2: finally
3: caught F
1: A
2: caught E

    Why: three rules. (1) finally's return value is ignored — chain 1 still delivers 'A'. (2) A throw inside finally replaces the result immediately, so chain 3 settles fastest. (3) Otherwise finally waits on a promise wrapping its callback's result before passing the original value or reason through, which costs extra ticks — so 1: A and 2: caught E come last.

    E7 · Promise combinators with timers ★★★#

    js
    const wait = (ms, v, fail) =>
  new Promise((res, rej) => setTimeout(() => (fail ? rej(v) : res(v)), ms));

Promise.race([wait(30, 'slow'), wait(10, 'fast-fail', true)])
  .then(v => console.log('race:', v), e => console.log('race rejected:', e));
Promise.any([wait(10, 'x', true), wait(20, 'y')])
  .then(v => console.log('any:', v));
Promise.all([wait(5, 'a'), wait(15, 'b', true), wait(25, 'c')])
  .catch(e => console.log('all rejected:', e));
Promise.allSettled([wait(1, 'ok'), wait(2, 'bad', true)])
  .then(r => console.log('allSettled:', r.map(x => x.status).join(',')));
    text
    allSettled: fulfilled,rejected
race rejected: fast-fail
all rejected: b
any: y

    Why: race settles with the first to settle (even a rejection); any waits for the first fulfilment (rejects only if all reject, with an AggregateError); all rejects on the first rejection (15 ms) without waiting for c; allSettled never rejects. Output order follows the timers: 2, 10, 15, 20 ms.

    E8 · forEach with an async callback doesn't wait ★★★#

    js
    const sleep = (ms, v) => new Promise(r => setTimeout(() => r(v), ms));
async function run() {
  [3, 1, 2].forEach(async n => {
    await sleep(n * 10);
    console.log('forEach', n);
  });
  console.log('after forEach');
  for (const n of [3, 1, 2]) {
    await sleep(n * 100);
    console.log('for-of', n);
  }
  console.log('done');
}
run();
    text
    after forEach
forEach 1
forEach 2
forEach 3
for-of 3
for-of 1
for-of 2
done

    Why: forEach ignores the returned promises, so all three run in parallel and finish in timer order. for…of with await runs sequentially in array order. The real-world version: use for…of for sequential work, Promise.all(arr.map(...)) for parallel, and a concurrency-limited pool (tab 1.3) for "parallel but at most N".

    E9 · Microtasks queued inside a timer ★★★#

    js
    setTimeout(() => {
  console.log('t1');
  Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log('micro inside t1'));
}, 0);
setTimeout(() => console.log('t2'), 0);
Promise.resolve().then(() => {
  console.log('p1');
  setTimeout(() => console.log('t3'), 0);
});
console.log('sync');
    text
    sync
p1
t1
micro inside t1
t2
t3

    Why: after each macrotask the microtask queue drains, so micro inside t1 runs before t2. t3 was scheduled later than t1 and t2, so it runs last. (Node.js before v11 batched timers and printed t2 before the microtask; modern Node matches browsers.)

    E10 · try/catch can't catch an un-awaited rejection ★★★#

    js
    async function f() { throw new Error('nope'); }
try {
  f();
} catch (e) {
  console.log('sync catch');
}
f().catch(e => console.log('promise catch:', e.message));
(async () => {
  try { await f(); } catch (e) { console.log('await catch:', e.message); }
})();
console.log('end of script');
    text
    end of script
promise catch: nope
await catch: nope
Uncaught (in promise) Error: nope

    Why: an async function never throws synchronously; it returns a rejected promise, so the first try/catch sees nothing and the rejection becomes unhandled (reported after the microtask queue drains; in Node 15+ an unhandled rejection crashes the process by default). Only .catch or await inside try catches it.

    E11 · return vs return await inside try ★★★★#

    js
    async function fail() { throw new Error('x'); }
async function noAwait() {
  try { return fail(); } catch { return 'caught'; }
}
async function withAwait() {
  try { return await fail(); } catch { return 'caught'; }
}
noAwait().then(v => console.log('noAwait:', v), e => console.log('noAwait rejected:', e.message));
withAwait().then(v => console.log('withAwait:', v));
    text
    withAwait: caught
noAwait rejected: x

    Why: return fail() returns the promise before it rejects, so the try block has already exited and its catch never runs. return await waits inside the try. It also settles faster here (no promise-resolves-with-promise ticks, see E3). Rule: always return await inside try/catch.

    E12 · Awaiting a thenable ★★★#

    js
    const thenable = {
  then(resolve) {
    console.log('then called');
    resolve(42);
  },
};
(async () => {
  const v = await thenable;
  console.log('value', v);
})();
Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log('other microtask'));
console.log('sync');
    text
    sync
then called
other microtask
value 42

    Why: await treats any object with a then method as a promise. Its then is called in a microtask (not synchronously), and the value arrives one tick after that — which is how the other microtask slips in between. This is also why an object with an accidental then property breaks await.

    E13 · Sequential awaits on already-running promises ★★★#

    js
    const results = [];
const p1 = new Promise(r => setTimeout(() => { results.push('p1'); r('p1'); }, 20));
const p2 = new Promise(r => setTimeout(() => { results.push('p2'); r('p2'); }, 10));
(async () => {
  const a = await p1;
  const b = await p2;
  console.log(a, b, results.join(' > '));
})();
(async () => {
  const [x, y] = await Promise.all([p1, p2]);
  console.log(x, y);
})();
    text
    p1 p2 p2 > p1
p1 p2

    Why: promises start when created, not when awaited, so both timers run in parallel (p2 finishes first) and the sequential version takes 20 ms, not 30. The trap in real code is creating the promise inside the await (await fetchA(); await fetchB();), which really is sequential.

    16.3 Closures, scope and hoisting#

    C1 · var vs let in loops with timers ★★#

    js
    for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++) setTimeout(() => console.log('var', i), 0);
for (let j = 0; j < 3; j++) setTimeout(() => console.log('let', j), 0);
for (var k = 0; k < 3; k++) setTimeout(((k) => () => console.log('iife', k))(k), 0);
    text
    var 3
var 3
var 3
let 0
let 1
let 2
iife 0
iife 1
iife 2

    Why: var is one function-scoped binding shared by all callbacks, read after the loop ends. let creates a fresh binding per iteration. The IIFE copies the current value into a new parameter — the pre-ES2015 fix.

    The callbacks read the variable when they run, after the loop has finished. With var they all point at one binding; with let each points at its own.

    C2 · Hoisting of declarations vs expressions ★★#

    js
    console.log(typeof foo, typeof bar, typeof baz);
var foo = 1;
function bar() {}
var baz = function () {};
console.log(typeof foo, typeof bar, typeof baz);
    text
    undefined function undefined
number function function

    Why: function declarations are hoisted with their body; var declarations are hoisted but initialised to undefined, so a function expression assigned to a var isn't callable until that line runs.

    C3 · A function and a var with the same name ★★★#

    js
    var a = 1;
function a() {}
console.log(typeof a);

function b() {}
var b;
console.log(typeof b);
    text
    number
function

    Why: function declarations are hoisted first; a later var a declaration is ignored, but the assignment a = 1 runs at its line and overwrites the function. var b; without an assignment changes nothing.

    C4 · The temporal dead zone ★★★#

    js
    let x = 'outer';
function f() {
  console.log(x);
  let x = 'inner';
}
try {
  f();
} catch (e) {
  console.log(e.constructor.name + ': ' + e.message);
}
    text
    ReferenceError: Cannot access 'x' before initialization

    Why: the inner let x is hoisted to the top of f's scope and shadows the outer x, but stays uninitialised until its line. It doesn't fall back to the outer variable.

    The inner declaration claims the name for the whole function, so the read lands in the dead zone instead of falling back to the outer x.

    C5 · Function declarations inside blocks (sloppy mode) ★★★★#

    js
    console.log(typeof g);
if (true) {
  function g() { return 1; }
}
console.log(typeof g);
    text
    undefined
function

    Why: a legacy web-compatibility rule (Annex B of the spec): in sloppy mode, a function declared in a block also creates a function-scoped var g (initially undefined) that gets the function when the block runs. In strict mode and ES modules, g is block-scoped: the second line prints `undefined` again, and calling `g()` after the block throws ReferenceError. Good answer: "never declare functions in blocks; use const g = () => …".

    C6 · Closures capture variables, not values ★★★#

    js
    function createStore() {
  let state = { n: 0 };
  const snapshotAtCreation = state;
  return {
    set(v) { state = { n: v }; },
    getState: () => state,
    snapshotAtCreation,
  };
}
const s = createStore();
s.set(5);
console.log(s.getState().n, s.snapshotAtCreation.n);
    text
    5 0

    Why: getState closes over the variable state, so it sees the new object. snapshotAtCreation copied the reference at creation time. This is the same mechanism behind React stale closures (16.7, R8) and why external stores expose a getSnapshot function.

    C7 · Parameter scope vs body scope ★★★★★#

    js
    var x = 1;
function f(x, y = () => x) {
  var x = 2;
  return [x, y()];
}
console.log(f(3));
    text
    [ 2, 3 ]

    Why: when a function has default parameters, its parameters live in their own scope, separate from the body. The default y closes over the parameter x (3). var x = 2 in the body creates a second variable, initialised from the parameter and then reassigned. Very few engineers know this one.

    C8 · Named function expressions ★★★★#

    js
    const fn = function fact(n) { return n <= 1 ? 1 : n * fact(n - 1); };
console.log(fn(4), typeof fact);
var b = function c() {
  c = 5;
  return typeof c;
};
console.log(b());
    text
    24 undefined
function

    Why: the name of a named function expression is only visible inside the function, and that binding is read-only: assigning to it is silently ignored in sloppy mode (and throws TypeError in strict mode).

    C9 · Mutating the loop variable inside a let loop ★★★★#

    js
    const fns = [];
for (let i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
  fns.push(() => i);
  i++;
}
console.log(fns.map(f => f()));
    text
    [ 1, 3, 5 ]

    Why: each iteration gets its own copy of i, but the body's i++ mutates that iteration's copy after the closure was created, and the copy is carried into the next iteration before the loop's own i++. Iteration 1 sees 0 → 1; iteration 2 starts at 2 → 3; iteration 3 starts at 4 → 5.

    C10 · arguments aliasing ★★★★#

    js
    function f(a) { arguments[0] = 99; return a; }
function g(a) { 'use strict'; arguments[0] = 99; return a; }
function h(a = 0) { arguments[0] = 99; return a; }
console.log(f(1), g(1), h(1));
    text
    99 1 1

    Why: only in sloppy-mode functions with simple parameter lists are arguments and named parameters linked. Strict mode, default values, rest or destructured parameters all break the link. Another reason to use rest parameters instead of arguments.

    C11 · Getters are evaluated at destructuring time ★★★#

    js
    function makeCounter() {
  let count = 0;
  return {
    inc: () => ++count,
    reset() { count = 0; },
    get value() { return count; },
  };
}
const c1 = makeCounter();
const c2 = makeCounter();
c1.inc(); c1.inc(); c2.inc();
const { value } = c1;
c1.inc();
console.log(c1.value, c2.value, value);
    text
    3 1 2

    Why: each call to makeCounter creates an independent closure. Destructuring a getter reads it once and stores the number — value is a copy, not a live binding. The same trap appears when destructuring from a store or a React ref.

    16.4 this, classes and prototypes#

    Use this decision path on every question below: the first yes decides what this is, so you never need to reason about the later rules.

    T1 · Method extraction, arrows in object literals ★★★#

    js
    'use strict';
const obj = {
  name: 'obj',
  regular() { return this?.name; },
  arrow: () => this?.name,
  nested() {
    const inner = function () { return this?.name; };
    const innerArrow = () => this.name;
    return [inner(), innerArrow()];
  },
};
const { regular } = obj;
console.log(obj.regular(), regular(), obj.arrow(), obj.nested());
    text
    obj undefined undefined [ undefined, 'obj' ]

    Why: this is set by how a function is called. Extracting regular loses the object (strict mode gives undefined). An arrow function has no own this; one defined in an object literal takes the module's this, not the object's. The inner arrow inherits nested's this. Without 'use strict' in a browser classic script, regular() would read window.name — usually '', a notorious silent bug.

    T2 · bind can't be re-bound, but new overrides it ★★★★#

    js
    function who() { return this.n; }
const b1 = who.bind({ n: 1 });
const b2 = b1.bind({ n: 2 });
console.log(b1(), b2(), b1.call({ n: 3 }));

function P(name) { this.name = name; }
const BoundP = P.bind({ name: 'ignored' });
const p = new BoundP('made');
console.log(p.name, p instanceof P);
    text
    1 1 1
made true

    Why: a bound function ignores later bind, call and apply for this (it can still receive extra arguments). Binding priority: new beats explicit binding, which beats implicit (obj.fn()), which beats default.

    T3 · Class field arrows vs prototype methods ★★★#

    js
    class A {
  x = 1;
  arrow = () => this.x;
  method() { return this.x; }
}
const a = new A();
const { arrow, method } = a;
console.log(arrow());
try { method(); } catch (e) { console.log(e.constructor.name); }
console.log(Object.keys(a), 'method' in a, Object.hasOwn(a, 'method'));
    text
    1
TypeError
[ 'x', 'arrow' ] true false

    Why: class fields are created per instance, so the arrow captures that instance. Methods live once on the prototype; class bodies are strict, so a detached call has this === undefined and throws. Trade-off to mention: arrow fields cost memory per instance and can't be overridden with super.

    T4 · Replacing a constructor's prototype ★★★★#

    js
    function Car() {}
Car.prototype.wheels = 4;
const c1 = new Car();
Car.prototype = { wheels: 6 };
const c2 = new Car();
console.log(c1.wheels, c2.wheels, c1 instanceof Car, c2 instanceof Car);
    text
    4 6 false true

    Why: an instance's prototype link is fixed when it's created. instanceof checks whether Car.prototype (the current object) is on the instance's chain — for c1 it isn't any more.

    T5 · Shared mutable state on the prototype ★★★#

    js
    function Team() {}
Team.prototype.members = [];
const t1 = new Team();
const t2 = new Team();
t1.members.push('a');
t2.members = ['b'];
t1.members.push('c');
console.log(t1.members, t2.members, Team.prototype.members);
    text
    [ 'a', 'c' ] [ 'b' ] [ 'a', 'c' ]

    Why: reading t1.members walks up to the shared prototype array, and push mutates it. Assigning t2.members creates an own property that shadows it. Same bug as a module-level array shared across server requests.

    Both instances link to the same prototype. Mutating through t1 changes the array everyone shares; assigning on t2 only hides it for t2.

    T6 · super and static with this ★★★#

    js
    class Base {
  static create() { return new this(); }
  who() { return 'base'; }
  describe() { return this.who(); }
}
class Child extends Base {
  who() { return 'child'; }
  describe() { return 'C:' + super.describe(); }
}
console.log(Child.create().describe(), Child.create() instanceof Child);
    text
    C:child true

    Why: in a static method this is the class it was called on (Child), so new this() builds a Child. super.describe() runs the parent's method with the same this, so this.who() still dispatches to Child.

    T7 · Constructors that return a value ★★★#

    js
    function F() { this.a = 1; return { b: 2 }; }
function G() { this.a = 1; return 5; }
console.log(new F(), new G());
    text
    { b: 2 } G { a: 1 }

    Why: if a constructor returns an object, new gives you that object and discards this. Returning a primitive is ignored.

    T8 · Base constructor calls an overridden method before fields exist ★★★★★#

    js
    class Base {
  constructor() { this.init(); }
  init() {}
}
class Child extends Base {
  value = 'set';
  init() { console.log('init sees', this.value); }
}
const c = new Child();
console.log('after', c.value);
    text
    init sees undefined
after set

    Why: a derived class's fields are initialised only after super() returns. The base constructor runs first and calls the child's override, which reads a field that doesn't exist yet. A real bug in component and SDK class hierarchies — never call overridable methods from a constructor.

    Steps 1 to 3 happen before the derived class has initialised any field, which is why the override sees undefined.

    T9 · Getters and methods inherited through Object.create ★★★#

    js
    const base = {
  greet() { return 'hi ' + this.name; },
  get shout() { return this.name.toUpperCase(); },
};
const child = Object.create(base);
child.name = 'sam';
const greet = child.greet;
console.log(child.greet(), child.shout, Object.keys(child), 'greet' in child);
console.log(greet.call({ name: 'kim' }));
    text
    hi sam SAM [ 'name' ] true
hi kim

    Why: inherited getters and methods run with this set to the object you accessed them on. Object.keys lists only own enumerable properties; in checks the whole chain.

    16.5 Coercion, equality, keys and copying#

    K1 · The + operator and unary plus ★★★#

    js
    console.log([[] + [], [] + {}, 1 + '2' - 1, '3' * '4', true + true, null + 1, undefined + 1, +'', +' 42 ', +'4px']);
    text
    [ '', '[object Object]', 11, 12, 2, 1, NaN, 0, 42, NaN ]

    Why: + concatenates if either operand becomes a string after ToPrimitive; arrays and objects become strings ('' and '[object Object]'). Other arithmetic operators convert to numbers. null → 0, undefined → NaN, '' → 0, whitespace is trimmed, '4px' → NaN (use parseFloat for that).

    K2 · Loose equality traps ★★★★#

    js
    console.log([null == 0, null >= 0, undefined == null, NaN === NaN, Object.is(NaN, NaN),
  Object.is(0, -0), [] == ![], '' == 0, '0' == false, [1] == 1, [1, 2] == '1,2']);
    text
    [ false, true, true, false, true, false, true, true, true, true, true ]

    Why: null only loosely equals undefined, but relational operators convert null to 0, so null >= 0 is true while null == 0 is false. ![] is false (objects are truthy), then [] == false → '' == 0 → true. Booleans convert to numbers before comparing. Use ===, plus x == null as the one deliberate exception.

    Walk any == question through these five steps. The trace on the right shows why [] == ![] is true even though it looks like a value compared with its own negation.

    K3 · Default sort and locale ordering ★★★#

    js
    console.log([10, 9, 1, 2, 100].sort());
console.log([3, undefined, 1, , 2].sort());
console.log(['b', 'B', 'a', 'é', 'e'].sort());
console.log(['b', 'B', 'a', 'é', 'e'].sort((x, y) => x.localeCompare(y)));
    text
    [ 1, 10, 100, 2, 9 ]
[ 1, 2, 3, undefined, <1 empty item> ]
[ 'B', 'a', 'b', 'e', 'é' ]
[ 'a', 'b', 'B', 'e', 'é' ]

    Why: default sort compares UTF-16 strings, so numbers sort lexically and uppercase comes before lowercase. undefined and holes always go last. For user-facing lists use localeCompare or Intl.Collator (faster for big lists), which follow the user's language rules.

    K4 · map(parseInt) and Math edge cases ★★★#

    js
    console.log(['1', '2', '3'].map(parseInt));
console.log(['10', '10', '10'].map(Number));
console.log(parseInt(0.0000005), parseInt('08'), parseInt('0x1F'), Math.max(), Math.min());
    text
    [ 1, NaN, NaN ]
[ 10, 10, 10 ]
5 8 31 -Infinity Infinity

    Why: map passes (value, index), so parseInt('2', 1) and parseInt('3', 2) use invalid radixes. 0.0000005 stringifies as '5e-7', so parseInt reads 5. Math.max() with no arguments returns the identity for max, -Infinity.

    K5 · Objects as object keys vs Map keys ★★★#

    js
    const a = {}, b = { id: 1 }, c = { id: 2 };
a[b] = 'B';
a[c] = 'C';
console.log(a[b], Object.keys(a));
const m = new Map([[b, 'B'], [c, 'C']]);
console.log(m.get(b), m.get({ id: 1 }));
    text
    C [ '[object Object]' ]
B undefined

    Why: plain-object keys are strings (or symbols), so both objects become '[object Object]' and collide. Map keys use identity (SameValueZero): b works, a new look-alike object doesn't.

    K6 · Object key order ★★★★#

    js
    const o = { b: 1, 2: 'two', a: 2, 1: 'one', '-1': 'neg', '01': 'zero-one', 1.5: 'x' };
console.log(Object.keys(o));
    text
    [ '1', '2', 'b', 'a', '-1', '01', '1.5' ]

    Why: integer-like keys (canonical non-negative integers) come first in ascending order, then other string keys in insertion order. '-1', '01' and '1.5' aren't canonical array indices. This is why sorting an object of IDs like { 10: …, 2: … } silently reorders — use a Map or an array when order matters.

    K7 · Spread vs JSON vs structuredClone ★★★#

    js
    const orig = { n: 1, nested: { m: 1 }, date: new Date(0), list: [1] };
const spread = { ...orig };
const viaJson = JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(orig));
const deep = structuredClone(orig);
spread.n = 99;
spread.nested.m = 2;
spread.list.push(2);
console.log(orig.n, orig.nested.m, orig.list, typeof viaJson.date, deep.date instanceof Date, deep.nested.m);
    text
    1 2 [ 1, 2 ] string true 1

    Why: spread is shallow: top-level primitives are copied, nested objects are shared. JSON loses Dates (they become strings), undefined, functions, Map and Set, and throws on cycles. structuredClone keeps Dates, Maps, Sets and cycles, but can't clone functions, DOM nodes or class prototypes.

    Read across a row to pick the right copy for the data you hold. Each cell was checked in Node 22.

    K8 · What JSON.stringify drops or changes ★★★#

    js
    console.log(JSON.stringify({
  a: undefined, b: () => 1, c: Symbol('s'), d: NaN, e: new Date(0),
  f: [undefined, () => 1], g: new Map([[1, 2]]), h: -0, i: Infinity,
}));
    text
    {"d":null,"e":"1970-01-01T00:00:00.000Z","f":[null,null],"g":{},"h":0,"i":null}

    Why: undefined, functions and symbols are dropped from objects but become null in arrays. NaN and Infinity become null. Dates call toJSON. Map serialises as {} — a common bug when caching or sending state. Also: BigInt throws a TypeError.

    K9 · typeof table ★★#

    js
    console.log(typeof null, typeof NaN, typeof [], typeof function () {}, typeof class {}, typeof Symbol(), typeof 10n, typeof undeclaredVar);
    text
    object number object function function symbol bigint undefined

    Why: typeof null === 'object' is a historic bug kept for compatibility. Classes are functions. typeof on an undeclared variable doesn't throw — but on a let in its temporal dead zone it does.

    K10 · valueOf, toString and Symbol.toPrimitive ★★★★#

    js
    const money = {
  valueOf() { return 42; },
  toString() { return 'forty-two'; },
};
console.log(money + 1, `${money}`, String(money), money > 41, [money] + '', money + '');
const smart = {
  [Symbol.toPrimitive](hint) { return hint === 'number' ? 1 : hint === 'string' ? 'str' : 'default'; },
};
console.log(+smart, `${smart}`, smart + '');
    text
    43 forty-two forty-two true forty-two 42
1 str default

    Why: + and comparisons use the "default"/"number" hint (valueOf first). Template literals and String() use the "string" hint (toString first). [money] + '' calls the array's toString, which joins elements with String(). Symbol.toPrimitive overrides both and receives the hint.

    K11 · Floating point and integer precision ★★★#

    js
    console.log(0.1 + 0.2 === 0.3, (0.1 + 0.2).toFixed(2), 9007199254740993, 2 ** 53 === 2 ** 53 + 1,
  (1.005).toFixed(2), [NaN].indexOf(NaN), [NaN].includes(NaN));
    text
    false 0.30 9007199254740992 true 1.00 -1 true

    Why: binary floating point can't represent 0.1 exactly, and integers above 2^53 lose precision (IDs from a Java or Go backend can silently change). 1.005 is actually stored as 1.00499…, so toFixed(2) rounds down — never round money with toFixed; use integer minor units. indexOf uses === (NaN never matches); includes uses SameValueZero.

    K12 · delete on arrays and setting length ★★★#

    js
    const arr = [1, 2, 3];
delete arr[1];
arr.length = 5;
console.log(arr, arr.length, arr.map(x => x * 2), Object.keys(arr));
    text
    [ 1, <1 empty item>, 3, <2 empty items> ] 5 [ 2, <1 empty item>, 6, <2 empty items> ] [ '0', '2' ]

    Why: delete leaves a hole rather than shifting elements. Array methods like map and forEach skip holes (but for…of and spread treat them as undefined). Holey arrays are also slower in V8.

    K13 · Object.freeze is shallow ★★#

    js
    'use strict';
const cfg = Object.freeze({ a: 1, nested: { b: 1 } });
cfg.nested.b = 2;
try { cfg.a = 2; } catch (e) { console.log(e.constructor.name); }
console.log(cfg.a, cfg.nested.b);
    text
    TypeError
1 2

    Why: freeze only protects the top level; nested objects stay mutable. In strict mode a write to a frozen property throws; in sloppy mode it fails silently.

    K14 · Increment evaluation order ★★★#

    js
    let i = 0;
i = i++ + ++i;
let j = 5;
j += j++;
console.log(i, j);
    text
    2 10

    Why: operands are evaluated left to right. i++ yields 0 (i becomes 1), ++i makes i 2 and yields 2, so 0 + 2 = 2. j += j++ reads j (5) first, then j++ yields 5, so 5 + 5 = 10 — the increment is overwritten.

    K15 · Mutating an array while iterating it ★★★★#

    js
    const nums = [1, 2, 3];
const out = nums.map((n, idx, all) => { all.push(n * 10); return n; });
console.log(out, nums.length);
const removed = [];
const list = [1, 2, 3, 4];
list.forEach((n, idx) => { if (n % 2 === 0) list.splice(idx, 1); removed.push(n); });
console.log(list, removed);
    text
    [ 1, 2, 3 ] 6
[ 1, 3 ] [ 1, 2, 4 ]

    Why: map, forEach and friends fix their length at the start, so pushed items aren't visited. splice shifts later items left, so the element right after a removed one is skipped (3 is never visited). Fix: iterate backwards, or filter into a new array.

    16.6 Generators, descriptors, Proxy, Symbols and control flow#

    G1 · Passing values into a generator ★★★★#

    js
    function* gen() {
  const x = yield 1;
  console.log('got', x);
  const y = yield x * 2;
  return x + y;
}
const it = gen();
console.log(it.next('ignored'), it.next(10), it.next(5), it.next());
    text
    got 10
{ value: 1, done: false } { value: 20, done: false } { value: 15, done: true } { value: undefined, done: true }

    Why: the first next() argument is discarded — nothing is waiting for it. Each later next(v) becomes the result of the paused yield. got 10 prints first because all four next calls are evaluated before console.log runs. The return value appears once with done: true.

    Blue arrows carry values into the generator, orange arrows carry results out. The value sent by next() lands in whichever yield is currently paused, so the first one has nowhere to go.

    G2 · Generator return values and early exit ★★★#

    js
    function* g() { yield 1; yield 2; return 3; }
console.log([...g()], Array.from(g()));
const it = g();
for (const v of it) { console.log('for-of', v); break; }
console.log(it.next());
    text
    [ 1, 2 ] [ 1, 2 ]
for-of 1
{ value: undefined, done: true }

    Why: spread and for…of ignore the return value. break calls the iterator's return(), which closes the generator, so a later next() reports done instead of 2. That's how finally blocks in generators clean up resources.

    G3 · Non-writable, non-enumerable properties ★★★#

    js
    const user = {};
Object.defineProperty(user, 'id', { value: 7 });
user.id = 9;
console.log(user.id, Object.keys(user), JSON.stringify(user), Object.getOwnPropertyNames(user));
    text
    7 [] {} [ 'id' ]

    Why: defineProperty defaults writable, enumerable and configurable to false. The write fails silently in sloppy mode (throws in strict). Non-enumerable properties are hidden from keys, spread and JSON but visible to getOwnPropertyNames.

    G4 · Proxy traps ★★★★#

    js
    const target = { a: 1 };
const p = new Proxy(target, {
  get(t, k, r) { return k in t ? Reflect.get(t, k, r) : `missing:${String(k)}`; },
  set(t, k, v) { t[k] = v * 2; return true; },
});
p.b = 5;
console.log(p.a, p.b, p.c, target.b, 'c' in p);
    text
    1 10 missing:c 10 false

    Why: writes go through set, which stored 10 on the real target. Reads of missing keys go through get. The in operator uses the has trap, which isn't defined, so it falls through to the target. This is how Vue and MobX reactivity intercept reads and writes.

    G5 · Symbol keys ★★★#

    js
    const s = Symbol('secret');
const o = { [s]: 1, visible: 2 };
console.log(Object.keys(o), JSON.stringify(o), Object.getOwnPropertySymbols(o).length,
  o[Symbol('secret')], Symbol.for('k') === Symbol.for('k'));
    text
    [ 'visible' ] {"visible":2} 1 undefined true

    Why: symbol keys are skipped by keys, for…in and JSON. Every Symbol() call is unique, even with the same description; Symbol.for uses a global registry, so it returns the same symbol for the same key.

    G6 · finally can override return ★★★★#

    js
    function f() {
  try { return 'try'; } finally { console.log('finally runs'); }
}
function g() {
  try { throw new Error('x'); } catch { return 'catch'; } finally { return 'finally'; }
}
console.log(f(), g());
    text
    finally runs
try finally

    Why: finally always runs, even after return. A return inside finally replaces the pending return value — or swallows a pending exception entirely. Lint rules (no-unsafe-finally) ban it.

    G7 · Labelled break and continue ★★★#

    js
    outer: for (let i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
  for (let j = 0; j < 3; j++) {
    if (j === 1) continue outer;
    if (i === 2) break outer;
    console.log(i, j);
  }
}
    text
    0 0
1 0

    Why: continue outer jumps to the next iteration of the outer loop as soon as j reaches 1; at i === 2, break outer exits both loops before anything is logged.

    G8 · Set and Map equality and order ★★★#

    js
    const set = new Set([1, '1', 1, NaN, NaN, 0, -0, {}, {}]);
console.log(set.size);
const map = new Map();
map.set('b', 1).set('a', 2).set('b', 3);
console.log([...map]);
    text
    6
[ [ 'b', 3 ], [ 'a', 2 ] ]

    Why: Set and Map use SameValueZero: NaN equals NaN, 0 equals -0, 1 and '1' differ, and two {} are different objects. Updating an existing Map key keeps its original insertion position.

    G9 · Mutating vs non-mutating array methods ★★★#

    js
    const a = [3, 1, 10, 2];
const b = a.sort();
const c = a.toSorted((x, y) => y - x);
console.log(a, b === a, c, a.at(-1), [1, 2, 3, 4].findLast(n => n % 2));
    text
    [ 1, 10, 2, 3 ] true [ 10, 3, 2, 1 ] 3 3

    Why: sort sorts in place (as strings by default) and returns the same array. ES2023 added non-mutating toSorted, toReversed, toSpliced and with — ideal for React state. at(-1) reads from the end; findLast searches backwards.

    G10 · || vs ?? and optional chaining ★★#

    js
    const cfg = { retries: 0, title: '', nested: null };
console.log(cfg.retries || 3, cfg.retries ?? 3, cfg.title || 'Untitled', cfg.title ?? 'Untitled',
  cfg.nested?.deep.deeper, cfg.missing?.());
    text
    3 0 Untitled  undefined undefined

    Why: || replaces every falsy value (0, ''), ?? only null and undefined (the empty title prints as an empty string). Optional chaining short-circuits the whole rest of the chain, so .deeper isn't evaluated; ?.() skips the call when the function is missing.

    G11 · Destructuring consumes iterators ★★★★#

    js
    const counter = {
  count: 0,
  [Symbol.iterator]: function* () { while (this.count < 3) yield ++this.count; },
};
const [first, ...rest] = counter;
console.log(first, rest, counter.count, [...counter]);
    text
    1 [ 2, 3 ] 3 []

    Why: array destructuring uses the iterator protocol, not indexes. The rest element drains the iterator, which mutated count to 3, so a second spread yields nothing. Destructuring a Set or a generator works the same way.

    G12 · Error causes and error types ★★#

    js
    const e = new Error('outer', { cause: new TypeError('inner') });
console.log(e.message, e.cause.constructor.name, e instanceof Error, e.cause instanceof Error);
try { null.x; } catch (err) { console.log(err.constructor.name); }
try { undefinedFn(); } catch (err) { console.log(err.constructor.name); }
    text
    outer TypeError true true
TypeError
ReferenceError

    Why: cause (ES2022) chains errors without losing the original — use it when wrapping API errors. Property access on null is a TypeError; an undeclared identifier is a ReferenceError.

    16.7 React: render, effect and update order#

    Verified with React 19.3 (development build) rendering into a DOM. Logs collected in an array, as an interviewer would show them.

    R1 · Mount order: render, layout effects, effects ★★★#

    jsx
    function Child() {
  log('Child render');
  useLayoutEffect(() => log('Child layout effect'), []);
  useEffect(() => log('Child effect'), []);
  return null;
}
function Parent() {
  log('Parent render');
  useLayoutEffect(() => log('Parent layout effect'), []);
  useEffect(() => log('Parent effect'), []);
  return <Child />;
}
    text
    Parent render
Child render
Child layout effect
Parent layout effect
Child effect
Parent effect

    Why: rendering goes top-down (parent calls its children). Effects run bottom-up, children first, so a parent's effect can rely on children being mounted. All layout effects run (synchronously, before paint) before any regular effect.

    This grid is the backbone for every React question below: numbers show the log order, rendering flows down from parent to child, and both kinds of effect flow back up.

    R2 · Batching and stale state in a handler ★★★#

    jsx
    function Counter() {
  const [count, setCount] = useState(0);
  log(`render count=${count}`);
  const onClick = () => {
    setCount(count + 1);
    setCount(count + 1);
    setCount(c => c + 1);
    log(`in handler count=${count}`);
  };
  // …button calls onClick once
}
    text
    render count=0
in handler count=0
render count=2

    Why: the three updates are batched into one render. count is a constant (0) for this render, so the first two both set 1; the updater then receives the queued value 1 and returns 2. Reading count after setCount still shows the old value.

    R3 · Effect cleanup order on update ★★★★#

    jsx
    function Child({ n }) {
  useEffect(() => { log(`Child effect ${n}`); return () => log(`Child cleanup ${n}`); }, [n]);
  return null;
}
function Parent() {
  const [n, setN] = useState(1);
  useEffect(() => { log(`Parent effect ${n}`); return () => log(`Parent cleanup ${n}`); }, [n]);
  return <Child n={n} />;
}
// mount, then setN(2)
    text
    Child effect 1
Parent effect 1
--- update ---
Child cleanup 1
Parent cleanup 1
Child effect 2
Parent effect 2

    Why: on an update React runs all cleanups of changed effects first, then all new effects, each child-first. A cleanup always sees the values from its own render (1), not the new ones. (On unmount React 19 ran Parent cleanup 2 before Child cleanup 2 — never depend on cleanup order across components.)

    All cleanups from the previous render finish before any new effect starts, and each cleanup still sees the values from its own render.

    R4 · StrictMode in development ★★★#

    jsx
    function App() {
  log('render');
  useEffect(() => { log('effect'); return () => log('cleanup'); }, []);
  return null;
}
root.render(<StrictMode><App /></StrictMode>);
    text
    render
render
effect
cleanup
effect

    Why: in development, StrictMode renders twice to catch impure render logic, then mounts, unmounts and remounts effects to prove cleanups work. Production prints render and effect once. If something breaks (double API call, duplicated subscription), the cleanup is wrong — the fix is never to remove StrictMode.

    R5 · What does and doesn't cause a re-render ★★★#

    jsx
    function App() {
  const [n, setN] = useState(0);
  const [obj, setObj] = useState({ a: 1 });
  const ref = useRef(0);
  log(`render n=${n} ref=${ref.current}`);
  // later, one at a time: setN(0); ref.current++; setObj({ a: 1 });
}
    text
    render n=0 ref=0
-- setN(0)
-- ref.current++
-- setObj({ a: 1 })
render n=0 ref=1

    Why: setting state to the same value (Object.is) bails out. Mutating a ref never re-renders — the new value only shows up when something else re-renders. A new object with the same contents is a different value, so it re-renders. (React may occasionally do one extra render before bailing out on an equal value; never rely on render counts.)

    R6 · Changing key remounts ★★★#

    jsx
    function Panel({ id }) {
  const [local] = useState(() => { log(`init state for ${id}`); return id; });
  useEffect(() => { log(`mount ${id}`); return () => log(`unmount ${id}`); }, []);
  log(`render id=${id} local=${local}`);
  return null;
}
// <Panel id="A" key="A" />  →  <Panel id="B" key="A" />  →  <Panel id="C" key="C" />
    text
    init state for A
render id=A local=A
mount A
-- same key, new prop
render id=B local=A
-- new key
init state for C
render id=C local=C
unmount A
mount C

    Why: with the same key, React keeps the instance: state isn't re-initialised, so local stays 'A' even though the prop changed (the classic "state derived from props doesn't update" bug). A new key unmounts the old instance and mounts a fresh one with fresh state. Note the new component renders before the old one's cleanup runs.

    R7 · setState inside an effect ★★★#

    jsx
    function App() {
  const [ready, setReady] = useState(false);
  log(`render ready=${ready}`);
  useLayoutEffect(() => log(`layout ready=${ready}`), [ready]);
  useEffect(() => {
    log(`effect ready=${ready}`);
    if (!ready) setReady(true);
  }, [ready]);
  return null;
}
    text
    render ready=false
layout ready=false
effect ready=false
render ready=true
layout ready=true
effect ready=true

    Why: the effect runs after the first commit, sets state, and causes a full second render–commit cycle. The user may see one frame of ready=false. If the value can be computed during render, compute it there instead.

    R8 · Stale closure in an interval ★★★★#

    jsx
    function Ticker() {
  const [count, setCount] = useState(0);
  useEffect(() => {
    const id = setInterval(() => setCount(count + 1), 10);
    return () => clearInterval(id);
  }, []);                       // count is captured from the first render
  log(`render count=${count}`);
  return null;
}
    text
    render count=0
render count=1
render count=1

    It then stays at 1 forever, while the same component using setCount(c => c + 1) printed 0, 1, 2… in the same run.

    Why: the effect ran once, so its interval closes over count from the first render (0) and keeps setting 1. Fixes: the functional updater, a ref that holds the latest value, or listing count in the dependencies (which restarts the interval each time). The second count=1 render is React rendering once more before it notices the value didn't change.

    The closure is created once, during render 1, so every tick computes 0 + 1. After the second render React sees the same value and stops rendering.

    16.8 Input → output: objects and arrays#

    Each problem shows the input, the exact expected output, and a solution that passed an automated test. Shared helpers used below:

    js
    const isPlain = v => Object.prototype.toString.call(v) === '[object Object]';
const BLOCKED = new Set(['__proto__', 'constructor', 'prototype']); // prototype-pollution guard

    H1 · Flatten a nested object to dot paths ★★★#

    js
    // input
{ user: { name: 'Asha', tags: ['admin', { level: 2 }], meta: {} }, active: true, joined: null }
// output
{ 'user.name': 'Asha', 'user.tags.0': 'admin', 'user.tags.1.level': 2, 'user.meta': {}, active: true, joined: null }
    js
    function flatten(obj, prefix = '', out = {}) {
  for (const [k, v] of Object.entries(obj)) {
    const key = prefix ? `${prefix}.${k}` : k;
    if (v !== null && typeof v === 'object' && !(v instanceof Date) && Object.keys(v).length) flatten(v, key, out);
    else out[key] = v;                    // primitives, null, Dates and empty containers are leaves
  }
  return out;
}

    Edge cases to say out loud: null is typeof 'object'; empty objects and arrays must survive as leaves or they vanish; Dates are objects with no own keys; very deep input could overflow the stack (use an explicit stack if depth is unbounded).

    H2 · Unflatten dot paths back into objects and arrays ★★★#

    js
    // input
{ 'a.b': 1, 'a.c.0': 'x', 'a.c.1.d': 2, e: 3 }
// output
{ a: { b: 1, c: ['x', { d: 2 }] }, e: 3 }
    js
    function unflatten(flat) {
  const root = {};
  for (const [path, value] of Object.entries(flat)) {
    const keys = path.split('.');
    let node = root;
    keys.forEach((k, i) => {
      if (i === keys.length - 1) { node[k] = value; return; }
      if (node[k] === undefined) node[k] = /^\d+$/.test(keys[i + 1]) ? [] : {}; // next key numeric → array
      node = node[k];
    });
  }
  return root;
}

    H1 and H2 are inverses: each dashed line pairs a leaf with its path key. Draw this on the whiteboard before coding either one.

    H3 · Nested group-by with totals ★★★#

    js
    // input
const orders = [
  { id: 1, status: 'paid', customer: 'ann', amount: 120 },
  { id: 2, status: 'pending', customer: 'bob', amount: 80 },
  { id: 3, status: 'paid', customer: 'bob', amount: 50 },
  { id: 4, status: 'paid', customer: 'ann', amount: 30 },
];
groupTotals(orders, ['status', 'customer'], 'amount');
// output
{ paid: { ann: 150, bob: 50 }, pending: { bob: 80 } }
    js
    function groupTotals(rows, keys, valueKey) {
  const out = {};
  for (const row of rows) {
    let node = out;
    keys.forEach((k, i) => {
      const g = row[k];
      if (i === keys.length - 1) node[g] = (node[g] ?? 0) + row[valueKey];
      else node = node[g] ??= {};
    });
  }
  return out;
}

    Mention Object.groupBy(items, fn) (ES2024) for one level, and that money should be summed in integer minor units.

    H4 · Deep merge (objects merge, arrays replace, inputs untouched) ★★★★#

    js
    // input
const defaults = { theme: { mode: 'light', accent: 'blue' }, features: ['a'], retries: 3 };
const user = { theme: { mode: 'dark' }, features: ['b'], retries: 0 };
deepMerge(defaults, user);
// output
{ theme: { mode: 'dark', accent: 'blue' }, features: ['b'], retries: 0 }
    js
    function deepMerge(...sources) {
  const out = {};
  for (const src of sources) {
    for (const [k, v] of Object.entries(src ?? {})) {
      if (BLOCKED.has(k)) continue;                       // blocks {"__proto__": {...}} from JSON
      out[k] = isPlain(v) && isPlain(out[k]) ? deepMerge(out[k], v)
             : isPlain(v) ? deepMerge(v)                  // copy, so output never aliases inputs
             : v;
    }
  }
  return out;
}

    The traps interviewers look for: retries: 0 must win (don't use ||), arrays are replaced not merged (say so and offer an option), and the defaults object must not be mutated.

    Each result cell is coloured by the input it came from. The two rows interviewers check are features (replaced, not concatenated) and retries (0 must survive).

    H5 · Deep diff with paths ★★★★#

    js
    // input
diff(
  { name: 'Asha', address: { city: 'Kolkata', pin: '700001' }, tags: ['a'] },
  { name: 'Asha', address: { city: 'Pune' }, tags: ['a', 'b'], phone: '99' },
);
// output
{
  added:   { phone: '99' },
  removed: { 'address.pin': '700001' },
  changed: { 'address.city': { from: 'Kolkata', to: 'Pune' }, tags: { from: ['a'], to: ['a', 'b'] } },
}
    js
    function diff(a, b, path = '', out = { added: {}, removed: {}, changed: {} }) {
  const keys = new Set([...Object.keys(a ?? {}), ...Object.keys(b ?? {})]);
  for (const k of keys) {
    const p = path ? `${path}.${k}` : k;
    const inA = a && Object.hasOwn(a, k), inB = b && Object.hasOwn(b, k);
    if (!inA) out.added[p] = b[k];
    else if (!inB) out.removed[p] = a[k];
    else if (isPlain(a[k]) && isPlain(b[k])) diff(a[k], b[k], p, out);
    else if (!Object.is(a[k], b[k]) && JSON.stringify(a[k]) !== JSON.stringify(b[k])) out.changed[p] = { from: a[k], to: b[k] };
  }
  return out;
}

    Real use: audit logs ("what changed on this case"), form dirty-checking, and sending PATCH payloads.

    H6 · Deep equality ★★★#

    js
    // input → output
deepEqual({ a: [1, { b: NaN }], d: new Date(0) }, { d: new Date(0), a: [1, { b: NaN }] }) // true
deepEqual([1, 2], { 0: 1, 1: 2 })                                                     // false
deepEqual({ a: undefined }, {})                                                        // false
    js
    function deepEqual(a, b) {
  if (Object.is(a, b)) return true;                                   // NaN equals NaN
  if (a instanceof Date && b instanceof Date) return a.getTime() === b.getTime();
  if (typeof a !== 'object' || typeof b !== 'object' || !a || !b) return false;
  if (Array.isArray(a) !== Array.isArray(b)) return false;
  const ka = Object.keys(a), kb = Object.keys(b);
  return ka.length === kb.length && ka.every(k => Object.hasOwn(b, k) && deepEqual(a[k], b[k]));
}

    Follow-ups: cycles (track visited pairs in a WeakMap), Map/Set support, and whether { a: undefined } should equal {} (it shouldn't by default — key presence matters).

    H7 · Flat list ↔ tree in O(n) ★★★★#

    js
    // input (parents can appear after children)
const rows = [
  { id: 1, parentId: null, name: 'Root' },
  { id: 3, parentId: 2, name: 'Grandchild' },
  { id: 2, parentId: 1, name: 'Child A' },
  { id: 4, parentId: 1, name: 'Child B' },
];
// output of listToTree (shown by name)
[{ name: 'Root', children: [
  { name: 'Child A', children: [{ name: 'Grandchild', children: [] }] },
  { name: 'Child B', children: [] },
] }]
// output of treeToList, rendered with indentation
['Root', '  Child A', '    Grandchild', '  Child B']
    js
    function listToTree(list) {
  const byId = new Map(list.map(r => [r.id, { ...r, children: [] }])); // pass 1: index
  const roots = [];
  for (const node of byId.values()) {                                   // pass 2: attach
    const parent = byId.get(node.parentId);
    (parent ? parent.children : roots).push(node);
  }
  return roots;
}
function treeToList(nodes, depth = 0) {
  return nodes.flatMap(n => [{ name: n.name, depth }, ...treeToList(n.children, depth + 1)]);
}

    The naive answer (for each node, scan for its children) is O(n²); interviewers expect the two-pass map. Orphans with a missing parent become roots here — ask whether to drop or flag them.

    The Map is what makes it linear: attaching a child is one lookup instead of a scan of the whole list.

    H8 · get / set by path string with brackets ★★★#

    js
    // input → output
const o = set({}, 'cart.items[0].sku', 'IP17');
set(o, 'cart.items[0].qty', 2);
// o                                  → { cart: { items: [{ sku: 'IP17', qty: 2 }] } }
get(o, 'cart.items[0].qty')          // 2
get(o, 'cart.items[3].qty', 0)       // 0
get(o, 'cart.total.amount', null)    // null
    js
    const toPath = p => p.replace(/\[(\w+)\]/g, '.$1').split('.').filter(Boolean);
function get(obj, path, fallback) {
  const v = toPath(path).reduce((o, k) => (o == null ? undefined : o[k]), obj);
  return v === undefined ? fallback : v;
}
function set(obj, path, value) {
  const keys = toPath(path);
  keys.reduce((o, k, i) => {
    if (BLOCKED.has(k)) throw new Error('unsafe key');
    if (i === keys.length - 1) o[k] = value;
    else if (o[k] == null) o[k] = /^\d+$/.test(keys[i + 1]) ? [] : {};
    return o[k];
  }, obj);
  return obj;
}

    H9 · Normalize a nested API response ★★★#

    js
    // input
[
  { id: 'o1', customer: { id: 'c1', name: 'Ann' }, items: [{ sku: 's1', qty: 1 }] },
  { id: 'o2', customer: { id: 'c1', name: 'Ann' }, items: [{ sku: 's2', qty: 3 }] },
  { id: 'o3', customer: { id: 'c2', name: 'Bob' }, items: [] },
]
// output
{
  orders: {
    byId: {
      o1: { id: 'o1', items: [{ sku: 's1', qty: 1 }], customerId: 'c1' },
      o2: { id: 'o2', items: [{ sku: 's2', qty: 3 }], customerId: 'c1' },
      o3: { id: 'o3', items: [], customerId: 'c2' },
    },
    allIds: ['o1', 'o2', 'o3'],
  },
  customers: { byId: { c1: { id: 'c1', name: 'Ann' }, c2: { id: 'c2', name: 'Bob' } }, allIds: ['c1', 'c2'] },
}
    js
    function normalize(list) {
  const out = { orders: { byId: {}, allIds: [] }, customers: { byId: {}, allIds: [] } };
  for (const { customer, ...order } of list) {
    out.orders.byId[order.id] = { ...order, customerId: customer.id };
    out.orders.allIds.push(order.id);
    if (!out.customers.byId[customer.id]) {
      out.customers.byId[customer.id] = customer;
      out.customers.allIds.push(customer.id);
    }
  }
  return out;
}

    Why it matters: updating customer c1 now changes one record instead of every order — the Redux / Apollo normalized-cache idea.

    H10 · Aggregate, sort, top N plus "other" ★★★#

    js
    // input
const txns = [
  { cat: 'food', amt: 1250 }, { cat: 'travel', amt: 5400 }, { cat: 'food', amt: 300 },
  { cat: 'bills', amt: 2200 }, { cat: 'fun', amt: 450 }, { cat: 'gifts', amt: 150 },
];
topCategories(txns, 3);
// output
[{ cat: 'travel', total: 5400 }, { cat: 'bills', total: 2200 }, { cat: 'food', total: 1550 }, { cat: 'other', total: 600 }]
    js
    function topCategories(list, n) {
  const totals = list.reduce((m, { cat, amt }) => m.set(cat, (m.get(cat) ?? 0) + amt), new Map());
  const sorted = [...totals].sort((a, b) => b[1] - a[1]);
  const top = sorted.slice(0, n).map(([cat, total]) => ({ cat, total }));
  const rest = sorted.slice(n).reduce((s, [, t]) => s + t, 0);
  return rest ? [...top, { cat: 'other', total: rest }] : top;
}

    This is the data behind every "spend by category" donut chart in a banking app.

    H11 · Convert API keys from snake_case to camelCase, deeply ★★★#

    js
    // input
{ user_id: 7, created_at: new Date(0), line_items: [{ unit_price: 10, sku_code: 'a_b' }] }
// output (values untouched, Dates kept as Dates)
{ userId: 7, createdAt: new Date(0), lineItems: [{ unitPrice: 10, skuCode: 'a_b' }] }
    js
    const camel = s => s.replace(/_([a-z0-9])/g, (_, c) => c.toUpperCase());
function camelizeKeys(v) {
  if (Array.isArray(v)) return v.map(camelizeKeys);
  if (isPlain(v)) return Object.fromEntries(Object.entries(v).map(([k, val]) => [camel(k), camelizeKeys(val)]));
  return v;                                   // Dates, primitives, class instances pass through
}

    H12 · Deep compact: remove empty values ★★★★#

    js
    // input
{ a: 1, b: null, c: '', d: { e: undefined, f: [] }, g: [0, null, false, ''], h: { i: { j: 'x' } } }
// output (0 and false are kept — they're real values)
{ a: 1, g: [0, false], h: { i: { j: 'x' } } }
    js
    function compact(v) {
  if (Array.isArray(v)) {
    const arr = v.map(compact).filter(x => x !== undefined);
    return arr.length ? arr : undefined;
  }
  if (isPlain(v)) {
    const entries = Object.entries(v).map(([k, x]) => [k, compact(x)]).filter(([, x]) => x !== undefined);
    return entries.length ? Object.fromEntries(entries) : undefined;
  }
  return v === null || v === undefined || v === '' ? undefined : v;
}

    The trap is filter(Boolean), which wrongly drops 0 and false. Note that d disappears because it becomes empty after cleaning.

    H13 · Pivot long rows into wide rows ★★★#

    js
    // input
[
  { date: '2026-11-01', metric: 'cpu', value: 40 },
  { date: '2026-11-01', metric: 'mem', value: 70 },
  { date: '2026-11-02', metric: 'cpu', value: 55 },
]
// output
[{ date: '2026-11-01', cpu: 40, mem: 70 }, { date: '2026-11-02', cpu: 55, mem: null }]
    js
    function pivot(list, rowKey, colKey, valKey) {
  const rows = new Map();
  const cols = new Set();
  for (const r of list) {
    cols.add(r[colKey]);
    if (!rows.has(r[rowKey])) rows.set(r[rowKey], { [rowKey]: r[rowKey] });
    rows.get(r[rowKey])[r[colKey]] = r[valKey];
  }
  return [...rows.values()].map(row =>
    Object.fromEntries([[rowKey, row[rowKey]], ...[...cols].map(c => [c, row[c] ?? null])]));
}

    This is how dashboards turn time-series API data into chart or table rows; missing cells become null so every row has the same columns.

    H14 · Merge overlapping bookings per room ★★★★#

    js
    // input
[
  { room: 'A', start: 9, end: 11 }, { room: 'B', start: 10, end: 12 },
  { room: 'A', start: 10, end: 13 }, { room: 'A', start: 14, end: 15 },
]
// output
{ A: [[9, 13], [14, 15]], B: [[10, 12]] }
    js
    function mergeByRoom(list) {
  const byRoom = Object.groupBy(list, b => b.room);
  return Object.fromEntries(Object.entries(byRoom).map(([room, slots]) => {
    const merged = [];
    for (const { start, end } of slots.toSorted((x, y) => x.start - y.start)) {
      const last = merged.at(-1);
      if (last && start <= last[1]) last[1] = Math.max(last[1], end);
      else merged.push([start, end]);
    }
    return [room, merged];
  }));
}

    Group, sort, then a single merge pass: O(n log n). Ask whether touching intervals (11 and 11) should merge — here they do (<=).

    Room A's first two bookings overlap from 10:00 to 11:00, so they fuse into 9–13; the 14–15 booking starts after that block ends and stays separate.

    16.9 Input → output: strings, trees and utilities#

    These reuse get from H8, plus this escaping helper:

    js
    const escapeHtml = s => String(s).replace(/[&<>"']/g, c =>
  ({ '&': '&amp;', '<': '&lt;', '>': '&gt;', '"': '&quot;', "'": '&#39;' })[c]);

    I1 · Run-length encode and decode ★★#

    js
    // input → output
rle('aaabccdddd')   // 'a3b1c2d4'
unrle('a3b1c2d4')   // 'aaabccdddd'
rle('')             // ''
    js
    const rle = s => s.replace(/(.)\1*/g, run => run[0] + run.length);   // (.)\1* = one char and its repeats
const unrle = s => s.replace(/(\D)(\d+)/g, (_, ch, n) => ch.repeat(+n));

    Follow-up they ask: "what if the input contains digits?" — then the encoding is ambiguous; switch to a delimiter or an array of [char, count] pairs.

    I2 · Template interpolation with paths, defaults and escaping ★★★★#

    js
    // input
render(
  "Hi {{ user.name }}, you have {{cart.items[0].qty}} x {{cart.items[0].sku}}. Coupon: {{ coupon || 'none' }} {{ user.bio }}",
  { user: { name: 'Ann', bio: '<b>dev</b>' }, cart: { items: [{ sku: 'IP17', qty: 2 }] } },
);
// output
'Hi Ann, you have 2 x IP17. Coupon: none &lt;b&gt;dev&lt;/b&gt;'
    js
    const render = (tpl, data) =>
  tpl.replace(/\{\{\s*([\w.[\]]+)\s*(?:\|\|\s*'([^']*)')?\s*\}\}/g,
    (_, path, dflt = '') => escapeHtml(get(data, path, dflt)));

    The senior signal is escaping by default: user.bio is user-supplied, so it must render as text, not HTML. Never build this with new Function or eval.

    I3 · Parse a query string with repeated keys and nesting ★★★★#

    js
    // input
parseQuery('?q=iphone&color=black&color=blue&filter[price][max]=1000&filter[inStock]=true&name=A%20B')
// output
{ q: 'iphone', color: ['black', 'blue'], filter: { price: { max: '1000' }, inStock: 'true' }, name: 'A B' }
    js
    function parseQuery(qs) {
  const out = {};
  for (const [rawKey, value] of new URLSearchParams(qs)) {          // handles %20, + and the leading ?
    const keys = rawKey.replace(/\]/g, '').split('[');             // 'filter[price][max]' → ['filter','price','max']
    let node = out;
    keys.forEach((k, i) => {
      if (BLOCKED.has(k)) return;                                  // ?__proto__[admin]=1 must not pollute
      if (i < keys.length - 1) { node = node[k] ??= {}; return; }
      node[k] = k in node ? [].concat(node[k], value) : value;     // repeated key → array
    });
  }
  return out;
}

    This is exactly what a product listing page's filter state does. Mention that every value is a string — type coercion ('1000' → 1000, 'true' → true) belongs in a schema layer, not the parser.

    I4 · JSON.stringify that survives circular references ★★★★#

    js
    // input
const node = { name: 'a', child: { name: 'b' } };
node.child.parent = node;
const shared = { x: 1 };
safeStringify(node);
safeStringify({ left: shared, right: shared });
// output
'{"name":"a","child":{"name":"b","parent":"[Circular]"}}'
'{"left":{"x":1},"right":{"x":1}}'
    js
    function safeStringify(value) {
  const stack = [];                                    // current ancestor chain, not "everything seen"
  return JSON.stringify(value, function (key, v) {     // a function, not an arrow: we need `this` = parent
    if (typeof v !== 'object' || v === null) return v;
    while (stack.length && stack.at(-1) !== this) stack.pop();
    if (stack.includes(v)) return '[Circular]';
    stack.push(v);
    return v;
  });
}

    The common wrong answer uses a WeakSet of everything seen, which marks the second shared as circular even though it isn't. Tracking only the ancestor path marks true cycles only. Real use: logging Redux state or error objects to a telemetry service without crashing the logger.

    A shared object reached twice is not a cycle. Only an object that appears on its own ancestor path is, and the stack is exactly that path.

    I5 · classnames / clsx ★★#

    js
    // input → output
cx('btn', { 'btn-primary': true, disabled: false }, ['lg', null, ['icon', { active: 1 }]], 0, undefined)
// 'btn btn-primary lg icon active'
    js
    function cx(...args) {
  const out = [];
  for (const a of args.flat(Infinity)) {
    if (!a) continue;
    if (typeof a === 'string' || typeof a === 'number') out.push(a);
    else if (typeof a === 'object') for (const [k, on] of Object.entries(a)) if (on) out.push(k);
  }
  return out.join(' ');
}

    I6 · Variadic sum and generic curry ★★★#

    js
    // input → output
sum(1)(2, 3)(4)()   // 10
sum(5)()            // 5
const vol = curry((l, w, h) => l * w * h);
vol(2)(3)(4); vol(2, 3)(4); vol(2)(3, 4);   // 24, 24, 24
    js
    function sum(...a) {
  const total = a.reduce((s, x) => s + x, 0);
  return (...b) => (b.length ? sum(total, ...b) : total);   // empty call ends the chain
}
const curry = fn => function c(...args) {
  return args.length >= fn.length
    ? fn.apply(this, args)
    : (...more) => c.apply(this, [...args, ...more]);
};

    Watch out: fn.length ignores parameters with defaults and rest parameters, so curry((a, b = 1) => …) fires after one argument.

    I7 · Render a virtual DOM object to an HTML string ★★★★#

    js
    // input
{ type: 'div', props: { className: 'card', id: 'p1', hidden: false }, children: [
  { type: 'h2', children: ['Fish & Chips <hot>'] },
  { type: 'img', props: { src: '/a.png', alt: 'Fish "special"' } },
  { type: 'button', props: { disabled: true }, children: ['Buy'] },
  null,
] }
// output
'<div class="card" id="p1"><h2>Fish &amp; Chips &lt;hot&gt;</h2><img src="/a.png" alt="Fish &quot;special&quot;"><button disabled>Buy</button></div>'
    js
    const VOID = new Set(['img', 'input', 'br', 'hr', 'meta', 'link']);
function toHtml(n) {
  if (n == null || n === false) return '';                         // like React: null/false render nothing
  if (typeof n === 'string' || typeof n === 'number') return escapeHtml(n);
  const attrs = Object.entries(n.props ?? {})
    .filter(([, v]) => v !== false && v != null)                    // hidden: false → omitted
    .map(([k, v]) => {
      const name = k === 'className' ? 'class' : k;
      return v === true ? ` ${name}` : ` ${name}="${escapeHtml(v)}"`; // disabled: true → bare attribute
    })
    .join('');
  if (VOID.has(n.type)) return `<${n.type}${attrs}>`;               // no closing tag for void elements
  return `<${n.type}${attrs}>${(n.children ?? []).map(toHtml).join('')}</${n.type}>`;
}

    This is a tiny renderToString. Interviewers check escaping in both text and attributes, className → class, boolean attributes, and void elements. A follow-up is streaming: return chunks from a generator instead of one string.

    I8 · Async pipe ★★★#

    js
    // input → output
await pipeAsync(x => x + 1, async x => x * 2, x => `total:${x}`)(1)   // 'total:4'
    js
    const pipeAsync = (...fns) => input =>
  fns.reduce((p, fn) => p.then(fn), Promise.resolve(input));

    It works for sync and async steps alike because .then unwraps both. A rejection in any step skips the rest and lands in the caller's catch, which is the short-circuit you want for a checkout pipeline (validate → price → reserve → pay).

    I9 · Nested comment thread with a depth limit ★★★#

    js
    // input
const thread = [
  { id: 1, text: 'Great phone', replies: [
    { id: 2, text: 'Agreed', replies: [{ id: 3, text: 'Battery?', replies: [{ id: 4, text: 'Two days', replies: [] }] }] },
  ] },
  { id: 5, text: 'Price?', replies: [] },
];
renderThread(thread, 2);
// output
['Great phone', '  Agreed', '    … 2 more replies', 'Price?']
    js
    function renderThread(nodes, maxDepth, depth = 0) {
  return nodes.flatMap(n => {
    const line = '  '.repeat(depth) + n.text;
    if (depth + 1 >= maxDepth && n.replies.length) {
      const count = n.replies.reduce(function c(s, r) { return s + 1 + r.replies.reduce(c, 0); }, 0);
      return [line, '  '.repeat(depth + 1) + `… ${count} more replies`];   // collapse the whole subtree
    }
    return [line, ...renderThread(n.replies, maxDepth, depth + 1)];
  });
}

    In React this becomes a recursive <Comment> component whose "show more" button loads the hidden subtree on demand; the count must include every descendant, not just direct replies.

    I10 · "Recently viewed": unique, newest first, capped ★★★#

    js
    // input → output
recentlyViewed(['p1', 'p2', 'p1', 'p3', 'p4', 'p2'], 3)   // ['p2', 'p4', 'p3']
    js
    function recentlyViewed(events, cap) {
  const seen = new Map();                         // a Map keeps insertion order
  for (const id of events) { seen.delete(id); seen.set(id, true); }   // re-insert moves it to the end
  return [...seen.keys()].reverse().slice(0, cap);
}

    The delete-then-set trick is the same idea as an LRU cache built on a Map: O(1) per event. Follow-ups: persist it in localStorage with a try/catch, and sync across tabs with the storage event.

    A Map keeps insertion order, so delete then set is a constant-time "move to the end". The same trick is the core of an LRU cache.
